V
Checkpoints and Sovereign Borders
Emily Jacir’s 2003 billboard “TRANSLATE ALLAH” like her cancelled projecSt tazione for the 2009 Venice Biennial (that would have
identified the city’s vaporetto stops in Arabic), or a photograph in her Brooklyn studio of a Marlboro ad in the West Bank floating above
Israeli tanks on a Palestinian street (a sign of what one critic calls “supranational agora branding”), plays off the technique of inserting visual
checkpoints and language barriers into public spaces where they are least expected.1 The piece carries the force of an imperative: “Allah”
should be translated as “God” because “God” is the correct, standard English translation of the word. By leaving the term untranslated, Jacir
implies, the American media have effectively isolated (and Othered) it as an Arabic word used only by Muslims to refer to God. This
obscures the fact that for over five hundred years before Muhammad, Jewish Arabs and Christian Arabs in the Arab world called God by the
name of “Allah.” It also ignores the fact that for centuries and still today there are millions of Arab Christians who call God “Allah” in their
prayers, bibles, writings and practices of worship. “TRANSLATE ALLAH” flushes out Islamophobia and uses language as a site-specific
medium limning the volatile bounds of translational interdiction, the obligation to translate or not to translate and the politics of offense.
This example sets the scene for a critique of the way in which translation theory has used “border-crossing” as a prime metaphor of
general equivalence, ready meaning-exchange, and interdisciplinarity. My own delineations of a “translation zone” implied an emphasis-shift
in literary studies from discrete languages contained within perimeters of standardized usage to plurilingual process; that is to say,
languages-in-translation, pidgins, creoles, idiomatic sampling, loan-words, calques, code-switching, and the burgeoning ciphers of finance
capitalism, which notably include “translatese” and “netlish.” The politics of borders was fully activated in my book The Translation Zone: A
New Comparative Literature, published in 2006, especially where I analyzed ways in which language becomes weaponized during situations
of war and conflictual cultural nationalism. And yet, in the intervening time, I have noticed that “border-crossing” has become such an all-
purpose, ubiquitous way of talking about translation that its purchase on the politics of actual borders—whether linguistic or territorial—has
been attenuated. Prompted by recent work focusing on how sovereign legitimacy is tested by the architecture of walls, checkpoints, transit
stations, and virtual barricades of surveillance—Jeremy Harding’s The Uninvited: Refugees at the Rich Man’s Gate, Eyal Weizman’s
Hollow Land: Israel’s Architecture of Occupation and The Least of All Possible Evils, Wendy Brown’s Walled States, Waning
Sovereignty—I want to recall the force de frappe of the state in translation theory, and, with that, the physical borders of sovereignty more
generally.2 Work by a number of artists has proved crucial to understanding how a translational checkpoint may be mobilized as a kind of
“antiborder border.”
In 2007 and 2009, Khaled Jarrar, an artist from Jenin, installed At the Checkpoint, a project consisting of photographs of everyday life in
Palestine arrayed on the fences of the Huwarra and Qalandia checkpoints in the occupied West Bank3. Making explicit reference to
“Checkpoint Charlie,” Jarrar drew a contrast between checkpoints that have become defunct or repurposed as tourist sites and those, like
the ones in occupied territories and border-zones, that remain actively militarized, surveillant turnstiles of human triage.
The rituals of being checked at the border; of not having permission to cross the line; and of risking subjection to interrogation,
harassment, and incarceration were taken up in a subsequent work called Live and Work in Palestine included in the 7th Berlin Biennial for
Contemporary Art, which ran from April 27 to July 1, 2012. Using the logo of the Palestinian sunbird, Jarrar fashioned a “State of Palestine”
passport seal (in English, Arabic and Hebrew). He proceeded to invite people at the Ramallah Central Bus Station to have their passports
stamped, much as they might do with touristic passport stamps offered at Machu Picchu or the Tower of Pisa. “I wanted to welcome people,
as a Palestinian, to Palestine,” Jarrar said. The project’s riskiness was enhanced in September 2011 in the weeks leading up to the
Palestinian statehood bid at the United Nations when a number of travelers who opted for the stamp (among them ten Israeli citizens) were
detained at Israel’s airports.4
The stamp functioned as a mobile checkpoint, challenging the legitimacy of Israeli border stations in the West Bank as well as the refusal
to grant Palestinian statehood. As Simon Faulkner notes:
In her discussion of the Israeli checkpoint system, Ariella Azoulay has suggested that to suppress the possibility of a proper Palestinian border
the occupation regime has created multiple points of division that often have the appearance of a border. Thus she observes that “the border
passes wherever a Palestinian body stands.” … Every time a Palestinian seeks to travel, Israel takes advantage of the opportunity to reassert its
sovereignty. There where he would like to live his life, an ad hoc border-marker is posited—not a borderline but a border-point, a “spot.” …
Jarrar’s project mimics and at the same time inverts this system of “border-points”, by setting up border-points of his own that enable the
assertion of a phantom Palestinian sovereignty. Wherever he or his ambassadors decide to stamp passports is where the border is. If the
checkpoint is the oppressive manifestation of Israeli sovereignty over Palestinians and Palestinian land that also asserts the antiborder condition
that is the occupation, then Jarrar’s project contests this sovereignty and its denial of a proper border.5
If there is no Palestinian state, there can be no border, and yet, in this project, there is a checkpoint. As Ronen Shamir has observed, there is
a “paradoxical antiborder logic” in play; a condition of “inclusive exclusion” that defines the Palestinian checkpoint as an assertion of
phantom sovereignty. This becomes apparent in a work by Jarrar that uses the names “Palestine,” “Palestina” and “Palestinian territory” in
multiple languages and alphabets as visual elements of a map that translates a non-state into existence. In arrogating to himself the power of
a sovereign state, Jarrar, by his own admission, “wanted to provoke the whole idea of the state” and to make people think about how a logo
works to enable government control, policing, and the functioning of national state apparatuses.6 How this concretization of the state works
its way into questions of legally targeted translation (as in cases of a denied right to translation in situations of deportation or judicial
defense), or the cultural and religious exigencies of translational interdictions, is an area of translation theory rife with possibility.
In its foregrounding of the checkpoint, Jarrar’s work raises the issue of how legal, territorial markers are used figuratively in translation
theory, just as the keyword “translation” is broadly applied as a metaphor for non- or extra-linguistic transference, migration, and the traffic in
information, things, and commodities. Generally, I find it worrisome when translation is used so loosely. That said, its philology—inclusive of
the Greek words hermêneuin and metapherein; the Latin words transferre, convertere, traducere; and the German words übertragen,

übersetzen, überlieferung, all freighted with associations of transport, passage, and transmission—lends itself to border-crossing
scenarios. Such scenarios have been crucial to the great standard bearers of cultural translation, from Gloria Anzaldúa’s Borderlands/La
Frontera: The New Mestiza (1987) to Gayatri Chakravorty Spivak’s Death of a Discipline (2004) and An Aesthetic Education in the Era of
Globalization (2012).7 This last book invokes “border cultures” in relation to structures of internal colonization (applicable in Spivak’s
examples to First Nation peoples, Chicanos, and African-Americans living within U.S. borders) and as a wedge against the “easy traffic in
ethnicity” characteristic of the American “dream of interculturalism.”8
Border-speak spilled into the parlance of literary studies as actual national borders were increasingly weaponized and surveilled post-
9/11 yet rendered fungible by the economic instruments and institutions of global finance capital. In the world of super-states,
“borderlessness” became an operator, sometimes yoked theoretically to Deleuzian/Guattarian “spaces of flow” (often strangely detourned
from nomadology to become the perfect watchword for information and currency trading), or dignified as a term for the cosmopolitical polity;
a model that Étienne Balibar has qualified as the “European triple point (of heresy)”—a Europe of East, West and South made up of
overlapping territorial sheets and cultural and linguistic layers (nappes qui se recouvrent).9 For Balibar, the triple-point implies highly
politicized instances of non-compliance with the old cultural nationalisms of nation-states.
Balibar is the last political theorist that one would readily affiliate with symptomatic “border-think.” This said, his revival of the medieval
droit de cité—roughly speaking, a primordial right of access to the city-state—was taken into critical discourses of “hospitality” and “open
borders” in ways that only implicitly referred to the administrative entities regulating ports of call at official checkpoints. So here is one of my
main arguments: This idea of fluid borders—concretized by NAFTA, Shengen, the Eurozone—“forgets” the checkpoint in promoting the
border. And it is this soft, hospitable border that made its way most frequently into literary cartography. Wai Chee Dimock, for example,
would make the case for casting American literature no longer as a self-governing, autonomous, sovereign domain but as an entity closer to
a municipality or network, something resembling a Habermasian global civil society or postnational constellation. “American” is thus defined
by diverse languages and literary forms organized into sets or aggregates of sets. In place of territorial limitrophes, there are shapes and
configurations, environments equipped with porous semiotic skins. These are articulated ecologically and abstractly: as “nesting” recursive
structures, reversible landscapes, convex or concave patterns, bulges and burrows. Dimock uses the term “heterarchy” to designate
reversible hierarchy, divergent but coexistent patterns of relation, feedback loops, and complex systems that break out of predetermined
patterns. Her paradigm of “deep time” is characterized by telescoped time and nonspecific space. Dimock opts for wildly transtemporal and
transcontinental genres, discernible as fractals of comparison. A Mandelbrotian fractal, she asserts,
is a set of points that cannot be plotted as one, two, or three dimensions. It is a geometry of nonintegers, a geometry of what loops around, what
breaks off, what is jagged, what comes only in percentages … Unlike the Euclidean objects—squares, circles, and triangles, with their clean
resolution—the shapes that interest Mandelbrot are shapes with serried outlines: the wispy puffs of clouds, the lacy fronds of ferns, the pocked
and porous surface of the sponge, the coiled dimensions inside a ball of twine.
Dimock’s epic is an omnivorous, translational fractal: “a linguistic sponge, springing up in contact zones, it is super-responsive to its
environment, picking up all those non-Greek words …” Generally speaking, Dimock’s fractals, as enlisted for comparative work in Through
Other Continents, seem impervious to national borders. In Shades of the Planet, a collective volume edited by Dimock that also forms part
of her bid to transnationalize American literature, she and fellow contributor Rachel Adams have recourse to Walter Mignolo’s notion of
“border gnosis.” Mignolo, for his part, coins the expression with the express purpose of rectifying the erasure of difference wrought by
unidirectional colonial translation. Mignolo grafts the expression from Valentin Y. Mudimbe’s concept of “African gnosis” as developed in his
1988 touchstone The Invention of Africa: Gnosis, Philosophy and the Order of Knowledge. It designates a way of thinking about knowledge
conflicts typical of borderlands, be they epistemological, ethical, secular or religious. Such conflicts are assumed to be connected to actual
fences and walls that bifurcate nations, aggress landscape, and divide community. But Mignolo never focuses on the material structures of
the state when enjoining the powerful metaphorics of the border. As the danger of “crossing” supersedes the violence of “grounding” in his
elaboration of border gnosis, Mignolo adumbrates not an area to be studied but “a kind of thinking beyond the social sciences and
positivistic philosophy, a kind of thinking that moves along the diversity of the historical process itself.”10 Historical process is acknowledged
epistemologically in his larger scheme of “de-colonialism,” but translation as a practice engaged in by border police at checkpoint stations or
relied upon in court cases adjudicating immigration and deportation is nowhere referenced.
Whether it is an icon like “Checkpoint Charlie,” that fully loaded specter of the Cold War state; generic station buildings set to monitor
input, output flows of people; or a fence of the kind that divides Nogales, Mexico from Nogales, Arizona (which illustrates the uncanny double
sovereignty of a single town), the checkpoint particularizes political ground. It is not just a topological “neighborhood” nor the general term for
a logic of ontological scission, nor a cosmological sky-rim eliding the space-bar between life and transfinitude, nor the kind of topographic
divider typically found at virtually any kind of border. It is, rather, an apparatus of state function dedicated to divisive acts of profiling and
selectionism. As Karim Mattar and David Fieni have argued:
The checkpoint functions not only to control the flow of migrants, illicit goods, and insurgents/terrorists, but also to divide contiguous lands and to
reproduce politically and legally encoded distinctions between “us” and “them.” Thus performing sovereignty, the checkpoint appears to be
symptomatic of fears of catastrophe, whether economic, political, or social, in various national and global contexts.11
The checkpoint in this ascription is used as a figure of performative sovereignty inseparable from the politics of catastrophism; a
catastrophism of national non-recognition and subjectivation by a contiguous hegemonic power.
The Catalan artist Antoni Muntadas has used language in translation as a medium that makes visible invisible zoning laws of circulation
and mobility, allowing us to appreciate how translation is instrumentalized in domestic round-ups and border control as well as international
espionage, where it is now a routine feature of spyware and cyberwar. In his work On Translation: Die Stadt (1999–2004), a white delivery
truck emblazoned with the logo “On Translation” and beneath it the word for “city” in Catalan, French and German, is photographically logged
in transit around Barcelona, Lille and Graz, all known as capitals of culture. The shots of the whirring vehicle—which was equipped with a
camera and thus doubled as a filmmaking apparatus and mobile movie theater—not only reveals the extent to which translation is a process
that is happening all the time and everywhere but also the uncanny effect induced by perceiving the discrepancies among ciutat, ville and
Stadt. Die Stadt translates into English as “the city,” but it doubles acoustically as der Staat, with attendant associations of the state as a
police force patrolling citizens. And this draws attention to the significance of the truck as a rogue vehicle whose functions might include
ferrying cargos of contraband and foreign migrants. Die Stadt from this perspective draws attention to the double standard in wealthy
countries whereby multiculturalism is lauded while illegal residents are harassed and deported.
In zoning territory and mediating the right to travel across state lines, translation is both a metaphor for border control and a practice
availed of by state agents to determine the legal standing of persons at the gate. A number of projects riveted on the Israel/Palestine crisis
mobilize points of tension between border tropes and physical borders with respect to the politics of partition. As Danielle Drori suggests,
the problem may be symptomatically overdetermined to some degree by the fact that the Hebrew word for “language,” safa, signifies not only
“lip” (an idea much glossed by Derrida in his commentary on Celan’s poem, “Shibboleth”) but also “edge,” “rim,” and “riverbank.” In physics
and mathematics, the Hebrew tna’ey safa refers to the boundaries (or more precisely the resolution conditions) of a mathematical problem.
Safa, Drori argues, is a border in the restricted sense of a threshold, delineating a beginning or ending of something. While not

interchangeable with gvul, the precise term in Hebrew for “border” (qua line of separation), safa and gvul belong to a semantic cluster in
which notions of language, cognitive delineation, border and territorial boundary bleed into each other.12 Yesh Gvul, roughly translated as
“there is a limit” or “the border exists” became the name of a movement initiated by Israeli reservists who refused to invade Lebanon at the
outbreak of war in 1982. Their name reclaims and regrounds the border as the semantic currency of anti-occupation; gvul in this instance is
coded politically as a pendant to the group’s dictate: “refuse to serve,” which led to many of their number being court-martialled and
imprisoned.
The semiotic fluidity of borders becomes especially problematic in the context of Israeli border policy, which is characterized according
to Helga Tawil-Souri by a “contradictory logic … of not having stated borders but wanting to enforce and defend them.”13 This “there but not
there” character of the political border is undercut by the checkpoint, clearly visible in the landscape and in the urban mutations that it
spawns: sliced neighborhoods, packer containers, cement blocs, leftovers of vanished villages, unplanned, featureless faubourgs, and rag-
tag communities. These areas serve as evidence of biopolitical governmentality and resist the abstractions of border rhetoric 14 It is
precisely for this reason that Helga Tawil-Souri makes the rather strange case for preserving the checkpoint as a “low-technical-political
border” defined by color-coded ID cards and biometric tests administered face-to-face by the guards. Hi-tech borders, in her view, are
mechanisms of “frictionless control” that sanitize the act of occupation, privatize the business of surveillance, and substitute ground-based
detainment with telephonic enclosure (as in the curtailment of Gaza’s access to bandwidth and Internet flow)—all of which blunt perception of
government responsibility.15
In Hollow Land: Israel’s Architecture of Occupation, Eyal Weizman insists that the checkpoint preserves its referential status as
infrastructure even as it is inflated metaphorically as a “translation” of state sovereignty at the border. Of the one-way mirror installed after
Oslo in the Allenby Bridge Checkpoint Terminal, he says:
It is in fact a kind of translation: … more than the mere apparatus of control—it functioned also as an international border of sorts. In fact, not only
did the mirror demarcate a border, but in its positioning and function it created a new conceptual border to the concept of sovereignty.
Throughout Hollow Land, walls are treated as a language with its own vernacular, including “separation walls,” “barriers,” “blockades,”
“closures,” “road blocks,” “checkpoints,” “sterile areas,” “special security zones,” “closed military areas,” and “killing zones.” How a wall is
named depends on which side of the wall one lives:
The discontinuous lines of fences, ditches, concrete walls and high-tech sensors—referred to by the Israeli government as the “seam-line
obstacle,” by the general Israeli public as the “separation fence,” and by those Israelis and Palestinians opposing it as the Wall or sometimes as
the “Apartheid Wall”—are only the most visible and mediatized barriers built in a frenzy of fortification construction that has pockmarked the entire
West Bank since the beginning of the Oslo Process in 1993, with the aim of separating Palestinians from Israelis at every opportunity.16
The lexicon expands theoretically to include “prosthetic sovereignty,” an architecture of surveilled cartography; “elastic geography” (movable
boundary lines and porous barriers defined by the tunnels designed to move contraband goods and material); and “political plastic” (or
“politics in matter”), which designates the crowds of actors at the frontier, from military personnel, settlers, cellular network providers, and
state planners to activists, NGOs, humanitarian organizations, armed resistance groups, and media entities. This form of “plastic,” unofficial
checkpoint becomes the riveting subject of Emad Burnat and Guy Davidi’s documentary film5  Broken Cameras (2011). A barbed-wire
fence near the West Bank wall that expropriates over 50 percent of agricultural lands belonging to the West Bank Palestinian village of Bil’in,
and imposed in order to protect an illegal Israeli settlement, becomes the site of violent, protracted protests.
In his more recent book, The Least of All Possible Evils, Weizman coins the term “wallfare” to signal both warfare at the Israeli/
Palestinian border and the hardware and software implemented to enhance security. One particularly chilling form of wallfare is defined by
the “humanitarian” minimal limit of subsistence necessary to sustain life. Calculations of this limit were discovered in an Israeli military
document titled “Redlines,” itself, of course, a border metaphor. These calculations were used to determine the quantities of food and fuel
allowed to flow to Gaza during the siege, intensified in fall 2007. “The ability,” Weizman writes, “to exercise control through the modulation of
flow—in which the checkpoints and terminals within the wall function as valves and switches—has made Israel’s warfare on Gaza resemble
an inverse Milgram experiment.”17
Weizman uses the word “translation” not only as a general operator referring to the conversion of systems of governance and control into
diffuse, centripetal forms of spatial organization but also in a more specialized sense to describe what happened in a courtroom battle over
the placement of a wall. At the center of the case was a topographic scale model that relied on aerial maps of boundaries. The model
indicated where a projected security wall would sever three hundred acres of farmland from the village of Beit Sourik, located northwest of
Jerusalem. In describing the court proceedings, Weizman refers to the model itself as a translation, taking his cue from Wittgenstein who had
become fascinated with a model used forensically to reenact an accident in Paris involving a truck and a pram: “Wittgenstein’s reflection on
the way a model was able to illustrate legal language,” Weizman writes, “might help shed some light on the story that follows, a story that is
itself engaged with acts of translation, undertaken in court, from reality to its representation on a physical model, and vice versa” (my
emphasis). Adopting an approach sympathetic to object agency, Weizman narrates from the viewpoint of the model, underscoring how it
disturbs courtroom protocol by throwing off the scale of seats and benches, introduces informal modes of address into legal language, and
turns the somber atmosphere of court proceedings into something resembling an architectural desk crit or design session.18 Though in the
end Israeli security concerns inevitably trumped Palestinian land claims in the ruling, Weizman’s border war, narrated from the vantage point
of a wall, is shown seeding category confusion and cognitive dissonance at every turn. The wall and its double, the scale model, function as
translational actants establishing relation among discrepant orders of material objects, modes of social discourse, logical propositions,
politicized cartography, and reason of state. Translation is also given pragmatic, literal force as a show of justice, or a juridical performative
in the technical sense of the French expression traduire en justice, meaning “to bring to justice,” “to prosecute.”
Defined as a form of translated sovereignty performed by checkpoints, the state must contend with the elusiveness of its own
morphology. Whether presented in the guise of the Hobbesian Leviathan; in Foucauldian biopolitics; in the “wherever,” or Wohin auch
immer?, factor inherent in surveillance and cyber-security technologies and in all-over warfare by drones; or in the status of eroding nation-
states within the Eurozone (summed up in a question posed by the title of a special issue of the French journal Critique, translated as “O
State, Where Art Thou? Are You There?”),19 the state is pictured as tenuous and everywhere diffused. Every person becomes a potential
checkpoint in the “war on terror,” a body-scan medium, a transporter of cellular data, a first responder, a civilian backup to law enforcement.
In the era of cyber-surveillance, checkpoints can be as anodyne as a Facebook wall, a paywall, or a document fingerprint, or as menacing as
a citizen’s self-authorization to apply a stand-your-ground law. Border guards actively embody “check-pointization,” a process set in motion
when “a person of interest” moves into the crosshairs of a raised gun.20 Soldiers corporealize the checkpoint, as do agents of parastates—
militias, vigilantes, warlords, guerilla armies, bandits and pirates—who enable groups with no sovereign standing to act like a state.
Sovereign fear flares at the checkpoint, itself a kind of gunpoint of juridical encounter. It goes without saying that when the checkpoint is
sited in a global hotspot or when the traveler happens to be a migrant or suspected terrorist, the threat factor escalates exponentially.
In Annemarie Jacir’s feature-length film Salt of This Sea (2008), a Brooklyn-born Palestinian-American named Soraya, en route to visit
her ancestral home in Jaffa, is similarly subjected to protracted interrogation and a strip search in the airport. Virtually every scene in the

movie is dramatically structured as a checkpoint, an obstacle course, or a gauntlet on which the characters must evaluate the vagaries of
patrol cars, the distance of police helicopters, or the choice of language—English, Arabic, or Hebrew—for initiating contact. The film
foregrounds Soraya mastering the art of “getting through” barriers by negotiating the minefields of translation and cultural untranslatability.21
States legitimate themselves through official or commonly spoken languages, so it is interesting to see which languages are used at
Israeli checkpoints in this film as well. English, the language of British colonialism and American imperialism in the Middle East,i s the lingua
franca that the border speaks, irrespective of the fact that Hebrew is the national language. Soraya deploys her American accent to obtain
security clearance at banks in order to defuse the suspicions of soldiers who have picked up her mates. Arabic, on the other hand, leads to
Soraya’s reawakening as a Palestinian. She discovers the nakbah at the scale of prosaic encounters; small acts of sitting around and
arguing with newly discovered Palestinian relatives who have internalized walls and checkpoints as psychic states of occupation and daily
humiliation.
Checkpoints are sites of push-back and resistance in the seminal novel Checkpoint (2004) by Palestinian author, activist and former
Knesset member Azmi Bishara. As in Annemarie Jacir’s films, the checkpoint defines a world unto itself and an archipelago dominated by
guards (the “masters of the checkpoint”) and galvanized by a young girl who crosses the border every day to attend school. “The word,
mahtrom, a Hebrew term for checkpoint taken into Arabic, comes to signify two worlds fused in political disequilibrium.”22 Published in the
year in which construction of Israel’s walls and electric fences was massively ramped up, Bishara’s novel depicts a landscape so cut up by
barriers and so neutralized by the brownish-grey color of cement blocs and stones that space-time delineations recede. The checkpoints
rise up as solo architectural players on a featureless horizon.
It is this destituted scape that is rigorously depicted by the Palestinian artist Taysir Batniji, whose Watchtowers series was included in the
12th Istanbul Biennial in 2012. Simulating Bernd and Hilla Becher’s typological studies of clocktowers and water-towers, Batniji substitutes
Israeli watchtowers, creating a landscape of state surveillance out of the visual language of photorealism and removing the political neutrality
of the checkpoint as naturalized industrial architecture. Batniji’s modest exercise in appropriation art participates in a grander scheme of
things in what Rema Hammami calls “the moral economy of checkpoints.” Hammami describes checkpoint actions on the border where
people use their bodies as coagulants of assembly that jam the ports of ingress and egress installed by the Israeli state. There is a defiant
wish fulfillment communicated by these gestures of embodied blockade, and they recall a remarkable magical realist scene of fulfilled
wishes in Elia Suleiman’s Divine Intervention, made in 2002 before the wall was erected.
The film is constructed around long takes of a man and a woman sitting in a car waiting; endlessly watching the checkpoint between
Ramallah and Jerusalem as if it were the movie of their lives.23 Suddenly there is a break-out moment in which the woman brazenly crosses
over without being “checked.” Attired in high style, she turns the tarmac into a fashion runway, daring the soldiers to shoot as she saunters
past their raised guns. Her gaze—supernaturally empowered like a weapon of mind control—causes the tower to collapse.
In each of these projects by artists, architects and writers, we see the translation zone defined not as a porous boundary facilitating
supranational comity and regimes of general equivalence but as a threshold of untranslatability and political blockade. In bringing back the
checkpoint to undercut the way in which translation studies has flaccidly appropriated metaphors of border-crossing, the aim has been to
insist on the persistence of the function of the state police within the field of language politics. It may be a time of waning sovereignty insofar
as nation-states are trumped by liquid capital and the global dissemination of non-aligned standing armies, but checkpoints, whether mobile
bodies or stationary landmarks, produce a logic of anti-sovereign occupation contoured by walls of non-transitivity and untranslatability.

Part Two
Doing Things with Untranslatables

I
Keywords 1: “Cyclopaedia”
I owe the discovery of Uqbar to the conjunction of a mirror and an encyclopedia. The mirror troubled the depths of a corridor in a country house
on Gaona Street in Ramos Mejía; the encyclopedia is fallaciously called The Anglo-American Cyclopaedia (New York, 1917) and is a literal but
delinquent reprint of the Encyclopedia Britannica of 1902.
—Jorge Luis Borges, Tlön, Uqbar, Orbis Tertius
Both a history of philosophy and a massive translation exercise, Barbara Cassin’s Vocabulaire européen des philosophies: Dictionnaire
des intraduisibles stems the tide of unilateralist, one-world literary practice. It offers an instrument for “philosophizing in languages,” which,
though relying on nominalist entry terms, derails the sedimentation of meanings that congeal within the names of concepts. By revealing the
history of mistranslation and misfired speech-acts in the history of philosophical terms, it disestablishes verbal monoliths and idea myths. In
its attention to the historically shifting ideologies embedded in words and emphasis on linguistic encounters in actual situations, the
Vocabulaire invites comparison with Raymond Williams’s Keywords: A Vocabulary of Culture and Society, a cherry-picked collection of
English words (“anarchism,” “bureaucracy,” “collective,” “consumer,” “creative,” “democracy,” “dialectic,” “doctrinaire,” “elite,” “equality,”
“liberal,” “management,” “materialism,” “progressive,” “radical,” “reactionary,” “sociology,” “subjective,” “taste,” “unemployment,” and so forth),
distinguished by their class valence, independence from “proper meaning,” and susceptibility to controversy and misunderstanding. Sparked
by his recognition that since World War II the word “culture” had gyred wildly between its “teashop” use (as a word for social superiority as
defined by money, class, position and behavior) and its “way of life” use, as both a synonym for intellectual and artistic production and an
anthropological attitude toward everyday practices, Williams was especially sensitive to untranslatability within a common language, so
much so that I have borrowed his examples of “keywords” in selecting case studies of Untranslatables in subsequent chapters.1
Cassin’s Vocabulaire problematizes the concern of what to call the keyword as itself a genre. It exhibits affinities at different points with
Williams’s keywords and Reinhart Koselleck’s Geschichtiliche Grundbegriffe, a dictionary of political and social Concept-History. As a work
coming out of France, it harks back to Bayle’s Dictionnaire historique et critique (1702) and to Diderot and D’Alembert’s Encylopédie, ou
dictionnaire raisonné des sciences, des arts et des métiers (1751–66); in the latter case not because it models the Encyclopédie’s wildly
eclectic topical juxtapositions—electricity, Africans, Machiavellianism, sex, monarchy, man, stocking manufacture—or embellishes its
concept entries with planches and extended discussions of science, craft and technology. Rather, like its predecessor, the Vocabulaire is
interested in using translation to extend what philosophy does. For Howard Caygill, the Encyclopédie and the Vocabulaire share the
endeavor of radical philosophy:
In his study of the dissemination of Spinozist ideas—Radical Enlightenment—Jonathan Israel described the early Enlightenment dictionary as a
vector for radical philosophy, “a philosophical engine of war which massively invaded the libraries, public and private, of the whole continent.”2
Diderot and D’Alembert’s project was vast in scale (thirty-two volumes, seventy thousand articles), but the Vocabulaire can in key respects
be seen as a continuation of the Encyclopédie. The Encyclopédie began its life as a translation (a French rendering of Ephraim
Chambers’s Cyclopaedia: or, An Universal Dictionary of Arts and Sciences [1728], itself heavily based on translations of French
dictionaries and treatises), and the Vocabulaire was similarly built up out of translated material. Both projects were Europe-centered despite
a worldly purview. Both were imbued with Frenchness though nationally unbounded in their production and reception. Both jostled
competitively with British philosophical traditions, narrating (though not always intentionally) a story about the dueling prospects both of
idealism and materialism and of metaphysics and pragmatism. Both projects confronted the burden of the past in the guises of Greek
philosophy, Roman law and Biblical theology. Both created conditions favorable to the conceptual unexpected. Both functioned as advocacy
documents in favor of conscripting philosophy in the struggle against fanaticism, intolerance, censorship, dogmatism, arbitrary sovereignty,
enslavement, coercion, injustice and inequality. Both flaunted their programmed obsolescence in pointing ahead to future knowledge
platforms, interactive formats, and modes of dissemination. And both imagined themselves as self-correcting research engines revising their
way, Wiki-style, into relevance for generations to come.
Cassin and her editorial équipe acknowledged that they had the Encyclopédie in mind as a counter-model when they initially charted the
Vocabulaire. Where D’Alembert had cast the plurality of languages as an obstacle to a universalist, philosophic history, Cassin (in the
sophistic vein) and her colleagues embraced the messiness of linguistic multiplicity and conceptual pluralism. “What really suits us
philosophers is the plural,” she wrote,
The Dictionary of Untranslatable Terms does not pretend to offer “the” perfect translation to any untranslatable, rather, it clarifies the
contradictions and places them face-to-face in reflection; it is a pluralist and comparative work in its non-enclosing gesture, rather more
Borgesian or Oulipian—“the modern form of fantasy is erudition” Borges tells us—than destinal and Heideggerian.3
Interestingly, one of the best definitions of untranslatability in line with Cassin’s usage crops up in Diderot’s entry for the word “Encyclopédie.”
Diderot allows that, while the aim of an encyclopedia is to deliver a precise definition on whose meaning all users universally concur, the
reality is otherwise. Assigning attributes and qualities, he admits is no common skill:
A universal dictionary is an opus which proposes to fix the meaning of the terms of a language, by defining those which can be defined, through a
short, meticulous, clear, and precise enumeration or the qualities of ideas attached to them. The only good definitions are those that group the
essential attributes of the thing designated by the word. But is just anyone capable of knowing and exposing these attributes? Is the art of
defining as common as that? Are we not all, more or less, in the same situation as children who apply, with the greatest precision, innumerable
terms in the place of which they would be utterly incapable of substituting the correct collection of qualities of ideas they represent? Whence, how
many unforeseen difficulties, when it comes to setting down the meaning of the commonest expressions? We constantly experience the fact that
the ones we least understand are also those we use the most. What is the reason for this strange phenomenon? It is that we are forever in the
situation of pronouncing that a thing is such; almost never in the necessity of determining what it is to be such. Our most frequent judgments fall

on specific objects, and the general usage of the language and experience suffices to guide us. We merely repeat what we have heard all our
lives.4
Diderot’s exemplary Untranslatable is an ordinary speech-act or term of common usage whose signification is supposedly unambiguous but
which is anything but. In such cases, the gap between the word for a thing and the qualia referentially associated with it is greatest with
respect to words whose meaning we think we know. The Untranslatable comes into focus as that x-factor that disqualifies presumptive
knowability in matters of linguistic definition.
If the entry on “Encyclopédie” in the Encyclopédie identifies the Untranslatable with a restriction imposed by language on access to
meaning, or to knowledge of a thing as such (ce que c’est qu’être tel ), it also brings to the fore the problem of its own self-naming as a
generic form. Chambers’s neologism “Cyclopaedia” weighs heavily. What did he intend by this term, which he defended against accusations
that it was a crime of nomenclature? It was at once a new form (comparable to Hook’s Micrographia or Wolfius’s Aerometria), and a very
particular kind of dictionary if we go by the work’s full descriptive title: Cyclopaedia, or, An Universal Dictionary of Arts and Sciences:
Containing the Definitions of the Terms, and Accounts of the Things Signify’d Thereby, in the Several Arts, both Liberal and Mechanical,
and the Several Sciences, Human and Divine: the Figures, Kinds, Properties, Productions, Preparations, and Uses, of Things Natural
and Artificial; the Rise, Progress, and State of Things Ecclesiastical, Civil, Military, and Commercial: with the Several Systems, Sects,
Opinions, etc.; among Philosophers, Divines, Mathematicians, Physicians, Antiquaries, Criticks, etc.: The Whole Intended as a Course
of Ancient and Modern Learning. Martine Groult maintains that what distinguished “Cyclopaedia,” as Chambers defined it, was an
organicist model of mutually reinforcing part/whole relations and an overarching “view of knowledge” (a “cyclopian” dimension connoting the
all-seeing eye).5 Chambers’s use of the term “stamen” confirmed a germinative ecology of knowledge-production. Each word was to grow in
the manner of a God-given originary particle within the parameters of its disciplinary space. For the Encylopedists, by contrast, the divine
originary particle was replaced by a human-centered system of intellection animated by interactivity of disciplines and knowledge-forms.
The Encyclopedia was designed then to supersede the Cyclopaedia, leading to its retirement as an outmoded model of knowledge
production. But this move did not obviate classificatory indeterminacy: For “encyclopedia” and “dictionary” both figure in the title:
Encyclopédie, ou Dictionnaire universel des arts et des sciences. Cassin’s Vocabulaire would exhibit a comparable instability,
equivocating in the title between calling itself a vocabulary and a dictionary. In deciding how to render the book’s title in English, a decision
was taken to go with Dictionary of Untranslatables: A Philosophical Lexicon. The substitution of “lexicon” for “vocabulary” was intended to
foreground the active process, in living languages, whereby new words are “entered into the lexicon.” But it it did nothing to resolve the issue
of generic identity. How was this work expressly not an encyclopedia? Was the duck–rabbit construct Vocabulaire-Dictionnaire a signal to
readers that they should not think of the work as an “encyclopedia” of philosophy even though the text contained inventory that was
encyclopedic in organizational structure, scope, and depth? Were dictionary and vocabulary wholly interchangeable? A dictionary is broadly
defined as a reference book containing an alphabetical list of words with information about them, while a vocabulary, in addition to being the
generic term for sets of words that people are familiar with in a language is similarly used as a term to describe alphabetized and explained
word ensembles, usually for a pedagogical purpose relating to a special field. But a quick survey of titles sheds little light on their difference:
Google turned up vocabularies of “faith,” “thinking,” “culture and society,” “massacre and genocide,” “golf,” and so forth, as well as
dictionaries of “Buddhism,” “Urdu,” “philosophy,” “scientific quotations,” “period Russian names,” and “dentistry.” Very broadly speaking, it
appeared that vocabulary emphasized something more conceptual, whereas “dictionary” underscored technical reference, but there seemed
to be no rigorous distinction holding between them. This instability around naming the motherbook of all definitions was unnerving, pointing
up the strangeness of the object in question, its peculiarity as the form of the drive to allness. As Diderot famously described it: “The purpose
of an encyclopedia is to collect knowledge disseminated around the globe.”6 There was also D’Alembert’s equally celebrated pendant
statement in the Discours préliminaire:
As an Encyclopedia, it is to set forth as well as possible the order and connection of the parts of human knowledge. As a Reasoned Dictionary of
the Sciences, Arts and Trades, it is to contain the general principles that form the basis of each science and each art, liberal or mechanical, and
the most essential facts that make up the body and substance of each.7
D’Alembert’s anamorphic projection of global knowledge as “shaped extension” joins Roland Barthes’ description of the Encyclopédie as a
“technological organ” dedicated to transmission, amplification and relay. “The Encyclopedic image,” he wrote in his essay on the planches,
“constitutes a structure of information … we find prophetically formulated the very principle of cybernetic ensembles; the plate, image of the
machine, is indeed in its way a brain; we introduce substance into it and set up the ‘program’…”8 So, here we have what is by now the
commonplace positioning of the Encyclopédie as ancestor to the World Wide Web, Google (“Our mission is to organize all the information
in the world”), and, more recently, the Internet Relay Chat Channel; constantly uploading from below, calibrating data waves, leaking
information, and facilitating social networks.
Much has already been written and there remains much more to be said about the Encyclopédie as the original cybernetic tool and
prime object of all media. Yves Citton, among others, has noted how the system of cross-references (renvois) induced not only a kind of
indisciplinarity in prompting translateral reading and a broken attention span but also provided a pragmatic means of evading censorship. In
the “Encyclopédie” article, Diderot alluded to the way in which the renvois operated as a semaphore system directing readers without ready
detection to entries that overturned “ridiculous opinions” and pointed the way to the Encyclopédie’s rationalist, irreligious precepts. “It is the
art of tacitly deducing the boldest consequences,” Diderot wrote. “If such confirming and refuting references are foreseen well in advance,
and skillfully prepared, they will give an encyclopedia the character which a good dictionary ought to possess: that of changing the common
mode of thinking.”9
Though the Encyclopédie may be considered the ancestor to WikiLeaks in its contestation of government media control, it arguably
censored traces of its own genesis as a translation of Chambers’s Cyclopaedia; redacting content willy-nilly and attributing to French
authors plagiarized or barely paraphrased material. The fact that the guiding lights of the Encyclopédie often did their best to disavow their
own text’s origin as a translation also attests to a complex translation history that shows up the influence of nationalism even in professedly
internationalist projects. Diderot, for example, in his own entry on the term “Encyclopédie,” dilated on the inferiority of the English original.
Chambers, he alleged,
limited himself to compilation from our dictionaries and the analysis of a small number of works, invented nothing, and stuck strictly to what was
known, everything being equally interesting or indifferent to him, having neither a preference for any topic, nor favorable or unfavorable time for
work, save that of a migraine or spleen, he was a laborer who plowed his furrow, shallow, but even and straight.… Chambers’ articles are fairly
regularly distributed; but they are empty. Ours are full, but irregular. If Chambers had filled his up, I have no doubt his organization would have
suffered.
His depiction of the Cyclopaedia as intellectually undiscriminating and derivative of French dictionaries uses the myth of French brilliance to
obscure the Encyclopédie’s debt to the English original. Diderot went on to convert the Encyclopédie’s formal disabilities—“Nous sommes
alternativement nains et géants, colosses et pygmées; droits, bien faits et proportionnés, bossus, boiteux et contrefaits” [we are by turns
dwarves and giants, colossuses and pygmies; standing straight, nicely built and well proportioned; hunchbacked, lame and deformed] into a

virtue. John Lough notes that despite grand claims to a complete revision of Chambers, there were entries that made virtually no alterations
to Chambers’s original as if fulfilling (though with “cyclopaedia” and “encyclopedia” in reverse position) Borges’s characterization of the
Anglo-American Cyclopaedia of 1917 as a “literal but delinquint reprint” of the Encyclopedia Britannica of 1902 in “Tlön, Uqbar, Orbis
Tertius.” The preface to the Encyclopédie’s second volume announced with great fanfare, for example, that Buffon had contributed the
“Nature” article, but closer inspection of the articles on “Nature (Philos)” and “Nature, Lois de la” revealed little more than a direct translation
of Chambers.10
Translation is haunted by the anxiety that it is unoriginal or illicitly appropriated intellectual property. The legal definition listed under the
“Translation” entry in the Encyclopédie underscores the withdrawal of property from its place: “the Act of transferring or removing a thing from
one Place to another.”11 Further on, translators are treated as analogous to criminals (with help from the old saw “Traduttore, Traditore”):
“Translators frequently endeavor to execute themselves at the Expense of their Language; and ask Pardon for it, as if it were not rich and
copious enough to express all the force and Beauties of the Original.”12 Translational crime is identified with the translator’s tendency to
blame the original language for shortcomings that are the translator’s own. In this context, it is interesting then that Diderot uses the word
“plagiat” with reference to Chambers, as if by impugning his original and going on the offensive he might erase any memory of the
Encyclopédie’s debt to its English source text. He lamented: “Que de temps perdu à traduire de mauvaises choses! que de dépenses pour
se procurer un plagiat continuel!”[How much time was lost translating bad things! What expenditure, from which to direct nothing more than
continual plagiarism!”]13 Here, “plagiat” signifies a travesty of enlightened learnedness, but ultimately, I think, it is an assertion of prerogative,
an effort to stake an ownership claim on the intellectual estates of the encyclopedia. By disputing Chambers’s assertion that the French
models for his encyclopedia—among them those of Abbé Furetière, Trévoux, Savary, Chauvin, and Bayle—had been made “subservient to
his purpose,” Diderot restored the French to first position.
Fast-forwarding to the Vocabulaire, we still find evidence of such nationalist rivalries. In his entry on “Français,” Alain Badiou defaults to
national language myths, especially when he insists that for Descartes, Bergson, Sartre, Deleuze, and Lacan to philosophize is inseparable
from the mother tongue. Unlike German, whose truth, Badiou holds, is attained through verbal and syntactic unraveling, French syntax is
transparent to truth. Like Adamic language, it lends itself to logical formalism, axioms, maxims, and universal principles. Above all, for
Badiou, French is conducive to the politicization of expression; unseating predicates through the play of substitutions and the art of the
imperious question (what Lacan called the “denunciatory enunciation”).14 In the end, Badiou’s entry emerges as less critical of French and
Frenchness than Voltaire’s entry on the same subject in the Encyclopédie. The effect of this display of national pride in the brilliance of the
French language is only exacerbated when one turns to the Vocabulaire entry devoted to “Anglais” (written by two French authors), which
concentrates on “the English language or the genius of the ordinary.”15
The nationalism card not only strengthened Diderot and D’Alembert’s bid to the crown for permission to publish free of censorship, it also
allowed them to champion an Enlightenment vision of encyclopedism. And while this vision was arguably more concerned with translating the
values of a group of “Brights”—secular humanism over and against theocracy, fanaticism, and religious dogma—into forms of legitimate
savoir than it was about translating an English original, the insistence on the unoriginality of Chambers’s Cyclopaedia attests to anxieties of
influence on the part of Encyclopédie editors. It was as if they sensed there would always be an English Cyclopaedia lurking behind the
scenes in the pages of the Encyclopédie. Similarly, one could say, the specter of Anglophone “Globish” haunts the Vocabulaire and
underwrites its efforts to correct for the hegemony of Anglo-American philosophy in the teaching of philosophy.
Ultimately, what the Encyclopédie and the Vocabulaire share, beyond their competition with Anglophone models, is a translation-
symptom attached to their unstable nominalism: Are they encyclopedias? Dictionaries? Vocabularies? Lexicons? Caygill, introducing the
Vocabulaire to English readers in the journal Radical Philosophy not long after it came out in French, noted the curious
absence of an entry on the philosophical dictionary itself, crucial not only as a site for the debate and codification of philosophical idioms but also
as a vector for the teaching and dissemination of philosophy… The subentry on lexicography in the entry on “Logos” is too general to help,
unless of course the entire Vocabulaire be considered as a vast entry on this very question.16
Perhaps the best English translation of the Vocabulaire would be none other than “Cyclopaedia,” retrieved from Chambers and assigned
new purpose as a name for the Untranslatable animating and exhausting the encyclopedic form.

1 Raymond Williams, Keywords: A Vocabulary of Culture and Society, New York: Oxford University Press, 1983, 11, 17; Williams’s sensitivity to untranslatability:
ibid., 12.
2 Howard Caygill, “From Abstraction to Wunch: The Vocabulaire européen des philosophies,” Radical Philosophy 138, July/August 2006, 10.
3 Barbara Cassin, “Philosophising in Languages,” trans. Yves Gilonne, Nottingham French Studies 49 No. 2 (Summer 2010): 18 (Original text: from a paper titled
“Violence de la traduction: Traduire l’intraduisible,” presented at the 22nd Assises de la traduction littéraire in Arles, 2005).
4 (“Un vocabulaire universel est un ouvrage dans lequel on se propose de fixer la signification des termes d’une langue, en définissant ceux qui peuvent être
définis, par une énumération courte, exacte, claire & précise, ou des qualités ou des idées qu’on y attache. Il n’y a de bonnes définitions que celles qui rassemblent
les attributs essentiels de la chose désignée par le mot. Mais a-t-il été accordé à tout le monde de connoître & d’exposer ces attributs? L’art de bien définir est—il un
art si commun? Ne sommes nous pas tous, plus ou moins, dans le cas même des enfans, qui appliquent avec une extrème précision, une infinité de termes à la place
desquels il leur seroit absolument impossible de substituer la vraie collection de qualités ou d’idées qu’ils représentent? De là, combien de difficultés imprévues, quand
il s’agit de fixer le sens des expressions les plus communes? On éprouve à tout moment que celles qu’on entend le moins, sont aussi celles dont on se sert le plus.
Quelle est la raison de cet étrange phénomène? C’est que nous sommes sans cesse dans l’occasion de prononcer qu’une chose est telle; presque jamais dans la
nécessité de déterminer ce que c’est qu’être tel. Nos jugemens les plus fréquens tombent sur des objets particuliers, & le grand usage de la langue & du monde suffit
pour nous diriger. Nous ne faisons que répéter ce que nous avons entendu.”) Denis Diderot, “Encyclopedia,” The Encyclopedia of Diderot & D’Alembert Collaborative
Translation Project, trans. Philip Stewart, Ann Arbor, MI: Scholarly Publishing Office of the University of Michigan Library, 2002. Original French text: Denis Diderot,
Encyclopédie, ou dictionnaire raisonné des sciences, des arts et des métiers, orig. ed. Denis Diderot and Jean le Rond D’Alembert, 5:635–648A (Paris, 1755);
republication: University of Chicago: ARTFL Encyclopédie Projet, Spring 2010 Online edition, Robert Morrissey (ed).
5 Martine Groult, “De la Cyclopaedia de Chambers à l’Encyclopédie: Les renvois de la traduction,” in Corpus 51 (2006): 122–5. My discussion of Chambers here is
greatly indebted to Groult’s expert work on the Encyclopédie.
6 Denis Diderot, “Encyclopedia,” The Encyclopedia of Diderot & D’Alembert Collaborative Translation Project, trans. Philip Stewart, Ann Arbor, MI: Scholarly
Publishing Office of the University of Michigan Library, 2002. (Original French text: “En effet, le but d’une Encyclopédie est de rassembler les connoissances éparses
sur la surface de la terre; d’en exposer le système général aux hommes avec qui nous vivons, & de le transmettre aux hommes qui viendront après nous.”
“Encyclopédie,” Encyclopédie ou Dictionnaire raisonné des sciences, des arts et des métiers, vol. 5, Paris, 1755.) As Daniel Brewer points out, both D’Alembert and
Diderot refer to the Encyclopédie as a “mappemonde” or world map. Brewer, The Discourse of Enlightenment in Eighteenth-Century France. Cambridge: Cambridge
University Press, 1993, 22.

8 Roland Barthes, “The Plates of the Encyclopedia,” in New Critical Essays, trans. Richard Howard, Berkeley, CA: University of California Press, 1980, 29, 33.
9 The Encyclopedia of Diderot & D’Alembert Collaborative Translation Project, trans. Philip Stewart, Ann Arbor, MI: Scholarly Publishing Office of the University of

prévus de loin, & préparés avec adresse, ils donneront à une Encyclopédie le caractere que doit avoir un bon dictionnaire; ce caractere est de changer la façon
commune de penser.” Diderot, “Encyclopédie,” Encyclopédie ou dictionnaire raisonné des sciences, des arts et des métiers, vol. 5, Paris: Briasson, 1755.)

égal et droit … Les articles de Chambers sont assez régulièrement distribués, mais ils sont vides; les nôtres sont pleins, mais irréguliers.” Diderot, “Encyclopédie,”
Encyclopédie ou dictionnaire, V.64Ir/b-64IV/a. as cited by John Lough, The Encyclopédie, London: Longman, 1971, 70. English translation my own. Lough notes, with
regard to the citation form, that Diderot’s article “Encyclopédie” “is foliated not paginated, hence the references which follow are to folios (r=recto, v=verso). It is obvious
that it was composed in a hurry at the last moment and that the printer had to fit it into the volume as best he could,” 69. (5: 642): “Alternativement nains & géants,
