16 Vinay Dharwadker, “Constructions of World Literature in Colonial and Postcolonial India,” The Routledge Companion to World Literature, ed. Theo D’haen, David
Damrosch, and Djelal Kadir, London: Routledge, 2012, 477.
17 Pascale Casanova, ed. Des littératures combatives: L’Internationale des nationalismes littéraires, Paris: Raisons d’agir, 2011; see Thomas Pavel, Univers de la
fiction (Paris: Seuil, 1988), and Tiphaine Samoyault, Excès du roman (Paris: Nadeau, 1999), as well as her “Le roman-fleuve ou le monde déplié,” in Actes du colloque
international d’Oslo: Point de rencontre: le roman (ed. Juliette Frölich, Oslo: KULTskriftserie 37: 2, 1995, 141–56); André Shiffrin, Words and Money (London: Verso,
2010). For a study of the landscape of translation and the international publishing industry, see Gisèle Sapiro, Translatio (Le marché de la traduction en France à
l’heure de la mondialisation) Paris: Éditions du CNRS, 2009.
18 Jean-Luc Nancy, The Sense of the World, trans. Jeffrey Librett, Minneapolis: University of Minnesota Press, 1997, (original French title: Le sens du monde).
Nancy, The Creation of the World, 28.
19 Ibid., 33–4, 49, 51.
20 Ibid., 64, 173. Nancy has the work of Hans Blumenberg in mind when he identifies the task of today as the creation of a new form of symbolization of the world.
Blumenberg’s The Legibility of the World (1979) draws on metaphorology to adduce time-sensitive worlds of sense. Figures that concentrate hermeneutical content like
“the book of nature” or “the book of the world” function as “answer points” to the question of what a world is, posed from the point of view of nature or the cosmos.
Metaphorology in this instance arguably effects a reverse “translation” from phenomena back to human language.
21 Peter Szendy, Kant chez les extraterrestres: Philosofictions cosmopolitiques, Paris: minuit, 2011, 144–5.
22 Gayatri Chakravorty Spivak, entry on “Planetarity” for the English edition of the Vocabulaire (forthcoming, Princeton University Press, 2013). Spivak confirms that
as an English word planetarity was first used in her paper “Imperatives to Re-Imagine the Planet,” presented at Stiftung-Dialogik in Zurich, December 16, 1997. The
revised talk was published in Imperatives to Re-Imagine the Planet/Imperative zur Neuerfindung des Planeten, ed. Willi Goetschel, Vienna: Passagen, 1999.
23 “Heterocosm” is qualified by Sam Tanenhaus as “alternative but accessible worlds, open to us all” that are the result of Harold Bloom’s “esoteric populism.” See
his review of Bloom’s The Anatomy of Influence in the New York Times Book Review, May 22, 2011, 13.

Part Three
Translating “World Literature”

I
Auerbach’s Welt-Theology*
In the life and work of Erich Auerbach, theologies of translation converge with the problem of World Literature. Auerbach arrived in America
in 1948 to take up a position at Penn State. An assimilated Jew, fired from his job in Marburg because of the Nuremburg Laws, he fled to
Istanbul in 1936 and spent eleven years teaching there. He was brought to the University of Istanbul by his friend and rival philologist, Leo
Spitzer. Though he arrived just as Spitzer was preparing to leave for Johns Hopkins, they collaborated on devising a Romance philology
curriculum and published a series of seminar papers that included contributions on and in multiple languages including Turkish.1
In contrast to Spitzer, Auerbach made the condition of his exile from Europe a constant refrain. The “foreign” academic context, and what
he referred to as Kemal Atatürk’s “primal nationalism” (eerily comparable, he affirmed in a letter to Walter Benjamin, to its counterpart in Nazi
Germany), induced a state of acute melancholia. This state was exacerbated by his witness to the “fantastical and ghostly” educational
modernity in which he was a participant; charged as he was with creating a modern European curriculum built on the not-yet extinguished
embers of Arabic and Persian. To quote from his letter to Benjamin:
Here they have thrown all tradition overboard, and they want to build a thoroughly rationalized, extreme Turkish-nationalist, state of the European
sort. It has quickly gone fantastical and ghostly, already there is hardly anyone who can understand Arabic or Persian, and even Turkish texts of
the past century will quickly become un-understandable, [unverständlich] since the language is being simultaneously modernized and newly
oriented on “Ur-Turkish,” and written with Latin characters. “Romanology” is fundamentally a luxury, and I am the only real cultural historian
among the newly hired Europeans. Moreover, I have to organize language instruction in all the Western languages in the university and many
other things besides. The work is really irksome, because one has to battle with the most curious difficulties, misunderstandings, and tensions,
but it is neither personally nor materially uninteresting.
As Robert M. Stein, the translator of this letter, notes:
The real loss of history in this project that claims to draw all its energies from history—the substitution of concrete historical development by its
ghost—is already at the center of Auerbach’s perception, and he sees it in terms of the crisis in language that materially disconnects the present
from the past.2
The temporal disjunction between linguistic past and present manifests itself as untranslatability—or more exactly, a distinct kind of
untranslatability qualified in the letter as unverständlich, or the ununderstandable. This is untranslatability defined not so much in terms of
mistranslation or failure to communicate but as a wedge of opacity in Verstehen perceptible at historical moments of political upheaval and
violent linguistic regime change. Little wonder then, that the project undertaken by Auerbach in Istanbul—the foundational Mimesis:
Dargestellte Wirklichkeit in der abendländischen Literatur, published in 1946 and familiar to Anglophone readers under the title Mimesis:
The Representation of Reality in Western Literature—made the un-understandable an operative principle. The book was hailed as a model
of comparative literature at a time when the discipline was still in its infancy, but Auerbach’s attentiveness to the untranslatability of cultural
expression and to discrepant literary traditions actually downplayed the equivalencies and similitudes cherished by comparatists. The field
that he forged is better labeled, as James I. Porter would have it, “incomparative literature.”3
To the theoretical constructs of un-understandability, untranslatability, and incomparativity (all three legible in the context of political and
historical crises), we can add Auerbach’s notion of “unGoethean” World Literature. The “Goethean,” as Avihu Zakai and others have noted,
was conscripted for a “historicist humanism,” steeped in the permeation of consciousness by historical reality.4 Auerbach mobilized the
adjective ungoethisch to subject to critique the form of non-cosmopolitan World Literature or standardized literary monoculture that he
perceived as ascendant in his own time and that, in his view, resisted historical, philological synthesis.5 Though he could easily have
positioned Goethe’s Weltliteratur as the forerunner of today’s market-driven Global Lit (strategically invented by Goethe to overcome
Germany’s lack of official status as a national polity and absence of a national literature competitive with that of England and France),
Auerbach preferred not to.6 He was more concerned with measuring the distance between Weltliteratur then and now, the better to inveigh
against his own era’s predilection for market-ready clichés that obfuscated the true literary objects of philology and left the shards of
disparate traditions with no way to connect humanistically.
And yet, it was precisely the broken state of postwar Weltliteratur in mass society that arguably lent impetus to Auerbach’s novel
approach to literary history: He reconnected past and present not by charting linear genealogies of influence or geographies of cultural
markets but by proposing an idea of linguistic “reality” that jumped across place and time.7 More important still, he developed the typological
construct of figura to refer to “the conception of reality in late antiquity and the Christian Middle Ages,” according to which “an occurrence on
earth signifies not only itself but at the same time another, which it predicts or confirms, without prejudice to the power of its concrete reality
here and now.”8 Casting a fresh eye on the Christian Middle Ages and the Renaissance, Auerbach repurposed the figural anachronism,
generating a theory of history that recuperated non-causal temporality. The emergent paradigm of a prophetic, discontinuous chronotope
capable of releasing the now-time while projecting a narrative hologram of divine program, can be seen as nothing short of an attempt to
reconcile worldly “reality” (Weltwirklichkeit) with messianic eschatology. In what follows, I will look more closely at the prophetic dimension of
Auerbach’s work, emphasizing how his idea of worldliness, which conflates theology and history, brings him closer to figures like Benjamin
and Taubes in ways that are rarely given their due.9
Auerbach’s references to “world,” and to mots-valises built up from its cognates (Weltgeschichte, Weltwirklichkeit, and Weltphilologie), are
impossible to disentangle from the story of what happened to European intellectuals when they went elsewhere. By all accounts, when
Auerbach first arrived in the United States and began teaching at Penn State, he had some difficulty figuring out where he was, and did his
best to get somewhere else (he spent 1949–1950 as a fellow at Princeton’s Institute for Advanced Study and then left for Yale). During this
short period of post-exile exile, he was thinking a lot about Dante, to whom he had devoted an early book Dante als Dichter der irdischen

Welt in 1929. This classic (rendered in English as Dante: Poet of the Secular World) raises the question of Welt in relation to the seminal
essay that he would publish in 1952, “Philologie der Weltliteratur.”10 The English title, “Philology and Weltliteratur” (Edward and Maire Said’s
translation), suppressed the preposition der, hence losing the genitive, and with it, the sense that Auerbach philology belonged to World
Literature; that World Literature engendered its own very particular philology; or that languages implied specific modes of world-having that
stood outside national, patrimonial, or identity claims.11 This drift is reinforced by Auerbach’s later allusion to “Weltliteratur und ihrer
Philologie,” with “ihrer” underscoring once again that philology remains the property of World Literature. The full sentence in which the phrase
appears is an interesting one: “This conception of Weltliteratur and its philology seems less active, less practical and less political than its
[meaning Goethean] predecessor.”12 The way to de-enervate and repoliticize Weltliteratur is to ground philology in earthly criticism: “In any
event, our philological home is the earth: it can no longer be the nation.” This Heimat-philology, positioned against the “blood and soil”
rhetoric of national socialism’s Heimat fixation, becomes the royal road to “a proper love for the world.”13 Auerbach ultimately wants to see
the world and the universe become parameters for the philological synthesis of Weltliteratur, but for now he will content himself with the earth
expansively apprized.14
Auerbach’s planetary orientation, and more specifically his earthly (irdisch) coloration of Welt (strongly registered in Jane O. Newman’s
rendering of the Weltliteratur essay’s second sentence, “Our planet, the earth—which is the world of world literature—is growing smaller and
becoming less diverse”), anticipates Edward Said’s secular criticism, a connection elaborated in the following chapter. In a broader literary-
historical framework, irdisch may be seen as the hinge between Hippolyte Taine’s “milieu” (the environmental factors shaping literature) and
contemporary articulations of “verbal ecology” that treat literature inclusively as an environment, with reference not just to land, weather and
social mores but to politics and institutional cultures as well.15
What irdisch World Literature amounts to in practice, though, especially in Mimesis, is less an environmental criticism than a theological
figuralism squared improbably with realism.16 In this respect, Auerbach’s work resembles that of a generation of literary critics on his heels
who made the Biblical world a fulcrum of literary criticism and theory; among them Northrop Frye, Harold Bloom, Hans Frei, Frank Kermode
and Robert Alter. If we focus on this theological dimension from a more philosophical perspective, Auerbach also invites comparison with
Jacob Taubes. As a critic with a highly developed sense of his own personal destiny in world-geist who, like the exiled prophet, saw himself
as one “called from without” (to borrow Taubes’s expression), Auerbach was susceptible to a way of thinking (also channeled by Benjamin,
Gershom Scholem, and Franz Rosenzweig) that Taubes referred to as “occidental eschatology.” Taubes later published a thesis under this
title, drafted in the late 1940s and briefly referred to in 1949 by Karl Löwith (in his book Meaning in History: The Theological Implications of
the Philosophy of History). The title was likely unknown to Auerbach, but many phrases resonate with Auerbach’s language: “Israel is the
restless element in world history, the leavening that first actually produces history”; “Life is exiled in the world; the homeland of life is beyond
the world”; “Alienness or exile” means “not to be at home in this world”; “The nonworldly comes into being through the call. That which is
entirely other is audible in this world, but still as that which is entirely other”; “The process of redemption occurs ontologically in God’s
sublation [Aufhebung] of the world.” Auerbach opened Mimesis with the staging of exile, contrasting Odysseus’s unhoming (geographically
defined and situated in relation to a pantheon of Gods) with the existential dislocatedness of Abraham in The Old Testament. God
addresses Abraham from no fixed place, and Abraham himself is wholly abstracted from the real world. “We do not know,” Auerbach begins,
where he is actually, whether in Beersheba or elsewhere, whether indoors or in the open air, is not stated; it does not interest the narrator, the
reader is not informed; and what Abraham was doing when God called to him is left in the same obscurity.17
If Taubes and Auerbach were both drawn to a picture of the Jewish subject as one destituted of world, they also, in different ways, committed
to a vision of culture born of what Taubes called the “Davidic Messiah king.” For Taubes, this is Jesus cast as the Son of David; he who
usurps Saul’s power and brings the kingdom of God closer to earth. This Davidic Messiah heralds a community of philosophical Jews who
transcend the claims of race and nation:
Groups within Roman society are drawn to the synagogue by monotheism and by the concept of the Law. But the Hellenistic Roman who is
attracted to the synagogue by the concept of the divine law is not prepared to take on the practice of the Jewish law. Those who enter the
community so as to lose the last characteristics shared with their nation and their race have no wish to join the national community of the Jews
and exchange a Roman for an Oriental nationality.18
Like Taubes, Auerbach projects, through the very example of Mimesis’s philological practice, a complex community, equal parts Hellenic
and Judeo-Christian. But where Taubes would enjoin eschatology to break the deadlock between an acosmic, anti-worldly Christian
inwardness (religiöse Umkehr) and the Marxian presage of worldly, proletarian revolution (weltliche Umkehrung), Auerbach would pursue a
path through the Old Testament and Dante to arrive at what I would call a prophetic ethical realism.19 This was, in effect, a realism that
articulated the insecurity of the Western world (its vulnerability to catastrophe, its crises induced by theodicy and secularism), while providing
a figural reading of cultural redemption.20
Auerbach described an ongoing connection between eschatology and “real history” in the concluding paragraphs of Dante: Poet of the
Secular World. He suggests that Christian eschatology might in Dante’s time have “lost its unity and vitality,” but “the European mind was so
permeated with the idea of human destiny that even in very un-Christian artists it preserved the Christian force and tension which were
Dante’s gift to posterity.” Dante’s genius, according to Auerbach, was to transfer eschatology in the guise of individual destiny to the “earthly
happening.” He affirmed that after Petrarch and Boccaccio,
the historical world acquired a fully immanent autonomy, and this sense of the self-sufficiency of earthly life spread like a fructifying stream to the
rest of Europe—seemingly quite estranged from its eschatological origin and yet secretly linked with it through man’s irrevocable bond with his
concrete historical fate. 21
This “secret link” would play out in Auerbach’s subsequent work as the secret theology of worldly philology.
In 1948, Auerbach delivered a talk on Dante at Penn State aimed at a general audience. He took his auditors through familiar highlights of
Dante’s life and work, but more than the lecture’s content it is the sound of his voice that is informative. In the recording unearthed by Martin
Vialon, one hears grammatically impeccable English delivered with a throaty, mitteleuropäische intonation. It is the uncanny voice of an exile
in Scranton, Pennsylvania, and its echo carries over into the very short gloss he wrote the following year on a passage of Canto 12 of the
Purgatorio that describes an allegorical bas-relief (Singleton’s commentary reproduces the image of a pavement tomb of the old Church of
Santa Reparata in Florence). As Dante in purgatory will atone for the sin of pride and earn removal of the damning letterP  from his brow, the
engravings accordingly feature souls who have paid for the sin of pride, among them Briareus, a giant who dared to make war against
Olympus; Nimrod, the commissioner of the Tower of Babel; and Niobe, the proud queen whose boasts of a plentiful brood spurred Apollo
and Diana to slay them on behalf of the slighted Latona, and Saul:
As, in order that there be memory of the, the stones in the church floor over the buried dead bear figured what they were before; wherefore many
a time men weep for them there, at the prick of memory that spurs only the faithful: so I saw sculptured there, but of better semblance in respect
of skill, all that for pathway juts out from the mountain …

O Saul, how upon your own sword did you appear there dead on Gilboa, which never thereafter felt rain or dew!22
As a reader of Mimesis, one might assume that Auerbach was attracted to these lines because of their depiction of the sculptor’s feat of
exact realism. Dante’s narrator exclaims:
What master was he of brush or of pencil who drew the forms and lineaments which there would make every subtle genius wonder? Dead the
dead, and the living seemed alive. He who saw the reality of all I trod upon, while I bent down, saw not better than I!
Rather than comment on this rendering of expert mimesis, however, Auerbach fastens on the ethical perplex posed by Saul:
It seems that no sufficient explanation has been given, either by ancient or modern commentators, for the inclusion of Saul among the examples
of superbia carved as bas-reliefs into the marble ground of the first girone of the Purgatorio. For the readers not familiar with the details of
medieval interpretation of the Bible, Saul’s wickedness seems rather to be envy or a kind of wrathful melancholy than pride (tristiria and ira c.
Inferno VII and VIII). Jacopo della Lana, the Anonimo Fiorentino, Francesco Buti and Pietro Alighieri give very general and insufficient
explanations of his superbia such as “because he fought against David,” or “because he did not wish to be captured alive by the Philistines” …
In Christian ethics superbia is linked with the original sin and disobedience to God. By his pride, i.e. by preferring his own will to the order of
God, Adam committed the disobedience which caused the fall of man. Thus, the first examples of pride in Purgatorio XII and several of the
following ones are examples of disobedience to God or contempt of divine power.
This disobedience, caused by pride, was committed by Saul when he acted against the will of God announced to him by Samuel: first by
offering the sacrifice before the arrival of Samuel (1 Samuel 13, 8–14)—and even more when he spared the life of Agag, the king of Amalec,
and saved from destruction the best of the booty, against the express orders of God (1 Samuel 15). From that moment God abandons him
and confers the kingdom upon another, upon David; from that moment God sends him “the evil spirit” who darkens his life … commentators
agree in considering these disobediences as superbia and as the cause of Saul’s misfortunes and his final ruin.23
In his late, posthumously published Literary Language & its Public in Latin Antiquity and in the Middle Ages (1958), Auerbach would
focus on the Saul, who “keeps the booty” against God’s orders. He refers to a passage of William Langland’s Piers Plowman in which Saul’s
retribution is used for practical ethics. It becomes a cautionary tale about the dangers of greed and profiteering; demonstrating, in the
historical context of fourteenth-century England, why priests should not take money for performing mass. The Saul episode stands as one of
“numerous examples of a practical investigation of social justice, conducted on a Biblical foundation.” In this instance, English ethical
realism, rooted in a down-to-earth scene of instruction, is staked out in marked contrast to Dante’s “God-given program for the earthly
world.”24
Saul’s Pride evinces a wholly different approach to ethics, realism, the earthly, and the God-given. Here, Saul represents the culpable
Jew, punished for performing sacrifices in disregard of God’s command, but he also represents the Jew as anointed prophet with reference
to an episode where he “prophesies among” a band of prophets sent by God to meet him in Gib´e-ah (1 Samuel 10). What interests
Auerbach is
“the principle involved,” which, he notes, “seems strange to modern minds, [it] is the principle of ‘polysemy’ in the figurative interpretation: it very
often considers, as in our case, the same person in morally contradictory meanings. The same Saul who has been rejected for his superbia is as
a figura Christi.25
Auerbach’s figura Christi, like Taubes’s Davidic Messiah (or for that matter, Hélène Cixous’s characterization of Derrida as “a Jewish
saint”), is more than just a trope of typological prefiguration; it is a performative of belief, a proof of the leap of faith that enables opposing
positions to be held together in non-contradiction, permanently resisting sublation. What is at stake is the naming of a Jewish Christ; at once
the sinning Jew covered with superbia and the Jewish God-elect. To understand this further, we do well to turn to Giorgio Agamben’s
analysis of the figure of “Jesus Messiah” as read through Paul. Agamben refers to this figure technically as a “nominal syntagma,” ascribing
to it the status of preeminent word of faith:26
What then does it mean that in Paul faith is expressed in the nominal syntagma “Jesus Messiah” and not the verbal syntagma “Jesus is the
Messiah”? Paul does not believe that Jesus possesses the quality of being the Messiah; he believes in “Jesus Messiah” and that is all. Messiah is
not a predicate tacked on to the subject Jesus, but something that is inseparable from him, without, however, constituting a proper name. For
Paul, this is faith; it is an experience of being beyond existence and essence, as much beyond subject as beyond predicate.
According to the logic of the nominal syntagma, Auerbach’s Saul emerges as a figure of political agency that comes into existence through
his own pronunciation (as in the kerygmatic proclamation assigned full thrush by Auerbach’s contemporary, the theological thinker Rudolf
Bultmann). In his guise as a figura Christi, Saul passes over the traditional dichotomy between republican and monarchist interpretations of
his fate: The former represents Saul as an unworthy legatee of his grandfather’s civic honor, the latter venerates him as a just and entitled
ruler commendable for showing mercy to his enemies. Auerbach’s Saul, by contrast, functions as a portal into an eschatology of world history
grounded in the “realism” of modern ethics; a world of ethical ambivalence in which P and not-P—the mutually cancelling values of Saul
superbia and Saul Messiah—coexist in the one Saul. Auerbach’s own practice of literary criticism models this principle insofar as it
conjugates the language of the earthly world, or mimetic realism, with what Paolo Valesio terms “theo-rhetoric.”27 The nominal syntagma of
realism and theology, like that of materialism and messianism in Benjamin, can be ascribed to the broader field of the Auerbachian
unverständlich insofar as it signifies an “exceptional” conjunction of secularism and divinity that is virtually beyond belief; that is to say, at the
razor’s edge of logical credibility.28
The problem of belief in Auerbach’s thought, rife with implications for how Welt-theology relates to realism and humanism, solicits
multiple interpretations. If belief is synonymous with belief in absolute Truth, then for some readers of Auerbach his notions of reality, realism
and everydayness are simply patches for the post-Enlightenment forfeiture of belief in absolute Truth and “cognitive and existential certainty”
(the argument of Hans Ulrich Gumbrecht). Adopting an analytic philosophical orientation, Barry Maine’s 1999 article “Erich Auerbach’s
‘Mimesis’ and Nelson Goodman’s “‘Ways of Worldmaking’: A Nominal(ist) Revision” aligns Auerbachian realism with philosophical realism
and applies a “constructivist” approach to the reality of words. The truth of realist textual representation depends on how to define a
believable truth-claim; on nominalist arguments around causality, existence, and the status of abstract objects (numbers, ethical properties)
in space and time. Belief in its relation to realism is taken in a wholly different ethico-religious direction by James I. Porter, whose basic
question, addressed to Auerbachian philology, is, How Jewish is it? The figure of Saul is relevant here as well. InM imesis, Saul and David
are evoked as characters “fraught with background”; that is to say, possessed of “great depths of time, fate and consciousness” in contrast
to their Homeric counterparts, defined as agential but without deep consciousness. The Bible “wins” over Hellenism in this instance, by
delivering psychological realism. Porter implies that these prophet-seers of the Old Testament are linked in Auerbach’s mind to a figure like
Rousseau, who epitomizes the faith-versus-reason crisis of the Enlightenment; a crisis leading to a debilitating “insecurity” in the Christian
world that reappears in a new guise as the crisis of national socialism in Europe.29 Porter plumbs Auerbach’s “Homeric/biblical-Jewish”
antinomies—treating them like prequels to the culture wars—but for my purposes his most interesting point is that Auerbach brings the
eschatological principles of “time, truth and revelation” (which he identifies as the very things Auerbach’s Jewish philology wants to

recuperate from a Nazified, de-Judaicized philology) down to earth, so to speak, in the representation of everyday reality. He suggests that
Auerbach brings the figural force of revelation and what Jane O. Newman characterizes as his desire for a re-sacralization of society to the
task of “realist world-making,” which is to say, to the politics of just worlds.30
This commitment comes into sharp relief in Auerbach’s essay on Pascal—“On the Political Theory of Pascal3”1—written in Istanbul in the
1940s, with a later version published in The Hudson Review in 1951. He begins with Pascal’s vexed approach to the Christian person of
faith in the age of absolutism. Why must the obedient Christian endure a regime of evil and injustice? Pascal, according to Auerbach,
determined that
the Christian religion is the only religion against nature and against common sense. To arrive at such an extremist conclusion he [Pascal] needed
the pessimistic and nominalistic ideas of the theorists of the raison d’État; he combined them with Augustinism; thus he created a conception
which, in spite of its Christian radicalism, contains germs of revolutionary criticism. The theorists of the raison d’État, more or less radically, more
or less reluctantly, had expressed the doctrine that the State, in order to fulfill its task, is not obliged to follow the laws of morality: fraud and
cunning, treason and perfidy, violence and cruelty, are legitimate in politics; the right of the State extends as far as it might and is based on it.
Pascal is willing to agree with all this. But those theorists were interested in the State for the State’s sake; they considered it as a terminal value;
they either enjoyed its dynamic vitality … or they were interested in the benefits it might provide for the individual if it were organized in the right
way.
All that is utterly immaterial to Pascal. For him, a dynamic life of the State did not exist; or if it existed, he would consider it as profoundly
wicked; he had no interest in the best possible form of the State, for all forms seemed to him equally bad. He combines the pessimistic empiricism
of the political theorists with Augustinian ideas; thus, he got to the paradox of might as a pure evil, which one has to obey unconditionally, without
any regard to a possible benefit—but without devotion, or better, out of devotion to God.32
In the manner of Rousseau and Saul, Pascal functions in this instance as a nominal syntagma—a figure signifying the leap of faith required to
hold in one concept both reason of state and faith without reason. These nominal syntagmas are far from being ethical ends in themselves,
but they name those rare instances in which worldly ethics and theology (or realism and eschatology)—though strictly speaking mutually
disqualifying—are, exceptionally, one and the same.33 Pascal’s Evil, Rousseau’s Insecurity, Saul’s Pride—each qualifies as an example of
the theological figuralism that contoured Auerbach’s thinking about “world” and Weltliteratur from the late 1920s to the early 1950s. They
prompt consideration of his work in the larger frame not only of Taubes’s Occidental eschatology or his own theory of the unverständlich in
models of World Literature past and future but also in relation to Said’s terrestrial humanism; his critical figuralism of just worlds.

* This chapter is indebted to the work of Jane O. Newman, in particular two chapters of her book-in-progress on Auerbach; the first “Theologus Auerbach” (ms.),
the second, “Force and Justice: Auerbach’s Pascal.” Of particular relevance to my concerns are her discussions of “theo-existential issues” informed by “Dante’s
Thomist realism as Auerbach saw it” and “Pascal’s Jansenism” (in “Theologus Auerbach”). Points of Departure: Political Theology on the Scenes of Early Modernity,
ed. Julia Reinhard and Graham Hammill, Chicago: University of Chicago Press, 2011, 304–36.

Translation Zone: A New Comparative Literature, Princeton, NJ: Princeton University Press, 2006; and Kader Konuk’s East-West Mimesis: Auerbach in Turkey,

essentially Eurocentric scope of Mimesis” and therefore justify a “figural” (in Auerbach’s sense of figura) reading of the reforms in Auerbach’s masterwork and in
Turkey’s humanist legacy more generally (19).
2 Erich Auerbach, “Letter 8” (to Walter Benjamin), trans. Martin Elsky, Martin Vialon, and Robert Stein, included in special feature “Scholarship in Times of
Extremes: Letters of Erich Auerbach (1933–46) on the Fiftieth Anniversary of his Death,” PMLA 122: 3, May 2007, 749. Robert M. Stein, “After Culture: Erich Auerbach
and Walter Benjamin in Correspondence,” unpublished paper.
3 Porter elaborates as follows: “Far from constituting a project in comparativism, thanks to which Auerbach would be remembered as the father of modern
comparative literature, his juxtaposition of the two major literary and cultural traditions of the West creates a dissonance within the projects of reading, of classicism, of
Biblical scholarship, and of literary history and criticism—in short, of philology. Auerbach ought to be remembered as the father of incomparative literature! By indexing
the present moment in his materials, Auerbach, the displaced German Jew in Istanbul, is historicizing philology. At the same time he is inverting the political polarities
of philology, not least by contrasting the two treatments (Homeric/biblical-Jewish) of time, truth, and revelation in the two traditions that he is less comparing than he is
critically pitting against each other. And he is undertaking all of this in opposition to the ingrained tendencies of an anti-Semitic classical philology and in the context of
efforts in Germany to de-Judaize Christianity. Auerbach is in a sense Judaizing philology. That is, he is constructing a new oppositional Jewish philology.” James I.
Porter, “Erich Auerbach and the Judaizing of Philology,” Critical Inquiry, Autumn 2008, 120–1.
4 Avihu Zakai, “Erich Auerbach and the Crisis of German Philology: An Apology for Western Judeo-Christian Tradition in an Age of Peril, Tyranny and Barbarism,”
unpublished book manuscript.
5 See Erich Auerbach, “Philologie der Weltliteratur,” in Weltliteratur: Festgabe für Fritz Strich zum 70 Geburtstag, ed. W. Muschg von W. Henzen and E. Staiger,
Bern: Francke, 1952, 40. The Said translation: “Philology and Weltliteratur,” trans. Edward and Maire Said, Centennial Review 13: 1, 1969, 3. This notion of the
“ungoethean” is lost in the Saids’ translation, where it is rendered as “what Goethe had in mind.”
6 Jonathan Arac makes just this point: “In Goethe’s time (1749–1832) no political entity existed that could be called Germany and we may imagine this to be a
significant reason for the appeal of the ‘world’ to Goethe, as over against the national state cultures of England and France.” Jonathan Arac, Shades of the Planet:
American Literature as World Literature, ed. Wai Chee Dimock and Lawrence Buell, Princeton, NJ: Princeton University Press, 2007, 22.
7 In Mimesis, Auerbach criticized “the statically aprioristic categories of Thucydides,” and more generally “an ethically oriented historiography” that uses “an
unchangeable system of categories and hence cannot produce synthetic-dynamic concepts of the kind we are accustomed to employ today.” Epochal categories—
Renaissance, Enlightenment, Romanticism, and so on—were better equipped in his view to “cover phenomena in motion.” Erich Auerbach, Mimesis: The
Representation of Reality in Western Literature, trans. Willard Trask, Princeton, NJ: Princeton University Press, 2006, 39 and 38, respectively.
8 Ibid., 555.
9 Jane O. Newman’s as yet unpublished essay “Theologus Auerbach” draws out a comparison between what she calls Auerbach’s “poetical theology” and Carl
Schmitt’s political theology.
10 Erich Auerbach, “Philologie der Weltliteratur,” in Weltliteratur: Festgabe für Fritz Strich zum 70 Geburtstag. ed. W. Muschg von W. Henzen and E. Staiger,
Bern: Francke, 1952, 39–50; “Philology and Weltliteratur,” trans. Edward and Maire Said, Centennial Review 13: 1, 1969, 1–17.
11 Emily Cersonsky, in an unpublished paper, has noted that “Philology and Weltliteratur,” in substituting the conjunction “and” for the original “der,” “democratizes
these two disciplines, preparing the reader not for a discussion of Weltliteratur’s influence on philology, but for a consideration of how these two disciplines reflect upon
and influence each other.” In her new translation, Jane O. Newman renders the title more faithfully as “The Philology of World Literature.” Time, History and Literature:
Selected Essays of Erich Auerbach, ed. James I. Porter, trans. Jane O. Newman, Princeton, NJ: Princeton University Press, 2013. My thanks to Jane O. Newman for
allowing me early access to a draft version of her translation.
12 Said, “Philology and Weltliteratur,” 6 (“Diese Vorstellung von der Weltliteratur und ihrer Philologie erscheint weniger aktiv, weniger praktisch und weniger politisch
als die einstige.” Auerbach, “Philologie der Weltliteratur,” 40.)
13 (“Die rechte Liebe zur Welt”); Auerbach, “Philologie der Weltliteratur,” 50; Edward and Maire Said, “Philology and Weltliteratur,” Philologie der Weltliteratur,” 17.
14 Said, “Philology and Weltliteratur,” 17.
15 See, for example, Wai Chee Dimock’s discussion of literary ecologies in her essay “African, Caribbean, American: Black English as Creole Tongue,” in Shades
of the Planet, or Alexander J. Beecroft, “World Literature without a Hyphen: Towards A Typology of Literary Systems,” New Left Review 54 November/December 2008,
91. Beecroft affirms that Niklas Luhmann’s discussion of environment in his book Ecological Communication informs his turn from economic to ecological metaphors
for World Literature. Charting a new theoretical direction entirely, Timothy Morton orients ecological thought away from the Romantic cultural construct of nature in his
books Ecology Without Nature (Cambridge, MA: Harvard University Press, 2007) and The Ecological Thought (Cambridge, MA: Harvard University Press, 2010).
16 On the attribution of Weltliteratur to Wieland, see John Pizer, The Idea of World Literature, Baton Rouge, LA: Louisiana State University Press, 2006, 1.

17 “Occidental eschatology” (“Abendländische eschatologie”), “Israel is the restless element …”: Taubes, 16; “Life is exiled in the world …”: ibid., 27; “Alienness or
Exile”: ibid., 26; “The nonworldly comes into being …”: ibid., 31; “The process of redemption …”: ibid., 38; Jacob Taubes, Occidental Eschatology, trans. David
Ratmoko, Stanford, CA: Stanford University Press, 2009. Auerbach, Mimesis, 8.
18 Taubes, 50, 61.
19 Timothy Bahti, in his seminal essay “Auerbach’s Mimesis: Figural Structure and Historical Narrative,” also reads eschatology into Auerbach. In bracketing
Auerbach with Benjamin, through the “allegory of the eschatology of historical meaning,” he makes a polemical point about literary history’s relationship to theory: “The
senses of the alternate allegories of history—nihilism or eschatology, dead meaning or meaning deferred—might be borne in mind today whenever literary history would
be so blithely opposed to the labyrinths of literary theory.” Timothy Bahti, After Strange Texts: The Role of Theory in the Study of Literature, ed. Gregory Jan and David
Miller, Tuscaloosa, AL: University of Alabama Press, 1985, 145.
20 In the early Dante book, Auerbach described the crisis in terms of Dante’s commitment to a Thomist vision of the “physical, ethical, and political unity of the
Scholastic Christian cosmos at a time when it was beginning to lose its ideological integrity.” Erich Auerbach, Dante: Poet of the Secular World, trans. Ralph
Manheim, Chicago: University of Chicago Press, 1961, 175.
21 Auerbach, Dante, 178.
22 Erich Auerbach, “On Dante,” sound recording of a lecture delivered at Penn State University, 1948. See, too, Martin Vialon’s discussion of this recording in an
essay published in the collective volume Erich Auerbach, Geschichte und Aktualität eines europäischen Philologen, ed. Martin Treml and Karl Heinz Barck, Berlin:
Kadmos Verlag, 2007; Dante Alighieri, The Divine Comedy: Purgatorio, trans. Charles S. Singleton, Princeton, NJ: Princeton University Press, 1973, 121–3.
23 Ibid., 125, lines 64–9; Erich Auerbach, “Saul’s Pride,” 40–2, Modern Language Notes 64: 4, April 1949, 267–9.
24 Erich Auerbach, Literary Language & its Public in Latin Antiquity and in the Middle Ages, trans. Ralph Manheim, Princeton, NJ: Princeton University Press,
1993, 236; ibid., 236.
25 Auerbach, “Saul’s Pride,” 268.
26 Agamben associates the nominal syntagma with “the zero degree copula, a complete assertion that does not contain a verbal predicate” (130). The expression
of faith arises, he says, from what Paul is doing with Deuteronomy. “Having just affirmed that the Messiah is the telos of the law, Paul, in a typical gesture of
hermeneutic violence, transfers onto faith and the Messiah what in Deuteronomy referred to Mosaic law … The word nearby, which was a word of command, now
becomes a ‘word of faith’” (130). Giorgio Agamben, The Time that Remains, trans. Patricia Dailey, Stanford, CA: Stanford University Press, 2005, 127.
27 Agamben, The Time that Remains, 130; see Rudolf Bultmann, Kerygma and Myth: A Theological Debate, ed. Hans Werner Bartsch, New York: HarperCollins,
2000; Paolo Valesio, foreword to Erich Auerbach, Scenes from the Drama of European Literature, Minneapolis: University of Minnesota Press, 1984, xvi.
28 Similarities in the thinking of Auerbach and Benjamin are not purely incidental. They shared a friendship going back to 1924, when they met in the
Staatsbibliothek in Berlin. Benjamin was writing his Habilitationsschrift, Der Ursprung des deutschen Trauerspiels and Auerbach was there working as a librarian.
29 Hans Ulrich Gumbrecht, “Pathos of the Earthly Progress: Erich Auerbach’s Everydays,” in Literary History and the Challenge of Philology: The Legacy of Erich
Auerbach, ed. Seth Lerer, Stanford, CA: Stanford University Press, 1996, 26; Barry Maine, “Erich Auerbach’s ‘Mimesis’ and Nelson Goodman’s “‘Ways of World
Making: A Nominal(ist) Revision,” Poetics Today 20, 1 (Spring, 1999): 41–52; James I. Porter, “Erich Auerbach and the Judaizing of Philology,” Critical Inquiry 35: 1,
Autumn 2008.
30 With regard to Franz Rosenzweig’s 1921 book Star of Redemption, Peter Eli Gordon asserts, “Rosenzweig is tempted to look upon any concession to
theological history as a denial of genuine theology. He feared it will lead to historicism, a mode of thinking which contextualizes and thereby destroys the actuality and
immediacy of biblical revelation. Consequently, for Rosenzweig history is evacuated of genuinely theological significance, and against the traditional understanding of
the Jews as a people bound to God who acts within history. Rosenzweig asserts the modernist vision of history as void of redemptive significance, and casts the
Jewish devotion to God as a suprahistorical condition of complete inaction.” Peter Eli Gordon, “The Concept of the Apolitical: German Jewish Thought and Weimar
Political Theology,” Social Research 74, Fall 2007, 865. Following Gordon’s exposition, one might characterize Auerbach’s position as the exact antithesis of
Rosenzweig’s. Auerbach insists on reconciling earthly historicism with a theology of revelation. For him, revelation can be contextualized (through figuralism), affording
it “actuality and immediacy” as an event of this world, seated in history and politics.
31 “Vier Untersuchungen zur Geschichte der französischen Bildung”
32 Erich Auerbach, “The Triumph of Evil in Pascal,” The Hudson Review 4: 1, Spring 1951, 78–9.
33 See Gumbrecht, “Pathos of the Earthly Progress,” 30–1. Stressing Auerbach’s theme of the “pathos of earthly progress,” part of a phrase taken from an essay
on Proust that Auerbach wrote after the publication of the Recherche, Gumbrecht lends credence to my reading of Auerbach’s ethical realism as coextensive with
Welt-theology. He notes that Auerbach was “not primarily interested in the world-creating function of memory but praised Proust for his representation of ‘the earthly
world’ as an ‘unknown, unexplored, mysteriously composed substance,’” and pointed to the last sentence of the essay that characterizes Proust’s opus as “the true
epic of the soul.” Gumbrecht, who adduces these examples in the name of Auerbach’s “practical existentialism,” offers, in this keen illustration of a Dantesque Proust
(what I’m tempted to call “Proust, poet of the secular world”), a sense of how theological telos imbues reality, everydayness, realism and earthliness in Auerbach’s
critical vocabulary.

II
Said’s Terrestrial Humanism
Edward Said’s late works Freud and the Non-European (2003), Humanism and Democratic Criticism (2003) and the posthumously
published On Late Style: Music and Literature Against the Grain (2006) attest to humanism’s persistence, both as a continuation of Leo
Spitzer’s exemplary investigations of “the modes of persistence of texts” and as an essential blueprint of the humanities in future iterations of
World Literature. Though Orientalism will, undoubtedly, remain Said’s most influential book, these later texts attest to the generative
possibilities of Saidian humanism for comparative literature as a discipline and World Literature as one of its historic subfields. A number of
critical studies of Said’s oeuvre support this surmise. Anthony C. Alessandrini focuses on the re-baptism of Said’s “residual humanism” as
“emergent humanism” (the former a term coined by Said himself to describe the kind of accusations typically lobbed against much of his
work outside of Orientalism). William V. Spanos’s The Legacy of Edward W. Said (2009) attends to the fact that Said “nowhere theorizes”
the history of humanism’s usage nor exactly “what he means by it” and insists that the stakes are of the highest order, pertaining to the
“global consequences for oppositional thought and politics.”1 For Spanos, Saidian humanism—what he calls his “historicist philology”—
contains the possibility of a radically worldly poststructuralist criticism.
In a collective volume, Edward Said: The Legacy of a Public Intellectua,l edited by Ned Curthoys and Debjani Ganguly, Patrick Wolfe
places the comparative study of settler-colonialisms within the compass of Saidian humanism, emphasizing
relationships such as those between Chinese and Tibetans, Tswana and Khoi-San, Russians and Chetchens, or Indonesians and Papuans … as
well as a “settler-colonialism that is internal to Europe, such as those imposed on the Irish, Basque or Sami peoples.”
In his contribution “Edward Said’s Unhoused Philological Humanism,” Curthoys credits Said’s exilic humanism with “enabling the
adventurous intellectual to reflect on their motivations, peculiar characteristics and worldly circumstances.” Debjani Ganguly associates
Saidian humanism with a vision that challenges “the three ‘negative’ models of human existence in the world today: nationalism, religious
ethnocentrism and exclusivism derived from identitarian thought.”2 Said’s philological humanism is cast as translational, transnational,
transtemporal and (according to Curthoys and Ganguly in their Introduction) “consciously at odds with dominant political and economic
registers of the ‘global.’”3 From this perspective, translational humanism bequeaths a literary cartography calibrated to the social life of
political cultures in real time. This is humanism charged with continually re-translating the cultural predicates of world literatures as they
fluctuate relationally; inflected by the now-ness of actuality, the contemporaneity of the event.
To understand more fully this characterization of Saidian humanism as a call to revise conventional terrestrial and territorial configurations
as well as the temporal coordinates of conventional periodization (intractably oriented around Europe-based chronotopes and genres such
as classicism, the Renaissance, romanticism, modernism, and so forth), it is useful to reconsider briefly how Said himself defined
humanism’s compact, especially vis-à-vis some of the “strong critics” of his time. Said forged his positions confrontationally in response to
two titans, Northrop Frye and Harold Bloom, both of whom, far more than Leo Spitzer or Erich Auerbach, set the terms of literary criticism
and theory for his generation. I would even conjecture that Said’s recourse to Spitzer and Auerbach was at least in part an effort to effect an
end-run around Frye and Bloom. When Said wrote in Freud and the Non-European that it was Auerbach who “had the capacity to sense that
a new historical era was being born, and he could tell that its lineaments and structures would be unfamiliar precisely because so much in it
was neither European nor Eurocentric,” he may have been erecting the figure of an anti-Eurocentric Auerbach in order to counter more
proximate forms of Eurocentrism associated with Bloom and Frye.4
In Said’s first book, Beginnings, Bloom is approvingly cited in relation to Walter Jackson Bate as the namesake of a revitalized influence
studies. But, by the time we reach Humanism and Democratic Criticism, Bloom has become the avatar of humanism’s wrong turn. As one
may recall, in 1998, the year Bloom put his imprimatur on humanism with a best-selling book Shakespeare: The Invention of the Human
(cementing his status as arbiter of the Western canon), he was also stepping up his campaign against what he disparagingly dubbed the
“school of resentment.” Resentment (ressentiment) is a particularly injurious word, leaving the party that is accused of it locked into a
position of psychic defensiveness, seeking scapegoats on which to displace feelings of failure, humiliation, and inferiority. It is a word often
used in pop-psychological explanations of Arab and Muslim hostility to Western secular culture. For Bloom, it also works as an exclusionary
term insofar as “resentniks”—critics who allegedly downgrade aesthetics for the sake of ideology—no longer belong to the fold of true
humanists who understand that a full-value humanities (epitomized by the Shakespearean human) possesses the force of secular religion.
For Said, the stakes went well beyond the condemnation of Bloom’s restriction of humanism to predominantly Euro, Anglo male authors.
Bloom’s “vatic trumpetings” were objectionable, to be sure, but it was his preeminent hold on Gnostic humanism together with his self-
appointment as guardian of the literary that were most worthy of contestation.5
Northrop Frye was a less controversial figure for Said, but he too was identified with a tradition of theological hermeneutics that impinged
on Said’s vision of secular criticism. In Said’s estimation, Frye’s 1957 Anatomy of Criticism offered a “Blakean-Jungian synthesis of the
humanistic system organized into a mini-life-world with its own seasons, cycles, rituals, heroes, social classes, and utopian pastoral as well
as urban settings,” which, though impressive as “the last synthesis of a worldview in the American humanities,” led inexorably to a Blakean
idea of the “human divine, a Judeo-Christian Eurocentric norm.” Whereas Said remained circumspect, Fredric Jameson cast an approving
eye on Frye’s construct of “the social,” deriving from Frye’s criticism of myth a “willingness to raise the issue of community and to draw basic,
essentially social, interpretive consequences from the nature of religion as collective representation.” Jameson also credited Frye’s
figuralism with leading to a demystificatory reading conducive to exposing the mythological substrate of ideology. Jameson’s proto-Marxist
Frye had no proto-postcolonial counterpart for Said. From Said’s viewpoint, Frye’s unproblematized sense of the “humanistic world of
agency” and insufficient attention to the politics of nationalism were major flaws as was his obliviousness to institutions like “the monarchy,
the treasury, colonial companies and land-settlement agencies.”6 Said placed institutional critique at the heart of humanism, for it was
precisely an institution’s structural hold on power and belief systems that made it a catalyst of secular criticism.

Aamir Mufti has crucially glossed Said’s notion of secular criticism as “critical secularism.” In his preface to a special issue ofb oundary
2, he wrote:
Said’s use of the term secular involves a displacement of its usual significations. Secular criticism in Said’s reckoning is, first of all, a practice of
unbelief; it is directed, however, not simply at the objects of religious piety but at secular “beliefs” as well, and, at its most ambitious, at all those
moments at which thought and culture become frozen, congealed, thinglike and self-enclosed … At no point is secular used in his work in simple
opposition to the religious per se. Above all, his concern has been with domination through the classification and management of cultures and of
human collectivities, into mutually distinct and immutable entities, be they nations, properly speaking, or civilizations or ethnicities. To the great
modern system for the classification of cultures Said gave the name Orientalism and viewed the hierarchies of this system as marking the
presence of a “reconstructed religious impulse, a naturalized supernaturalism.”7
Mufti points up the extent to which critical secularism functions as a theoretical pragmatics, a criticism of the mutually authorizing discourses
of humanism and imperialism, as well as a means of practicing worldliness.
For insight into what this worldiness entails, we do well to return to Edward and Maire Said’s 1969 translation of Auerbach’s 1952 essay
“Philology and Weltliteratur,” which seeded the important concept of Welt in Said’s oeuvre. In their introductory prelude to the translation, the
Saids noted that Goethe used Weltliteratur to designate “universal literature, or literature which expresses Humanität, humanity.”8 In
reworking the term for postwar times, they were clearly attuned to Auerbach’s imagination of a Weltliteratur that was not standardized but
made to measure on a planetary scale,9 suggesting that Auerbachian humanism stressed that “where earlier epochs dared to designate
man’s place in the universe … our philological home is the earth. It can no longer be the nation.” In calling for a “prenational” sense of Geist,
they said Auerbach had opened the borders to “a proper love for the world.”10
Welt and Erde, marked terms of Auerbach’s mature internationalism, redound to the title of his 1929 book Dante als Dichter der
irdischen Welt, which was rendered in English as Dante: Poet of the Secular World. Ralph Manheim’s translation of the title was
consequential for Saidian humanism. “Secular” is something of a mistranslation of “irdisch,” normally defined in English as earthly, temporal,
or transient. Moreover, secular translates into German as Weltlich, which would produce the tautology Weltlichen Welt or “Dante: Poet of the
Worldly World.” This figure of the “worldly world” may, however, be precisely what Saidian humanism was after. It arguably works well for
Saidian philology by underscoring the “earthliness” of secularism; something we see in evidence in his 2005 preface to Mimesis, in which,
following Auerbach, he stresses Dante’s ability to articulate earthly paradise and human incarnation by pushing the conventions of the sermo
humilis or low style for communicating the Christian sublime. As distinct from philosophical ascriptions of Abgrund, or the Heideggerian
terms Grund and Erdherrschaft (tainted by a nationalist rhetoric of nation and soil), irdisch connotes spirit imbued with the terrestrial, the
fleshly, the material, the mundane. And in the longer sequence of Untranslatables that includes Welt, Mundus, World, Terre, Cosmos,
Chôra, Globe, and Planetarity, irdisch takes its place as crucially constitutive of what Said, in the finale of his 1979 essay “Zionism from the
Standpoint of Its Victims,” characterized as political worldliness:
Finally, (a) what role as a producer of criticism and historical knowledge does the Western intellectual play given the background of Occidental
domination and oppression of the non-Occidental world; (b) what is the meaning of community given the construction and abuse of Others—
women, Blacks, Palestinians, etc.—and given also the sustained production of alienating technological discourses (colluded in by liberal
intellectuals) in the advanced capitalist world?
To this cluster of problems the critical consciousness can respond only with: the study of history, a belief in rational knowledge, a strong sense
of what political life is all about, a set of values grounded absolutely in human community, democracy, and faith in the future. Thus do theory and
praxis become aspects of each other, when intellectual work more closely approaches political worldliness.11
One of the most difficult tasks set by this terrestrial humanism is the naming of Palestine. The vexed philology related to the terms “Palestine”
and “Palestinian,” much like that related to “Jersualem” and “Jews,” triggers a syndrome associated by Mufti (in his essay “The Missing
Homeland of Edward Said”) with the problem of what it means “to be inside or outside a place that does not exist.” In “Zionism from the
Standpoint of its Victims,” Said deplored strategic efforts to confine the name Palestine to usage as an administrative designation valid only
during the Roman Empire. “There are innumerable and ongoing references to the name in Arabic from the ninth century on, as well as in
European literature from the Middle Ages to the present,” he points out. “Epistemologically, however, the name Palestine has transmuted
from a reality into a non-reality.” Said cites Moshe Dayan’s chilling words: “You do not even know the names of these Arab villages, and I
don’t blame you, because … these geography books no longer exist … the Arab villages are not there either.” In an interview, Daniel
Barenboim confirmed how this attitude prevailed when he was growing up in Israel in the 1950s:
We told ourselves that either the Palestinians had left because they wanted to leave, or they were encouraged to leave by other Arabs. I don’t
think I met a Palestinian growing up. I was a patriot. Then I remember in September 1970, when King Hussein killed a lot of Palestinians, Golda
Meir saying: “Who are these Palestinian people? We are the people of Palestine.” And for the first time I thought to myself, that can’t be right.12
Mufti takes full account of the strange life of “the name of the non-place” of Palestine. It has become, he writes, “in the process of its
disappearance a signifier that attaches itself to too many things, places and memories.” So, too, has the name for the Palestinian people,
which circulates as a generic, globally claimed moniker for dispersal and disposability, for an internationally iconic deterritorialization.
Palestiños, Mufti reminds us, is the Cuban term for rural migrant. Functioning in this regard as a paronym (a word with shared cognates and
multiple significations), it resembles the term musulman, a term of concentration camp argot applied to the survivor, Othered by encroaching
death and the most tenuous hold on bare life. The transportability of these names—“Palestinian,” “Muslim,” and “Jew”—reveals reservoirs of
historical irony and co-implication. Taking his cue from Hannah Arendt, Mufti notes, for example, that “the Jewish question was resolved by
producing a new category of refugees. Palestinian is the name of that refugee.” Palestinians also inherit the dubious mantle of statelessness
from the Jews. As Mufti notes, by the ’50s and ’60s there was a momentous “transference of the problem of statelessness from Europe to
the Third World.” Even more recently, Mufti argues, “Palestinian” has come to signify “a figure of blocked and thwarted mobility that negates
myths of large-scale mobility that structure thinking in the contemporary world.” This leads to what he calls “the maddening paradox of any
attempt at mobility if you come from nowhere.”
The story of a homeland gone missing is most strikingly rendered by Said in After the Last Sky, a photographic album of Palestinian lives
published in 1986, for which Said wrote the text that accompanied photos by Jean Mohr. I purchased my copy of this work in a bookstore in
Istanbul, and reading it there, was inspired to think about secular criticism as a distinct form of Saidian humanism that fit with cities of cultural
transverse, theological complexity, translatio imperii, and dense archaeologies of knowledge. Sensitive to the obvious parallels between
Istanbul in the 1930s, a haven to displaced German and Austrian humanists, and Said’s poignant writings about Palestinian dislocation, I
came to understand Saidian humanism as a new foundation of the discipline of comparative literature; one that emphasizes exilic
consciousness; the ethics of the human; textual filiation; worldliness; the critique of embedded epistemologies of Orientalism; and what I
would call negative philology—a play on negative theology that uses the syntax of negation to designate what is no longer there. In After the
Last Sky, Palestine is named in a drumbeat of “no’s” and “nots”:
There is no great episode in our history that establishes imperatives for our future course, partly because our past is still ragged, discredited, and
unassimilated … We have no dominant theory of Palestinian culture, history, society; we cannot rely on one central image (exodus, holocaust,

long march); there is no completely coherent discourse adequate to us, and I doubt whether at this point, if someone could fashion such a
discourse, we could be adequate for it. Miscellaneous, the spaces here and there in our midst include but do not comprehend the past; they
represent building without overall purpose, around an uncharted and only partially surveyed territory. Without a center. Atonal. [My emphases in
italics.]
Said relies, interestingly enough, on a language of “de-centering” typical of deconstruction, but he endows it with earthly and worldly
valences, especially with that reference to uncharted, partially surveyed territory. It is territory that underwrites so many of his declensions and
translations. Al-dakhil, meaning “from the interior” and a term used to refer to Israel’s expansion over communities and places, once the
name Palestine is translated by Said as “people still Palestinian despite the interdictions of the Israeli presence.” After the ’67 war, he
maintains, al-dakhil has been associated with inhabitants of the occupied territories: “those from inside,” those enjoying the privilege of
“obduracy.” Gradually, especially when Palestinians meet in other parts of the world, the semantic emphasis shifts slightly to designate the
strange kind of privacy that comes from living on the outside as if it were the inside; a public incognito based on using “‘their’ codes … to
mean something quite different.” The problem with this ambiguous zone of language is that meaning becomes perforce “evasive” and
“idiosyncratic.” In answer to the dilemma of living alongside outsiders while trying to define what you lay claim to on the inside, Said
describes a mode of speaking in enigmas:
We are a people of messages and signals, of allusions and indirect expression. We seek each other out, but because our interior is always to
some extent occupied and interrupted by others—Israelis and Arabs—we have developed a technique of speaking through the given, expressing
things obliquely and, to my mind, so mysteriously as to puzzle even ourselves.
Translation, as Said performs it, is always about a kind of territorial loss, be it cultural or geographic property. It deeply pains Said that the
Arabic word tabooleh is appropriated in Hebrew as a word meaning “kibbutz salad.” When the Lebanese refugee camp Ein el-Hilwé,
(meaning “sweet spring”) is pronounced in Hebrew Ein el-Khilwé, (meaning “a spring in an empty place”) language is clearly the
battleground of occupation. And the heavily freighted word “diaspora” is treated as an Untranslatable, a term measuring the subtraction of a
tragic Jewish heritage from contemporary usage:
I do not like to call it a Palestinian diaspora: there is only an apparent symmetry between our exile and theirs. Besides the Diaspora no longer
exists spiritually and culturally as it once did in Central Europe, with tragic figures like Kafka, Schoenberg, and Benjamin at its core. Today’s
Diaspora is represented centrally by American Zionism …13
After the Last Sky reveals just how irdisch Said was as a practical critic and reader of site-specificity, not only of language use in a particular
location but also of Palestine as a land riven by partition, erasure, and architecture. Alive to the crucial role of architecture in territorial
politics, Said’s caption to several images shot by Jean Mohr of a housing complex designed by the Polish-born Israeli architect Zvi Hecker in
1979 conveys with irony his vision of territorial occupants no longer there and new inhabitants not yet arrived: Settlements of Ramot near
Jerusalem, 1979. As the buildings neared completion, tenants were in short supply. Interestingly, the very designation of this place
emerges as a site of translation politics: Hecker calls it a “housing complex” and Said calls it a “settlement.” Said’s commentary was
pointed, associating “settlement” with Israel’s self-accorded license to offend Palestine:
The attitude expressed in the construction of settlements on the West Bank is unmistakable. Visually there is a rude interventionary power in
them that, I am told, shocks even Israelis. One thinks not only of a coarse army of heedless and rough crusaders, but also—given some of the
structures themselves—of a marching cancer. As for the effect on the landscape and Palestine’s ecology, the offense is deep and lasting.14
I too found myself profoundly disturbed by these images of Hecker’s polyhedric pods, with their menacing military aspect, their fortress-wall
massing, and windows resembling surveillant periscopes or stages for sharpshooters. But I could also see that the complex could read quite
differently if it were abstracted from context. Hecker was a rather interesting figure in the history of modernism, often hailed (by Daniel
Libeskind among others) as an architectural deconstructivist avant la lettre. He has been cast as an heir to the Viennese expressionists
Bruno Taut and Paul Scheerbart (who emphasized the crystalline perfection of natural geometries) and as a comparable figure to Moshe
Safdie, whose Habitat megastructure was featured at the 1967 Montreal Expo and like Hecker’s Ramot, was of a piece with work by Dutch
urbanists such as Aldo van Eyck (who coined the term “built homecoming” to refer to his vision of the reciprocity between cognition and
environment, social patterning and built form, domestic space and city) and Piet Bloom, whose much-loved Cubic Houses in Rotterdam, built
in the mid-1980s, serve as the city’s mascot on numerous websites.15 Conceived as living modules against the reigning orthodoxy of slab
block housing, and drawing on the ideal of cities like villages, the Cubic Houses were situated on top of a pedestrian bridge. The concept
behind the project was to create a forest, with each cube a tree. The windows opened onto the environment below, embracing the site.
Like these Dutch proponents of configurative design, Hecker belonged to a postwar movement that looked to stones, crystals and
honeycombs to furnish a formal system for housing. Nature provided a “systems” humanism that was intended to mitigate the relentless
straight lines of international modernism. For Hecker, “dodecahedra in space-packing arrangements” sent a message of collective
“individuality” and humanized material surfaces. In a subsequent elaboration, Hecker wrote:
Toward the end of 1972, while engaged on the Ramot Housing Complex near Jerusalem, I decided to undertake a systematic investigation of the
properties and potentialities of the dodecahedral form in architectural design. It was the last of the five Platonic solids (tetrahedron, cube,
octahedron, dodecahedron and icosahedron) that I had considered … it is a polyhedron whose occurrence in nature is rare … the length of a
diagonal of its pentagonal face corresponds to the Golden Section … used by Greek and Gothic architects, and applied by Le Corbusier… It was
my rather casual observation that a group of dodecahedra resembled a rough stone wall that captured my imagination … the catalyst in the
design of the Ramot Housing Complex with its landscape characterized by small hills, piles of rocks and human-made terraces.
In discussing Ramot, Hecker seems impervious to the impact of settlements on the Palestinian villages. Though he mentions,o ffhandedly,
that he made his preliminary sketches of polyhedral form “in the first days of the Arab–Israel war of 1973, when I was in the Israeli army, far
from my office,” there is no acknowledgment that his architecture was basically part of a strategic plan to use settlers as battlements, or that it
did violence to its surround. This violence is underscored by the photographic sequence in After the Last Sky. Mohr’s camera moves beyond
Hecker’s housing units, zooming in on the Arab village that survives in its shadow. The caption reads: “The foreground drops away, leaving
only the Arab village, its mosque and houses surrounded by fruit trees, olive trees, and stone walls.” The message is clear: if you want to find
an urban form that grows naturally from the territory, if you are in search of a formal ecology expressive of humanism, then look here. In fact,
the village seems to embody perfectly the humanist theoretical principles elaborated in Henri Focillon’s “life of forms,” advanced in his 1934
book that includes the phrase in its title:
We must never think of forms as simply suspended in some remote, abstract zone, above the earth and above man. They mingle with life, whence
they come; they translate into space certain movements of the mind … Whether stable or nomadic, all formal environments give birth to their own
various types of social structure: styles of life, vocabularies, states of awareness. Expressed in a more general way, the life of forms gives
definition to what may be termed “psychological landscapes,” without which the essential genius of the environments would be opaque and
elusive for all those who share in them.16
Two psychological landscapes—one militarized, the other naturalized—enter into historic face-off on the outskirts of Jerusalem in 1979. Two

conflicting visions of humanism battle it out on land, assigning terrestrial concreteness to the secular concept of irdischen Welt, cherished by
Auerbach and Said alike. In his own formulations of humanism, Said grappled with the well-worn quandary: how and why Jewish humanism
—updated at midcentury to include credos of progressive avant-gardism, political community, and formalist ecology—moved into
compromise formation with national territorialism, thus blinding itself to other humanisms and misrecognizing as a settlement a land gone
missing.
In posing this question, the aim is not to defer to the obvious answers: namely that humanism’s universalism is particular, relative to
territorial emplacement and local politics, or that one person’s humanism is another person’s oppression, depending on who possesses the
power of franchise and property exploitation. Nor is it sufficient simply to point out the irony of the fact that both the Israeli settlement and the
Palestinian village default to competing humanisms, each based on an ideal vision of collective habitat and participant sociality. For Said,
such evenhandedness would neuter the politics of humanism. There would no longer be a reason to contrive a terrestrial humanism that
tracks the history of forced relocation, exile, and occupation if humanism as such were applied willy-nilly as the great equalizer in places of
contested ground.
What Said seems to have been after was a vision of linguistic and cultural translation in the service of political justice; a vision of “justice
as translation” to borrow the title of a book by the legal scholar James Boyd White. This would entail a genuine reckoning with Israel and
Palestine’s chiastic relation, based on the shared fate of a missing homeland and a common exilic ancestor, “Moses, the Egyptian.” As Said
argued in Freud and the Non-European, Freud’s effort to “protectively huddle the Jews inside the sheltering realm of the European,” casting
them as “remnants of Mediterranean civilization,” is “janglingly discordant with his show of force about Moses’s Egyptian origins.”17 Said
uses his reading of Freud to introduce the shifting category of the “non-European” as a unit that complicates the politics of culture, and with it,
the practice of psychoanalytically inflected terrestrial humanism. Who is and who is not considered European is revealed to be
phantasmatically constructed as well as supremely contingent on the fragile historical circumstances of national belonging and withdrawn
citizenship. Underscoring the consequences of this contingency—in the interest, perhaps, of the unconditional right of return or internationally
sanctioned laws of hospitality—Said presses specific homologies: the de-Europeanization of Jews under the Nuremburg Laws of Nazi
Germany, the re-Europeanization of Jews as defenders of the West in the Middle East after the 1948 creation of the Jewish state in
Palestine, and the de-Mediterraneanization of Arabs after 1948.
In addition to foregrounding the political stakes that accompany the classification of peoples within heritage traditions (a major concern in
Orientalism), Said places the study of legal statutes governing citizenship and land entitlement within humanism’s purview. Equally important,
he urges the critic to remake humanism in the guise of ethical militance; thereby disrobing the congeniality of a liberal tradition that loves the
world but ignores the earthly violence of distributive injustice. Finally, Said’s emphasis on the word “world” in its widest ascription prompts
renewed philosophical investigations of what is irdisch, in the manner of late Kant, who advanced some wacky yet intriguing theories about
the earth as a self-sanctioning nomos. Peter Fenves reminds us (drawing on Kant’s Toward Eternal Peace as well as a number of
posthumously published writings invoking the “Earth-globe now”) that
the original meaning of the Greek word nomos is ‘division,’ especially the division and distribution of the land for the purpose of its occupation and
cultivation … Insofar as the nomos of the earth is more original than any particular legal order, there is no position from which it can be judged
right or wrong: it is a matter of fact, not of right … Toward Eternal Peace addresses the lawless character of this nomos of the earth.
Fenves argues that such a nomos, at least in Kantian terms, engenders not a world without laws but a motion toward concession; a place-
making for “another kind of human being” that would transition the planet “from the metaphysics of nature to physics.” Though this sounds
abstract, cosmic, even science-fictional, Kant’s wild speculations about “another law of the earth” in his late writings exhibit the traits of “late
style” that Said mined for future humanisms. Quirky thought experiments, strange geological and astrophysical projections, unpleasing
stylistic admixtures of archaism and decadence, recourse to anachronism and other mechanisms of revolutionary untiming—these were
among the riches discovered in “late style.” Celebrating Theodor Adorno’s “daring thesis [in an essay called “Alienated Masterpiece” in his
Essays on Music] that like Kant, Beethoven was posing the question of ultimate ontology in terms of subjectivity,” Said was particularly
struck by Adorno’s way of reading exclusion and renunciation into Beethoven’s Missa solemnis, for it enabled him to argue that what
Beethoven was also renouncing was “the aim of reconciling [what Adorno termed] ‘the universally human’ with a concrete way of being
human.”18 Said’s openness to “concrete ways of being human” that are not foretold in the conventional humanist program of universal values
strikes me as comparable in an interesting manner to the Kantian notion of a humankind to come. This irdisch human may be somewhat
otherworldly, but it is a kind of otherworldliness that discloses states of freedom, or heresy, or sublime justice (what Dante and Mahmoud
Darwish each called Paradise).
Having conceived Said’s terrestrial humanism in terms of multiple heuristics—a negative philology, a translational cartography, a
contrapuntal reading of world literature, a revelation of the weaponization of humanism in situations of territorial incursion—I will conclude by
emphasizing that it above all represents irdisch in the full if mistranslated sense of Weltlich Welt, “worldly world,” or earthly paradise. It is
tempting to draw a line of criticism that leads from late Kant, to Kostas Axelos (author of the 1964 Vers la pensée planétaire), and up to
Gayatri Chakravorty Spivak, whose anti-globalization theory of planetarity in Death of a Discipline has held sway in reconfiguring
comparative literature through a renewed area studies. Another line goes from Jacques Derrida (whose notion of chôra implies a
“cosmetics” of the world that considers the globe as both an earthly raiment and holding receptacle) to Jean-Luc Nancy, who poses the
question in Being Singular Plural:
Can we think an earth and a human such that they would be only what they are—nothing but earth and human—and such that they would be
none of the various horizons often harbored under these names, none of the “perspectives” or “views” in view of which we have disfigured
humans [les hommes] and driven them to despair?
Nancy designates “Sarajevo” as the “martyr-name” of “a world that does not even manage to constitute a world … a world lacking in world,
and lacking in the meaning of world.”19 Said designates “Palestine” in much the same way, but where Nancy refers to the signification of
“Sarajevo” in terms of what he describes as the world’s autism, Said coaxes “Palestine” into the loquacious place of humanism’s afterlife.
