III
Derrida’s Theologies of Translation
If the mind could, in a single act of knowledge, really grasp (begriefen) absolute totality as a system completed in all of its parts, it would
overcome its finitude. It would not need to translate.
—Jacques Derrida, “Theology of Translation”
So I’ve chosen, in attempting this quasi-concluding reflection as a kind of premise for the discussion that will follow, to mark, remark, and have
you remark with me the fact that at bottom everything we have spoken about came down to problems of translation.
—Jacques Derrida, Thirteenth and final session, March 27,
2002, Seminar on “The Beast and the Sovereign”
In April 1979, acting in his capacity as chair of comparative literature, Paul de Man corresponded with Jacques Derrida on the subject of
renewing his invitation as visiting professor at Yale. He mentions a translation seminar that Derrida will teach in 1979–80, and in a follow-up
missive, refers to the course as “Translation and the Question of Comparative Literature.” In his gracious acceptance of the offer, Derrida
refers in French to the same seminar as “The Concept of Comparative Literature and the Theoretical Problems of Translation.1” The
discrepancies are, of course, intriguing. Where de Man foregrounds “translation” as the first order of the seminar’s business, Derrida
foregrounds “comparative literature.” Where Derrida treats comparative literature as “concept,” de Man treats it as “question.” Where
Derrida frames “translation” as a set of “theoretical problems,” de Man lets translation stand without any allusion to theory. And where de Man
implies that translation as a praxis puts comparative literature into question, Derrida renders the very name comparative literature a
conceptual Untranslatable; the name of a strangely exotic discipline from the perspective of a philosopher.
Derrida’s transcript for the seminar (written in French and housed in UC Irvine’s Special Collections and Archives) reveals a marked self-
consciousness about his outsider status to the field of comparative literature.2 It shows too how consequential the course was for his future
work; in fact, it may be no overstatement to affirm that it was the crucible for his long-term meditation on disciplinary “rights” (the “right to
literature,” the “right to philosophy”), and, at the very end of his life (in the seminar on “The Beast and the Sovereign”), for his critique of how
the animal has been separated from the human.3 Disciplinary interrogation was in the air at Yale in the ’70s and ’80s and Derrida was fully
participant in the symptom. Already, in 1974 he had joined a group of educators in France that came to be known as GREPH (Groupe de
Recherches sur l’Enseignement Philosophique) that sought to rethink philosophical pedagogy. Imbued with post-’68 populism and interested
in examining the “what” of philosophy by displacing the “where” of philosophy (dislocating its transmission to places outside the university
walls), the group produced a document in 1975 that would critically investigate “models of didactic operations legible, with their rhetoric,
logic, psychagogy, etc., within written discourses”; “pedagogical practices administered according to rules in fixed places, in public or
private establishments since the Sophists”; “the teaching system’s legislative power and juridical and traditional code”; and “the stakes of
the struggles within and around philosophical education, today, in France.” In “Philosophy and its Classes,” an article published in Le Monde
in 1975, Derrida offered a passionate defense of the teaching of philosophy. His polemic was forged in opposition to the ultimately defeated
“Haby Reform,” which called for scaling back philosophical instruction in French schools and universities. Emphasizing the need for
“training,” “new techniques,” and the fashioning of “experiments,” Derrida proselytized for “the extension and transformation of so-called
philosophical teaching.”4
Derrida brought these political talking points to bear on the academically rarefied internal discussions at Yale over how to revamp the
literature departments to accommodate theory, or, more to the point, how to defend theory against its institutional adversaries.5 He would
give substance to this agenda in his seminars at Yale and elsewhere by developing a deconstructive technics that presaged comp lit’s future
as translatability studies.
Derrida’s 1979–80 seminar, the Concept of Comparative Literature and the Theoretical Problems of Translation, was of a piece with
others that he was offering around the same time: the Right to Philosophy (du droit à la philosophie), the Right to Literature (du droit à la
littérature), and the Reason of the University (la Raison universitaire), given in the years 1978–79, 1970–80, and 1982–83, respectively.
Each of these courses activated the pedagogical aporia. Evidence of this is found in “Vacant Chair,” an essay published in Eyes of the
University that wittily glossed Kant’s famous dictum “One does not learn philosophy, one can only learn to philosophize” by imagining models
of “teaching without learning,” and a “university itself that does not take place.” Recalling his course on the right to literature in an interview in
Acts of Literature, Derrida returned to the pedagogical aporia; imagining an ideal reader
who does not yet exist, whose competence cannot be identified, a reader who would be “formed,” “trained,” “instructed,” “constructed,” even
engendered, let’s say invented by the work … The work then becomes an institution forming its own readers, giving them a competence they did
not possess before: a university, a seminar, a colloquium, a curriculum, a course.
In the play on the vocabulary of universities—chairs, faculties, training, instruction—Derrida parodies the ceremonial forms of university life
but preserves their instructive force. Designating “the works” themselves as teachers (rather than the corporatist body of the professoriat),
Derrida opens a channel to thought that instantiates creative pedagogies (in a way that anticipates Jacques Rancière’s exploration, in his
1987 study Le maître ignorant: Cinq leçons sur l’émancipation intellectuelle, of Joseph Jacotot’s revolutionary models of emancipatory
learning).6 All this is important background to the comparative literature seminar, which sutures translation theory and practice to institutional
critique.
Derrida deals with the “concept” of comparative literature by deferring to the “theory” of translation. From whence did this interest in
translation derive? In part, of course, from his passionate engagement with the work of Walter Benjamin, whose French reception in the ’60s
and ’70s was revitalized by Derrida’s close friends and interlocutors Maurice de Gandillac (Benjamin’s major French translator), Peter
Szondi, Philippe Lacoue-Labarthe, Jean-Luc Nancy and Antoine Berman (who was working at the time onL ’Épreuve de l’étranger, a
watershed book of translation theory published in 1984).7 We should also recall that, during an earlier teaching stint at Yale (1976), Derrida

offered a class on his essay “The Task of the Translator” titled “Des tours de Babel,” which forged the basis of “Living On Border Lines” (in
Deconstruction and Criticism) and Des Tours de Babel (in Difference in Translation). As Dragan Kujundži notes in his introduction to the
publication of an English translation of the comparative literature seminar in Discourse, Derrida used Benjamin to establish
a contesting analysis of the concept of comparative literature, which at the time did not find a way into the published essay dedicated to Benjamin.
Similar to the name of God, Derrida argues in the seminar, works of literature are unique, singular, and operate something like proper names.8
Roughing in the full picture of what Derrida was teaching in the ’70s and ’80s, then, we begin to appreciate how distinct topologies
converged in this particular seminar. Theology, law, institutions, disciplinary logic, textual survival, techniques of reading, translation—each of
these concerns was in some sense a facet of the same problem: how to make philosophy and literature teachable as technē, or living
practice.
The seminar on comparative literature, as the occasion of Derrida’s anomalous entrance into a literature department, prompts a
disavowed questioning of the institutional legitimacy of his host institution. Derrida piles up a list of legalistic challenges that he—as invited
guest—feels unentitled to pose (including what the institution bases its claims to existence, property, occupancy, funding on). But, of course,
he has posed these and even more serious challenges under cover of preterition. It is through this backhanded case against the university
institution—whose authority rests on what he calls the “mechanical reproduction of its legality”—that Derrida argues for a “concept” of
comparative literature that does not just exist by automatic entitlement:
This is a department of comparative literature; I just arrived [j’y arrive]. Like many institutions, comparative literature did not wait for me, it did not
wait for us, in order to exist. Nor did departments of literature in the West and elsewhere. To exist, for an institution, is to affirm its right to
existence; it is to constantly refer, more or less virtually, to a legitimacy, but to a certain type of particular legitimacy, a historical legitimacy, an
entitlement that has its origin in a historical act or in historical acts of foundation. When the day comes that this act of foundation—that founds the
law upon nonlaw, upon an ajuridical situation—the day this act of foundation is contested by another claim to legitimacy, or simply the day when
no one feels the necessity or the possibility of referring to the foundation of the law, when no one draws authority from it any longer, then the
institution dies. It can outlive [survivre à] its own death, continue to translate itself in rituals, objective behaviors, reproductive procedures, and
give all the exterior signs of vitality, all the apparent guarantees of its smooth functioning, of its continuity, of its legitimacy. It can continue to
pretend to have a determined, rigorously identifiable object and to relate to it in a living, renewed, effective, productive way. Even if it no longer
has an object around which a living consensus can be established and can bring together a community of researchers, teachers, and students, a
department in a university can long outlive the disappearance of its object and of the living consensus relating to it. It is true that, in these cases,
the survival [survie], the time and the economy of survival (for example, the budget, the demographics of the institution, its region of influence,
etc.) are always ruled by its inscription in a larger sociopolitical space, of which we must never lose sight.9
When Derrida writes “comparative literature did not wait for me, it did not wait for us, in order to exist,” it is hard not to hear echoes of Emily
Dickinson’s famous line “Because I could not stop for Death / He kindly stopped for me.” Comp lit stops for Derrida, prompting him to re-
legislate comp lit’s institutional right such that it depends no longer on ajuridical statutes or a state of exception10 but instead rests on acts of
translational sur-vie that are equal parts Shelley and Benjamin in inspiration.11
Intent on disabling the reductive opposition between pedagogical life and institutional death, Derrida appeals to four mutually interfering
“nouns” whose formal arraignment recalls Lacan’s “four fundamental concepts”:
Now if I had to choose three or four nouns to designate what resists this opposition, what is instituted precisely to defeat this opposition, to
disqualify it, to make it lose its entitlements, its rights, and its conditions of existence, if I had to choose three or four nouns to designate what
contravenes and defies this law, I would say (1) Institution, (2) Literature, (3) Translation, and consequently, among other consequences, (4)
Department of Comparative Literature. What does this mean? [Emphasis in original]12
How, one wonders, do such “nouns” “defy laws” and say what they mean by it? The answer seems to lie in their collective power to overthrow
the “rule” of logic’s logic over “translations, transformations, figures and tropes” in a bid for a new order of translational justice.13
What we are calling “the right to translation” proves to be indistinguishable from the occasion of the seminar itself—a course at Yale in
comparative literature on comparative literature and translation theory. Though fully in line with the institution’s local debates over the
teaching of poetics and literary theory, Derrida’s course takes these pedagogical investigations in a new direction, exercising the right to
translation by “translating” the name “comparative literature” itself. Thus, he moves from the Italian letterature comparate, to the German
vergleichende Literatur, to the French and English correlatives:
I am a foreign “visiting professor,” speaking in his own language, but within the enclosure of an American university department dominated
linguistically by English and whose title is not “compared literature;” [littérature comparée], but, and I translate, “comparative literature” [littérature
comparative].14
By purposefully mistranslating the disciplinary proper name (“Department of Compared Literature”/Département de littérature comparative),
Derrida not only underscores the extent to which “comparative literature” qualifies as a prototypical Untranslatable (an incorruptible or
intransigent nub of meaning that triggers endless translating in response to its resistant singularity), he also incites the student to acts of
institution. One imagines “Departments of Compared Literature” or “Littérature Comparative” coming into existence dedicated to
translational différance that is to say, to the practice of attending closely to discrepant valences of “compared,” “comparativity,”
vergleichende and so on.
What proves tricky in this matter of disciplinary rights, however, is the negotiation between comparative literature and literature. It would
be altogether reasonable to assume that comp lit and lit would share the same rights, or at the very least a disciplinary common ground. But
here, literature acts as comp lit’s disestablishmentarian:
We addressed these questions—in particular the question of the formation of the narrow concept of “literature” in the eighteenth century—last
year, under the title of droit à la littérature. Is literature in the narrow sense, if there is such a thing, compatible with the encyclopedic project of
general and comparative literature? Because comparative literature, if these words have the meaning that the consensus has always attributed to
them, compares literature with itself, some literature [de la littérature] with itself, literatures, or literary phenomena amongst themselves. Whether
we speak of comparative literature as a work or as a discipline, it always claims to be at once on the side of the comparing and of the compared.
Comparative, compared, or comparing literature should, in the final analysis, only have to do with literature or with literatures as its specific object
or subject. And one can identify literatures or literary phenomena, literary products or productions, only if one knows, if one at least has a
precomprehension, of what “literature” or the “literariness” of literature means. Is this a “critical” question and in what way? Does it inaugurate
[ouvre] a crisis? Does it put the concept and the institution of “comparative literature” in crisis? And what relation does it have with the problem of
translation?
Comparative literature ostensibly derives its epistemological legitimacy from the singular right of literature to itself, but once this singularity is
made multiple (such that comp lit, in Derrida’s words, “compares itself with itself as other”), comp lit risks losing its disciplinary singularity in
the welter of compared comparisons or a generalized genericity.15
Derrida’s stated aim is to make intelligible the critical fold structuring comp lit’s claim to “the concept of general literature, that organizes
and disorganizes this entire discourse” (italics in the original). To this end, he assails the eminent French critic René Étiemble, who,

throughout the seminar is treated as the representative of an Enlightenment vision of comp lit, molded, like philosophy, by the ambition to
embody Spirit or absolute knowledge (“totalizing or finalizing the history of meaning or of culture”). Derrida pillories Étiemble’s entry on
comparative literature in the Encyclopedia Universalis for offering a UNESCO-like template of enlightened,“ techno-political” comparatism
that he defenestrates as literary data management; a fantasy, as he sees it, of “the entire world becoming an immense department of Comp
Lit”:
Universitas as rational realization of the universal state and decline of the state is a political problematic at the center (not at the margins) of this
great comparative literature dream. Although it is commensurate with a certain modern technico-politics (UNESCO, telecommunications, gigantic
memories of computerized libraries, a certain state of inter-state information transfer, etc.), the spirit of the philosophico-encyclopedic project is
obviously in synchrony with the great speculative systems of the nineteenth century on the Hegelian model (but of which the Hegelian example is
itself only a particularly powerful and spectacular phenomenon). I have not chosen this manifest by Étiemble, this encyclopedic text published in
an Encyclopaedia Universalis, in order to abuse or deride it. I believe that it expresses directly and without detours the broad outline of the spirit
that governs, that necessarily governs the original [principielle] foundation, the very constitution of every department of “complit.” (To be
complete or not to be.)
“To be complit or not to be”—a Beckettian Shakespeareanism of the first order that gives us the prospect of a “department of complete” or
“comp lit” pronounced with a French accent—is indeed the question. It can be elaborated as whether comp lit should be given over to
omnivorous encyclopedism and the comprador pursuit of allness (which, as the much later 1993 Bernheimer Report noted, could lead to the
abandonment of the purely literary object of study and the ceding of comp lit to cultural studies or to expansive discursive fields that juxtapose
literature non-hierarchically to extra-literary forms and mediums) or whether it should focus on the “in-complete”; that is to say, on the
deconstruction of the general. Derrida, of course, gravitates to the latter option, and in doing so he may well have been responding to the de
Manian problematic of literary Vorhabe which, as Ortwin de Graef has noted, preoccupied de Man in the late ’60s. Vorhabe encompasses
the idea of textual pregivenness, or the text as “a self-enclosed system of pure signs, unadulterated by the everyday exteriority of
reference.”16 Derrida’s version of Vorhabe is expressed as the “precomprehension of the literary,” an ambiguous phrase suggesting that
literature’s literarity and technicity always already precomprehend the text:17
In order to compare literatures or literary phenomena, I must first know, at least by way of precomprehension, what the literary is, lacking which I
risk comparing anything with anything in the name of comparative literature. I would compare for example a painting and a real plant, a cookbook
and a constitutional text, a novel and a bank check, a speech by Carter and the Iliad. Farfetched as these examples appear to you, you are well
aware that for want of a rigorous a priori, of an essence rigorously protected by its apriority, one can always argue that such comparisons belong
to the field of comparative literature. Since the existence of a pure literariness is and remains today more problematic than ever, the study of
general literature concerns an object that is more problematic than ever and thus comparative literature can also become a wandering discipline,
as delirious as it is bulimic in an unbridled encyclopedism: nothing that has to do with language is foreign to me, nothing that has to do with the
work of art is foreign to me, it says; not only do all languages interest me but so do all artifices, all artistic or artisanal practices; they all have to
do with language and thus with literature, etc.18
A second-order literarity of technics, comp lit is unique among disciplines in that its proper name assigns “comparative” a predicative rather
than an adjectival status. The name comparative literature stands as a double predicate, technics multiplied by two or Vorhabe squared. To
emphasize this distinctive feature, Derrida contrasts comparative literature with disciplines that cannot have “comparative” predicatively
affixed: so for example, there is no department of comparative mathematics, department of comparative pure physics, or department of
comparative general history. While it is possible to deploy “comparative” adjectivally in relation to fields (comparative politics, comparative
religion, etc.), the disciplines they qualify are the predicates. Comp lit, then, is a “hapax” or singular disciplinary occurrence whose effect is
captured as a tautology: “What do you expect,” Derrida comments, “comparative literature, is, well, comparative literature.” Earlier in the
seminar he uses a similar formula: “One must compare literature with literature,” a phrase milked for all its strangeness. Even if we want to
understand “comparative” as the translatio of technics, Derrida does not teach the “literature on literature” relation that simply: “Do not
forget,” he admonishes, “that this idea is at once very trivial and very strange, and the conditions of its appearance are far from being clear
today.”19
Taking stock of where Derrida was situated in literary history when he referred to comparative literature “today,” it is particularly
interesting that he fixed on translation in the 1979 seminar.20 Only a few years prior, in the 1975 American Comparative Literature
Association’s report on the state of the discipline, Derrida’s Yale colleague Thomas Greene had communicated the committee’s
disapproval of the increase in courses including literature in translation:
Courses in translation are potentially of great value to the student, but if no one in the classroom, including the instructor, is in touch with the
original language, then something precious has been lost to the learning experience, and something also of our Comparatist integrity. An
examination of several college catalogues suggests that, at more than one institution, the titles of Comparative Literature course offerings do not
exclude the dillentantish, the modish, even the frivolous.
Ever the one to brook, or even exult in, accusations of dilletantishness, modishness and frivolity, Derrida instinctively gravitates to the
translation problem as a flashpoint of disciplinary ire. He is of course by no means advocating monolingual pedagogy (his hermeneutic
procedure, as we know, famously devolved around appositions of Untranslatables in their original languages), but one senses throughout the
seminar an aversion to legislating where and how translation should take place. And, while hardly an apologist for World Lit, he endorses
René Wellek’s resurrection of Goethean Weltliteratur for the postwar era. He is also especially interested in tracking the vagaries of the
Babelian comparatist who wanders across state borders infringing trespass law, seeks access to the literary commons, experiences like the
emigrant the portage of language from place to place (übersetzen as übertragen), and relinquishes lasting title to authorial property to the
translator (the passage from Eigentumsüberträgung [the transfer of title] to Uberleben [translational afterlife, sur-vie]). The importance for
both Derrida and de Man of the philology of the German term for translation, Ubersetzung, cannot be emphasized enough. Translation was
their “home,” insofar as both were exiles operating continually in “foreign” languages and intellectual traditions. But “translation” was also a
metaphor for the disarticulation of “the original”; its refugee status as language in a state of perpetual transit, retreat, and referential
infinitude. As de Man observed:
Ubersetzen translates exactly the Greek metaphorein, to move over, übersetzen, to put across. Ubersetzen, I should say, translates metaphor—
which, asserts Benjamin, is not at all the same. They are not metaphors, yet the word means metaphor. The metaphor is not a metaphor.21
Ubersetzung in its very etymology denotes “to move over” or “to put across” with all of its associations: the incorporation of psychic
damage, the pain and suffering of language as such, the deportation of humans, and of literature. It was surely not incidental that de Man
would devote a substantial portion of his 1979 undergraduate comp lit seminar on Benjamin’s “The Task of the Translator” to the juridico-
ontological implications of the Ubertragung/Ubersetzung pairing, for these cognates performed as signifiers of linguistic evacuation,
homelessness, and abrogated citizenship.22 For his part, Derrida would explore the semantic panoply around translation and statelessness
in Le Monolinguisme de l’autre, ou, Laprothèse d’origine, whose original title, when it was delivered in 1992 in lecture form was Echoes
from Elsewhere/Renvoz’s d’ailleurs.

Though Derrida presciently articulated disciplinary anxieties that are commonly heard in the first half of the twenty-first century over
whether “it is possible to know what should constitute a specific training, a verifiable competence in comparative literature,” his spirited
practice of translation as pedagogical technique works through the sense of imminent crisis.23 The comp lit seminar records Derrida
teaching “live” and as we follow the paces of his translation of comp lit’s unaccessed Vorhabe in real time, we experience a surprisingly
confident disciplinary deconstruction. Comp lit is disabled as a proper name and imperialistic arbiter (that, lacking a singular object of study,
exerts its power by trying to be all things to all languages), but it is also instituted as an epistemologically self-reflexive logic of disciplinary
generality that will lead, in due course, to what he will subsequently call “the duty to translate.” Derrida developed this idea in a paper on the
theology of translation (published in English in Eyes of the University).
“Theology of Translation” originally formed part of a cluster of talks on language and translation delivered at Toronto in 1983–84, including
“If There is Cause to Translate I: Philosophy in itsN ational Language” (Toward a ‘licterature en françois’); “If There is a Cause to Translate II:
Descartes’ Romances or The Economy of Words”; “Vacant Chair: Censorship, Mastery, Magisteriality”; and “Mochlos, or The Conflict of the
Faculties.” The first paper takes up Descartes’ defense of his decision to write in French on the grounds that the vulgate offers a better
conduit to natural reason than Latin. The consequence, Derrida notes, is that for the first time French becomes a universal language of
philosophical method buttressing French law and the monarchist state. The most astonishing twist comes when Derrida refers to “suicide by
translation” to talk about cases like “I am speaking in French” that are enunciated in English. Here, the original, in his words, “lets itself be
destroyed almost without remainder, without apparent remainder inside the corpus.” Death by translation is of course coextensive with
translational living-on, familiar from Derrida’s Shelley and Benjamin readings. Equally significant is Derrida’s use of Descartes and later
Schelling to undo the Kantian divisionism of the university faculties, which, severed philosophy from action and poetics. In “Theology of
Translation,” Derrida marshals Schelling’s Lectures on the Method of University Studies against Kant’s Conflict of the Faculties in the
service of reuniting philosophical and literary pedagogies. “Poetry,” writes Derrida, “is at the heart of philosophy: the poem is a
philosopheme.” Schelling also provides him with the terms for asking where, how, and if philosophy and poetry can be learned. The task of
teaching how “to philosophize” is sharply distinguished from the task of teaching philosophy as an academic subject. The goal for Schelling
and fellow Romantics is to prove that philosophical intuition cannot be learned but rather is cultivated as bildung through the translation of
the ideal into the real; nothing short of a “theology of translation” in Derrida’s view:
The point is precisely that for Schelling, whose onto-theology I wished to present, language is a living phenomenon; life or living spirit speaks in
language; and in the same way nature is an author, the author of a book that must be translated with the skill of a philologist.
“Without man,” he concludes,
God’s very revelation would not be accomplished. By his very activity, man is to develop (entwickeln) that which is lacking in God’s total revelation
… That is what is called translation; it is also what is called the destination of the university.
David L. Clark finds in Derrida’s theologized “translatology” a form of romanticism in contemporary guise; that is to say, a categorical
imperative—the “duty to translate”—characterized, on the one hand, by acts of community that allow subjects to “tremble in their delirious
relationship to language,” and, on the other hand, by operations that are “machine-like” because they introduce “a form of prostheticizing
technicity into the heart of mortal life, the radically inhuman alterity of which language is, as de Man would say, an effacing ‘figure.’” 24
Derrida’s “Mochlos, or The Conflict of the Faculties,” shifts the discussion from the “duty to translate” and the “theology of translation” to
“untranslatability” as something endemic to the university institution that causes us to renegotiate the boundaries between self and institution,
life and death, language and world. Originally delivered as a lecture in English in 1980 to mark the centennial founding of Columbia
University’s graduate school,25 it continues the reading of Conflict of the Faculties but this time attends to “its points of untranslatability”;
which is to say, “anything that no longer reaches us and remains outside the usage of our era.” “Untranslatable nodes,” he argues, reveal
“certain contradictions, laws of conflicts, or antinomies of university reason” but also “what exceeds this dialectical rationality itself; and the
un-translatability we experience will perhaps signal the university’s inability to comprehend itself in the purity of its inside, to translate and
transmit its proper meaning.” In a kind of Hosannah moment, he calls out to the text to “translate me”: to interpret me, evaluate me, inherit
from me, make me live on.”26 This giving over of the subject to translation, I take to be a putting oneself into position, a resetting of the set-up,
the better “to negotiate” (the verb is Derrida’s) a new social contract with the university.27
To translate, then, is to enter into labor negotiations on different grounds, to gain leverage through philosophy. This gets us back to one of
the literal meanings of mochlos as a lever mechanism that operates in accordance with Archimedes’s “Law of the Lever.” In Book 1 of On
the Equilibrium of Planes, Archimedes, following the Aristotelian formula that says “the ratio of the weight moved to the weight moving it is
the inverse ratio of the distances from the center,” proposed that “magnitudes are in equilibrium at distances reciprocally proportional to their
weights.” “The Law of the Lever” was based on a pulley system that Archimedes designed to enable sailors to leverage objects that would
otherwise be too heavy to move. He became famous for the boastful quip “Give me a place to stand on, and I will move the earth.”
In addition to its applications in physics, mathematics, architecture and design, hypomochlia had medical and orthopoedic uses,
particularly relevant to the treatment of misaligned bones, joints and rotator cuffs. I recall the medical ascription because it has play in the
section “The Philosophy Faculty versus the Faculty of Medicine” in Kant’sC ritique of the Faculties, subtitled “On the Power of the Mind to
Master its Morbid Feelings by Sheer Resolution.” Here, Kant details his ailments: insomnia, hyopchondria, epileptic aches and “brain-
cramp.” As Peter Fenves notes, the inventory of his illnesses was no mere sidebar; it serves as a reminder of the fact that the organic body
“neither precisely alive nor precisely dead is always, from beginning to end, in ‘constant transition’ from life to death.”28 The emphasis, then,
on incremental transition is theoretically consequential, for it contests the idea of death as a radical break from time into eternity. It poses,
instead, the notion of transitioning organic bodies; bodies capable of accessing a new terrestrial and cosmological prospect “for our globe
and its inhabitants” through acts of mental leveraging.
Kant models this thought experiment in the last section of Conflict, describing how he overcame pain by concentrating his mind fully on
the supposedly neutral name of “Cicero.” And, in a footnote, he mentions the hypomochlium with reference to Prussian infantrymen who
are trained to start out with the left foot … they put this foot in front, as on a hypomochlium, in order to use the right side for the impetus of the
attack, which they execute with the right foot against the left.
Not surprisingly, Derrida is delighted by this note. He conjures, with the meanings of left and right footing, shoes that are not a pair, what it
means to “take the call” for a foundational leap to another foundational place, and the laying out of conditions for a new orientation in thinking:
When I asked a moment ago how we should orient ourselves toward the foundation of a new law, I was citing, as you no doubt recognized, the
title of another short work by Kant (How to Be Oriented in Thinking?: Was Heisst: Sich im Denken Orientieren? [1786]. This essay speaks,
among other things, of the paradox of symmetrical objects as presented in yet another essay of 1768, Von dem ersten Grunde des
Unterschiedes der Gegenden im Raume [Foundation for the Distinction of Positions in Space], namely, that the opposition of right and left does
not arise from a conceptual or logical determination, but only from a sensory topology that can only be referred to the subjective position of the
human body … as Kant will have told us, the university will have to walk on two feet, left and right, each foot having to support the other as it rises
and with each step makes the leap.

The task of putting the university on a different footing turns out to be a matter of “determining the best lever, what the Greeks would call the
best mochlos. Derrida further points out:
The mochlos could be a wooden beam, a lever for displacing a boat, a sort of wedge for opening or closing a door, something, in short, to lean
on for forcing and displacing. Now, when one asks how to orient oneself in history, morality, or politics, the most serious discords and decisions
have to do less often with ends, it seems to me, than with levers. For example, the opposition of right and left, in this originally parliamentary
sense, is perhaps largely, if not entirely, a conflict between several strategies of political mochlos.29
Channeling Kant via a philosophy of “untranslatable nodes” (like the term mochlos), Derrida advocates no violent break or suicidal leap into
the future. He is talking, pragmatically and mechanically, about finding the right lever, about a political mochlos strapped to a political muscle
capable of harnessing political will through the aggregative force of small numbers and almost imperceptible percentages. Translation as a
kind of leveraging of language, causes the university, and the entire world, to pivot, eventually if incrementally, on its axis.

prise de position—with regard to that institution … the attempt to found a new insitution and to reverse or displace the order of its hierarchy, however radical the
intention, could never force a radical rupture with the edifice—whether ruined or phantasmatic—that precedes it. In order to take a step forward or beyond, it would have
to ‘negotiate a compromise’ with the traditional order that it seeks to displace and that offers itself as the stepping stone from which the leap to another place could be
made, if such a leap is possible and desirable. Where this is so, everything would come down to finding and using ‘the best [or right] lever’ for change.” Hent de Vries,

IV
Kilito’s Injunction:
“Thou Shalt Not Translate Me”
Philosophy in translation studies has routinely referred not to logical questions pertaining to the threshold of sense within language but to
professional practice, as in, What is your philosophy of translation? Are you a literalist in the tradition of doctrinal Hebraic, Koranic and
mystical Christian traditions seeking to preserve to the maximum the original letter of the sacred text? A word-for-wordist who proceeds by
rendering textual segments at the same rank (morpheme, word, group, clause, sentence)? A partisan of the German Romantic tradition
(spanning August Wilhelm von Schlegel and Friedrich Schleiermacher to Walter Benjamin) who construes literalism as a form of foreignized
cultural translation? Or, do you subscribe to a philosophy of free translation in the tradition of Jerome and Boethius, according to which the
faithful translator is the one faithful to God “but not to the individual words of God’s Word”?1 In other words, a sense-for-sense practitioner of
unbounded (or mixed rank) translation or a poet playing with translation loosely in imitation and pastiche? For all the rich research these
opposing traditions have inspired, what interests me most is something more pointed: what does it mean to think of translation as a kind of
philosophy, or as a way of doing theory and its history?
Such questions prompt reflection on the full impact of translation on the making of cross-continental philosophy and theory. The intra-
European and trans-Atlantic transfer of texts while often acknowledged has yet to be accounted for as part of theory’s archival remit. A
cursory sampling provides a glimpse of this as-yet-unwritten translation history. Derrida translates appendix 3 of Edmund Husserl’s 1936
study, The Crisis of European Sciences and Transcendental Phenomenology, from German into French in 1962 (John P. Leavey
translates this translation into English, along with Derrida’s introduction, in 1989). Anthony Wilden translates Jacques Lacan’s “The Function
of Language in Psychoanalysis” in 1968. Gayatri Chakravorty Spivak translates Derrida’sO f Grammatology in 1976. Alan Sheridan
translates Foucault’s Discipline and Punish in 1977. Barbara Johnson translates Derrida’s Dissémination in 1981. Margaret Waller
translates Julia Kristeva’s Revolution in Poetic Language in 1984. Catherine Porter and Carolyn Burke translate Luce Irigaray’sT his Sex
Which is Not One in 1985. Geoffrey Bennington translates Derrida’s Of Spirit: Heidegger and the Question in 1989. Peggy Kamuf
translates Derrida’s Specters of Marx: The State of the Debt, and the Work of Mourning & the New Internationa lin 1994. Paul Patton
translates Gilles Deleuze’s Difference and Repetition (a text of 1968 which comes out in English in 1994). Daniel Heller-Roazen translates
Giorgio Agamben’s Homo Sacer: Sovereign Power and Bare Life in 1998. Andrew Parker translates Jacques Rancière’s 1983 The
Philosopher and His Poor in 2004. Lisabeth During translates Catherine Malabou’s The Future of Hegel: Plasticity, Temporality, and
Dialectic in 2004. Oliver Feltham translates Alain Badiou’s 1988 Being and Event in 2005. Bruce Fink translates Lacan’s 1966 Écrits in
2006. Eric Fassin and Cynthia Kraus translate Judith Butler’s Gender Trouble into French in 2006. Bruno Bosteels translates Badiou’s 1982
Theory of the Subject in 2009. In each instance, the translator plays a pivotal role in the history of theory in her or his own right. Translational
apprenticeship, as a way of learning and teaching philosophy and theory has always been recognized, but it is something that warrants more
explicit foregrounding as a critical pedagogy in the humanities.2
If the particular text chosen for translation is clearly determinative on the course of theory (spanning both its Anglo-European and
planetary gyres), so too is the date of a translation, especially where there are discrepant periodicities. The general rule seems to be: the
greater the time lag between a text’s original publication and its translation, the greater the chance it will be misread or creatively re-
contextualized. In the case of early texts by Deleuze, Guattari, Agamben, Butler and Badiou, for example, tectonic shifts between the moment
of their cultural production and the reception of their translation led to new semantic colorations of keywords like “difference,” “the sensible,”
“biopolitical,” “publicity” (öffentlichkeit), “gender,” and “evental.” Words, say, that had specific resonance with situationism, French Maoism,
or the events of May ’68 (“situation,” “logics of revolt,” “conjuncture”) come to be substantially re-signified by the time they reach the early
2000s, whether in Žižekian Leninism or the Badiouvian post-neoliberal “communist hypothesis.”3 My point here is that in studying the history
of translation within the history of philosophy and theory, we are not just performing a philological or intellectual exercise. By gauging the
deformations, reformulations, and temporal décalages of translated works, we are doing philosophy.
The contemporary foregrounding of philosophical translation owes much to the landmark volume Difference in Translation, a collection of
essays edited by Joseph F. Graham published in 1985. Here, we find essays by Phillip E. Lewis, Cynthia Chase, RichardR and, Alan Bass,
Derrida (his important reading of Benjamin’s “The Task of the Translator” and “Des Tours de Babel”), and the late and much regretted
Barbara Johnson. As an ensemble, the collection marks a moment in the history of deconstruction in which translating, instead of being
something assumed—what critics do as part of their training—is activated as theory. In the words of the book’s editor, “We might set
translation as a test for claims about deconstruction or any other disruptive effects of discourse.” Noting in her essay “Taking Fidelity
Philosophically” that Derrida’s work “has always already been (about) translation,” Barbara Johnson offered a nuanced summary of how
translation emerged as a way of doing philosophy for Derrida:
Derrida’s rearticulation of philosophy and translation is obviously not designed to evacuate meaning entirely. But his concept of textuality
displaces the very notion of how a text means. What goes on in every text far exceeds what can be reduced to so-and-so’s “thought.” Derrida’s
own ingenious translations, such as that of Hegel’s Aufhebung by la relève, are attempts to render all the often contradictory meanings of a term
in such a way that crucial logical complexities are not oversimplified. It is quite often by finding the pressure points previously lost in translation
that Derrida rearticulates philosophy with itself.4
The rearticulation of philosophy with itself enables something quite miraculous: It eases the difficult passage of natural language into
philosophy, characterized by Derrida as the “violent difficulty in the transference of a non-philosopheme into a philosopheme.” This difficult
transference is keenly felt in Derrida’s treatment of the word pharmakon: “No, it is not a pharmakon … it is rather a pharmakon.”5 to borrow
Peggy Kamuf’s phrase in her analysis in this issue of Profession of Derrida’s “Plato’s Pharmacy.”6 Kamuf fixes on Derrida’s identification of
the invisible pivot, the Greek-to-Greek “translation” of philosopheme/pharmakon, whereby an apparent non-translation translates

nonetheless. Jonathan Rée tracks a similar operation in the case of Heideggerian Dasein:
In most contexts Dasein is equivalent to the English “existence” (for example in the Darwinian “struggle for existence”), but in Being and Time
there is a distinct concept of Existenz which had a stronger claim on that word. It could also be translated, in some ways very faithfully, as “being-
there,” except that this might reactivate the metaphysical connotations that Heidegger was determined to avoid. So Macquarrie and Robinson
were obliged to invent an artificial expression to serve as Dasein’s counterpart in English. That new English word was “Dasein,” (with a capital
initial but no italics; plural: “Daseins”; possessive: “Dasein’s”), and it was duly included in their index of English expressions.7
“No, it’s not Dasein, it is rather Dasein.” What we have here is a distinct case of fidelity to the original in which the target is samed and
othered, which is to say, philosophized.
In “Taking Fidelity Philosophically,” Barbara Johnson is fascinated by philosophy’s fidelity to philosophy by means of this Othering
process of translation. The philosopheme taps into its own untranslatability and contacts a correlative untranslatability in another language.
The quilting point of Untranslatables is where philosophy happens. This suggests that philosophy answers to untranslatability, even as it
attempts to cross over into translation. As Johnson, glossing Heidegger, puts it:
The bridge of translation, which paradoxically releases within each text the subversive forces of its own foreignness, thus reinscribes those forces
in the tensile strength of a new neighborhood of otherness. Yet all travelers on that bridge are answering a summons that repulses them at every
step, a summons reminiscent of the sign Lautréamont sets up in front of Maldoror, containing a warning that is received just a moment too late to
be heeded: “Vous, qui passez sur ce pont, n’y allez pas.” “You, who are crossing over this bridge, don’t get to the other side.”8
With the help of Lautréamont, Johnson inadvertently moves translation theory, identified throughout Difference in Translation with such topics
as abusive translation, the property of the proper name, signature, fidelity, and prosthesis, to the arena of what might be called the
translational interdiction or the first order of untranslatability: “You who attempt to cross this bridge will not pass.” This ban on passing from
one language to another may be read not just as an example of the philosopheme’s obstacle course but as an idea that has particular
resonance today in efforts to rethink theological and secular criticism. It suggests a bound of sacrosanction; a theology of translation; a
“saving difference” (an expression used by Harold Bloom that I am detourning), which is a difference believed in because it saves or
preserves the Untranslatable.9 In his chapter of The Map of Misreading titled “The Primal Scene of Instruction,” Bloom seems most
concerned with staging a scene of primal injunction whose caption might read: “Thou Shalt Translate Me.” Such a command, in exacting
submission from its addressee, corresponds to what Bloom calls “God’s condescension, his accommodating gift of his Election-Love
(‘ahabah’),” whereby “the human receiver [of God’s book whether in Scripture or in nature] incorporates and makes himself one with the
Divinely given cipher.”10
If the duty to translate is rooted in Bloom’s writing in the cipher of the Hebrew Bible and its secular re-inscription in the aesthetics of
romanticism, one could say that in more recent philosophy and theory, the duty to not translate is derived from Islamic tradition and, more
generally, from principled opposition to facile computations of cultural equivalence. I am thinking here of Abdelfattah Kilito’s injunction “Thou
shalt not translate me,” a title he used for a lecture that plays off titles used in books: “Tu ne parleras pas ma langue”11 and “Défense de
parler ma langue”. 12
A chapter begins:
One day I realized that I dislike having foreigners speak my language. How did that happen? I used to think of myself as an open-minded, liberal
person who wished unto others what he wished unto his kin. Furthermore, I used to think it my duty to endeavor as best I could to make my
language radiate its brilliance, to increase the number of its learners, and so forth. But that noble goal disappeared when I realized that I dislike
having foreigners speak my language.
Later in the chapter, Kilito recounts the time when he spoke Arabic with an American woman whose uncanny mastery of the language made
him squirm. When she uses the Moroccan colloquial term wallahila, it strikes him as transgressive: “Did she realize that wallahila contains
the word ‘Allah,’ and that she let herself so easily tread on rough terrain? She referred, probably without knowing it, to a faith that apparently
was not hers. I leave this question open.”13 The question in question circles through the problem of sacred language used in ignorance as
well as the issue of broken trust among native speakers in the face of foreign entry into their language world. Ushering in an era of suspicion
toward the apparent neutrality of translation, he attends to situation-specific power differentials among language users, thus complicating the
politics of ethical translation and bilingualism in territories scourged by histories of imperialism.
Through lessons and anecdotes of translational travesty, Kilito formulates something like the divine right of untranslatability. In “Du balcon
d’Averroès” (“Averroës’s Balcony”), a pastiche of Jorge Luis Borges’s 1949 fiction “La busca de Averroes” (“Averroës’s Search”), he gives
us a parable of the Untranslatable. Just as in Borges’s tale where Averroës emerges as the father of untranslatability theory (he discovers
that Greek concepts of tragedy and comedy are untranslatable in Arabic), so too in Kilito’s short story he is cast (at least initially) as the
author of an untranslatable Arabic phrase that comes to the narrator in a dream. This phrase, loughatouna-l-a’jamiyya, includes the root
ajam, meaning non-Arab or Barbarian, and is rendered in French as notre langue étrangère, literally “our foreign tongue.”14 The enigmatic
sense of the expression obsesses the narrator: it refers to the common Barbarian tongue for both Arabs and Greeks (Persian); to a native
language as foreigners speak it; to foreignized or Othered speech within one’s native tongue; to the experience of being unhomed in the
mother tongue; and to many other meanings besides. The phrase seems to guard the trace of an Ursprache—a sacred, originary language
that will reveal the origin of philosophy.
Suspecting that philosophy has been lost in translation, the narrator stages a spectatorial encounter where a faceless Averroës (standing
for Philosophy) and the narrator’s translator (standing for Translation) confront each other in a de Chirico—like dream setting: a courtyard
overlooked by one opaque and three transparent windows. This face-off is interrupted, however, when a fight breaks out between the
translator and the narrator. The former accuses the latter of insulting the Arabic language, calling him to account for repressing the attribution
of the little phrase to Averroës (an attribution he claims to have discovered in a work by the narrator on the picaresque novel that he, the
translator, had translated into Arabic). “You did not even read my translation because it was in Arabic!” he rebukes him resentfully. The
hapless narrator learns that he has betrayed his own text by betraying Arabic. In the end, he makes amends, restoring Arabic to its position
of glory as a singular God-language: the origin and end of all languages, the master-original and the master target.
The narrator ultimately solves the mystery of his phrase. Pursuing a chain of associations that leads from Borges (whose Averroës is
horrified by the danger to classical Arabic posed by the colloquial mixture of Spanish and Arabic spoken by Spanish street kids) to the
Biblical legend of Noah’s Ark (where the deluge is taken as a metaphor for the catastrophic loss of Arabic), he traces it to the preface of a
renowned Arabic dictionary Lisane al-’arab, whose author, the thirteenth-century Egyptian scholar Ibn Manzour, was the first to apprehend
fully that Arabic was becoming an endangered language. Taking up Manzour’s cause (and in defiance of his feckless translator, who, in a
strange turnaround now speaks to him only in French), the narrator assumes the mantle of Arabic defender, becoming, as it were, a
champion of the translational interdiction. Though the story’s message is elusive—it would seem, after all to be satirizing Arabocentrism, and
by extension all forms of cultural nationalism (manifest, say, in the recent prosecutions of insults to Turkishness)—it nonetheless uncovers the
moral structures of guilt and betrayal that hold the translational prohibition in place and that theologically underwrite the complex cathexes of
writers to their languages.

Waïl S. Hassan, in his translator’s preface to the English edition of Thou Shalt Not Speak My Language, sets the context for Kilito’s
protective barrier around Arabic (referring to “literalism” in an argument similar to the one undergirding Jewish proscription against
translation of the Hebrew Bible). He reminds us that the “sacred book of Islam is, from the perspective of the faithful, untranslatable because
it is considered the literal word of God; human beings are incapable of exhausting its meaning, let alone transposing it into other
languages.”15 Hassan recalls too that within much Islamic tradition, translators are readily held to be apostates and heretics; a class of
interpreters worthy of punishment even if they also qualify for redemption. Emphasizing the folk etymologies clustering around the Arabic
words for translator mutarjim, turjuman, and tarjamah (terms that in actual fact redound etymologically to the Turkish tercümen, itself an
Ottomanized version of the ancient Babylonian word for “interpreter,” targumannu, later Europeanized as dragoman), Hassan notes that the
phonetically similar but nonetymologically related Arabic word tarjamah
shares the root verb rajama, which means “to stone to death,” with a sizable constellation of words that include rajm (killing, beating, battering,
reviling), rajam (well, cavity, hole in the ground, oven), rujum (shooting stars), rajeem (cast with stones, damned, an epithet of Satan, and also
driven away, reviled, insulted), rujmah (grave and tombstones). The verb rajama also appears in expressions like rajm bil-ghayb (conjecture,
soothsaying) and kalam marjum (uncertain or unreliable speech). Tarjamah therefore carries connotations of alienated speech that have the
flavor of falsehood, damnation, and death, but also possibilities of survival, narration and understanding.16
Thou Shalt Not Speak My Language articulates in a casual idiom the fearsome possessive requirements of owning Arabic. To own Arabic
is to assume its untranslatability, its status as an Adamic language.
In an earlier book, La langue d’Adam (“the language of Adam”), Kilito stipulates that babel, or plurilingual confusion, should no longer be
regarded as punishment for man’s hubris but instead as a divine sign; a manifestation of God’s intention to mark and preserve linguistic
difference among men.17 Monolingualism (particular to each language comprising the diversity of languages) becomes something to have
and to hold in reverence. Such an idea runs patently counter, of course, to the reigning orthodoxy of linguistic pluralism so commonly
embraced by comparative literature and World Literature programs. However, though it risks sounding anti-comparatist or at worst
xenophobic, Kilito’s bid for monolingualism seems, rather, directed at safeguarding the sacred in language, particularly as it resides in the
singularity of poetry. Arabic poetry is Kilito’s model Untranslatable—even more than the Qur’an—for in passing from one language to
another, the poem loses rhyme. Once rhyme is sacrificed, poetry turns into something other, which is to say, prose.18 Translation, according
to Kilito, cuts the poetic thread, the very same thread that organizes and regulates the cosmos. His injunction “Thou shalt not translate me”
rests on a heightened awareness of translational taboo—of translation’s potential for violating sacred tongues or the private language of
prayer and recitation. But it skirts the issue of ecclesiastical censorship imposed by clerical institutions.
One thinks here of the Wycliffe and Tyndale bibles, preeminent historical cases of death-by-translation. An Oxford-trained theologian of
the fourteenth century, referred to as the “Morning Star” of the Reformation, John Wycliffe translated the New Testament from the Vulgate into
Middle English in 1382. He garnered the hostility of the Church when he became a critic of indulgences, a supporter of the “poor priests,”
and a proponent of
the doctrine of “dominion by grace,” whereby each man was immediately and directly responsible to God, to obey God’s law, which meant not
canon law but the Bible, which was the codification, in both Testaments, of the law of God.
Wycliffe’s Bible was linked to the move toward independence from papal Rome, especially among commoners. After his death, he was
declared a heretic and his remains exhumed and desecrated. And under the Anti-Wycliffe Statute of 1401, his followers, known as Lollards,
were persecuted. The use of his unauthorized English Scripture became an offense punishable by death.19 Many Wycliffites were burned at
the stake; among them, the Bohemian religious thinker John Hus and Lord Cobham (Sir John Oldcastle), a favorite of Henry IV, who,
following a sensational escape from the Tower of London was recaptured and slowly roasted alive, all the while calling out for his right to
Scripture in English. Close to 150 years later, William Tyndale met the same fate. Swayed by Lutheran reforms, and profoundly influenced by
his teacher, Erasmus, who was already at work on the Greek translation of the New Testament, Tyndale used his gifts as a linguist to draw
on Hebrew and Greek texts for his English translation of the New Testament. When it appeared in 1526, vernacular editions were still illegal,
and Tyndale added insult to injury by impugning Cardinal Wolsey and Henry VIII in a political pamphlet. In a an ironic twist, Tyndale was
condemned as a heretic and executed in 1536 only to have his translation become the backbone of Henry VIII’s Great Bible four years after
his death. As translation martyrs, Wycliffe and Tyndale may be abstracted as examples of how the politics of sacred language becomes
imbricated in the politics of religious institutions (particularly when what is at issue is control over lay instruction).20
If matters of life and death hinge on translation in these cases, it is due in large part to the fact that the sanctity of the language of Holy
Writ is freighted with the oldest authorizing claims and questions of Judeo-Christian and Islamic hermeneutics: In what language did God
originally speak? Did Adam speak the same language before and after the fall? If the Logos is the divine Ursprache overriding any given
language, is Babel its tongue? When (as Derrida argues) John identified Christ as the mediator of thel ogos, did he set him up as the very
model of the sovereign mediator-translator? Why did God transmit the Qur’an to Mohammed (via the angel Gabriel) in Arabic? Was the
Septuagint’s “perfect” Greek translation of the Old Testament divinely inspired (such that the seventy interpreters, working in isolation,
miraculously reproduced identical copy, including the same mistranslations) or mediated by Alexandrian Jewish translators? Did linguistic
discrepancies between the Hebrew and Greek Bibles serve as God’s way of signaling to the Jews that the Hebrew Bible was exclusively
their own? Once Latin became the official language of the Bible in Europe and was decreed Untranslatable, how was this policy reconciled
with the fact that the Latin Bible was known to be a translation of Old Testament Hebrew and New Testament Coptic and Greek? How does
one sever, as Luther tried to do, the Jewish body of the Hebrew letter from the Christian spirit, even as one elevates the Hebrew Bible as the
authoritative source for the German translation? Should Walter Benjamin’s biblical model of interlinear translation at the end of the essay
“The Task of the Translator” be read as a call to translational messianism or as the scholarly acknowledgement of “a larger Jewish and
indeed international modernist movement toward ‘foreignizing’ translation, as evidenced by [Martin] Buber and [Franz] Rosenzweig’s
archaizing, Hebraizing Bible translation?”21 Such questions continue to resonate in contemporary debate: Are religious exponents of prayer
or religious ceremonies “in the original” adhering to what Wai Chee Dimock calls “an orthodox and fetishizing claim about untranslatability,”
or are they deferring to faith in untranslatability as the guarantor of, as Lawrence Rosenwald put it, “the idea of a sacred language distinct
from its secular alternative”?22 These problems of translation and theology have moved to the center of translation theory as it works through
problems of messianism, fedeism, orthodoxy, doctrinal fidelity, the bounds of secular law, tolerance, ethical neighboring, the right to offend,
literalism, and linguistic monotheism.
In The Translation Zone, I used the term “linguistic monotheism” to characterize the singularity of sacred tongues and, if I reprise it here,
it is in the context of theologies of untranslatability. My interest in these theologies stems not from the classic liberal position of “trying to
understand,” as a secular person, the attraction of believers to linguistic messianism or the uncontestable authority of religious books whose
sacrosanct status must be protected by censorship and fatwahs. It derives, rather, from the sense that translational interdictions define those
points where theologies of translation and philosophies of language converge, especially with regard to peformative proclamations. Ayman
El-Desouky ventures the proposition that there exists in Islamic doctrine the concept of “a kerygmatic single standing utterance [that] may be
initially viewed as comparable to the commandments of Genesis and the Call to Discipleship in the Gospels.”23 A term for proclamatory
enunciation kerygma may be enlisted as the new name for the Untranslatable in its condition of theological sanction and linguistic

inviolability, whether in the guise of glossolalia, the religious “call,” or the more commonplace ban on “you” speaking “my” language. 24

1 Douglas Robinson, entry on “Free Translation” in Routledge Encyclopedia of Translation Studies, ed. Mona Baker, London: Routledge, 1998, 87.
2 Rée, “The Translation of Philosophy,” in New Literary History 2001, 231. Jonathan Rée makes the following case for the importance of translation in the history of
philosophy: “In the English language in the twentieth century, for example, many of the finest translations are philosophical, and many of the greatest works of
philosophy are translations. Miller’s version of Hegel’s Phenomenology, published in 1977, is brilliant; its only clear drawback is that it has overshadowed the very
different but no less marvelous translation by Baillie, dating from 1910. Then there is the meticulous work of several dedicated translators of Husserl, and a remarkable
sequence of parallel texts (German and English on facing pages), which began with C. K. Ogden’s edition of Wittgenstein’s Tractatus in 1922 and continued in J. L.
Austin’s version of Frege’s Foundations of Arithmetic and G. E. M. Anscombe’s of Wittgenstein’s Investigations. Norman Kemp Smith’s commanding version of The
Critique of Pure Reason has been read ragged by thousands of students since it was first published in 1929, but hardly any serious weaknesses have come to light …
Probably the most magnificent achievement of all, though, is Macquarrie and Robinson’s version of Heidegger’s Being and Time, whose publication in 1962 marked an
epoch in the history of philosophy in English.”
3 Alain Badiou, L’hypothèse communiste, Clamecy, France: Nouvelles Editions Lignes, 2009.
4 Joseph F. Graham, “Introduction,” Difference in Translation, ed. Joseph F. Graham, Ithaca, NY: Cornell University Press, 1985, 17; Barbara Johnson, “Taking
Fidelity Philosophically,” in Difference in Translation, 144 and 145.
5 Derrida, cited ibid., 146; Jacques Derrida, “La Pharmacie de Platon,” in La Dissémination, Paris: Editions de Minuit, 1972; English translation: “Plato’s
Pharmacy,” in Dissemination, trans. Barbara Johnson, Chicago: University of Chicago Press, 1981.
6 In her essay “Passing Strange: The Laws of Translation,” Peggy Kamuf writes: “According to the myth Socrates recounts, an Egyptian demi-god presented his
invention of writing to the king of the gods, calling it a pharmakon and praising all the benefits it will bring humankind. The King responded in effect, no, it is not a
pharmakon, as you pretend, it is rather a pharmakon and you have been deluded by your fondness for this invention of yours. It will not benefit men but deprive them of
the benefit they now realize by having to exercise their memories. Between the first and second iteration of the word, the value or meaning of pharmakon and thus of
writing turns, as Derrida puts it, ‘on its strange and invisible pivot’ (97; 109). It is thus already a scene of interpretative translation, from Greek to Greek, where the King
gets to decide both what the other meant to say and what he did not say, but should have said, in other words, the truth about writing. At stake, then, in transposing
into another language this pivotal play is not just the repetition of a same word bearing different and indeed opposable meanings, but the point of view in the text from
which the true value of writing is decided. What is brought out by Derrida is the reproduction by translation of the point of view and the interpretation of the King.” (See
this issue of Profession, 66.)
7 Rée, “The Translation of Philosophy,” 232.
8 Johnson, “Taking Fidelity Philosophically,”148.
9 Harold Bloom, A Map of Misreading, Oxford: Oxford University Press, 1975, 43. The phrase “saving difference” is coined by Bloom in his response to Derrida’s
notion of différance. “Writing, as Derrida tropes it, both keeps us from the void and, more aggressively (as against voicing), gives us a saving difference, by preventing
that coincidence of speaker with subject that would entrap us in a presence so total as to stop the mind.”
10 Ibid., 51–2.
11 Thou Shalt Not Speak My Language.
12 (Rendered as “Thou Dost Not, and Shalt Not, Speak My Language.”) Abdelfattah Kilito, Tu ne parleras pas ma langue, trans. (from Arabic into French) Francis
Gouin, Paris: Sindbad, 2002. English edition, Thou Shalt Not Speak My Language, trans. (from Arabic into English) Waïl S. Hassan, Syracuse, NY: Syracuse
University Press, 2008.
13 Kilito, Thou Shalt Not Speak My Language, 87; ibid., 92.
14 “Du balcon d’Averroès,” in Le cheval de Nietzsche, Casablanca: Editions Le Fennec, 2007.
15 Kilito, Thou Shalt Not Speak My Language, xix.
16 Kilito, Thou Shalt Not Speak My Language, x. My thanks to David Bellos for his comments on the etymological history associated with the Arabic terms for
translator and translation.
17 Kilito writes: “Dissemination in space, diversity of languages and colors: that’s a positive thing, the concretization of God’s design. The confusion of languages is
not a malediction, it is a divine sign, like dispersion in space. God installs diversity in his creation, and it is an effect of his merciful will that men are finally
disseminated, evidently so as to populate and fructify the planet.” (Translation from the French text my own.) Abdelfattah Kilito, La langue d’Adam, Casablanca: Les
Editions Toubkal, 1999, 20.
18 Deferring to the ancient philosopher al-Jahiz, Kilito writes in a chapter of Thou Shalt Not Speak My Language: “He is of the opinion that ‘poetry cannot, and
should not, be translated. When translated, its rhyme is disrupted, its meter ruined, its beauty lost, and its wonder fades.’” Kilito, Thou Shalt Not Speak My Language,
19 David Daniell, The Bible in English, New Haven, CT: Yale University Press, 2003, 71–2; on John Wycliffe’s translation of the New Testament and use of his
unauthorized English Scripture, see, ibid. 171–2.
20 A contemporary example of the translational interdict as a form of religious censorship and persecution is the extension of the fatwah from Salman Rushdie
himself (condemned for The Satanic Verses) to the death of the book’s translators (Hitoshi Igarashi, the Japanese translator, was stabbed to death in 1991; the book’s
Italian and Turkish translators were injured in attacks in 1991 and 1993, respectively).
21 John identifying Christ as the mediator of the Logos: Derrida, The Beast and the Sovereign, 450; Naomi Seidman, “‘A Gift for the Jewish People’: Einspruch’s
Der Bris Khadoshe as Missionary Translation and Yiddish Literature,” unpublished paper.
22 These remarks were made in the context of a Facebook group forum initiated by Wai Chee Dimock titled “Rethinking World Literature.” The exchange took place
between Dimock and Lawrence Rosenwald under the discussion topic “Translation and Empire,” on January 7, 2010. Also relevant, Judith Butler’s query, formulated in
response to Étienne Balibar’s surmise that “The religious as such is a point of untranslatability.” Butler asks “if translation has a theological history, does that
theological history simply fall away when translation is positioned as the neutral arbiter of religious views?” See Judith Butler, Parting Ways: Jewishness and the
Critique of Zionism, New York: Columbia University Press, 2012, 16.
23 Ayman A. El-Desouky, “Global Translatio and the Sacred: On the Hermeneutics of Voice Between the Kerygmatic and the Revelatory,” paper presented at the
Modern Language Association Conference, January 2011.
24 Glossolalia, especially in the Christian Pentecostal tradition, which emphasizes the phenomenon of “speaking in tongues,” or speaking the tongue of the Angels,
exemplifies an interesting case of singular enunciation. At once linguistically incomprehensible to the non-initiate (Untranslatable) and intelligible to the community of
believers (universally translatable), glossolalia are conferred through the intermediary of The Holy Spirit doubling, as Daniel Heller-Roazen following Agamben has
emphasized, the “barbarian” within: “‘Glossolalia’ is a technical expression referring to a variety of speech act whose name derives from the Greek term for ‘speaking in
tongues.’ St. Paul may have been the first to define this linguistic practice. In his first letter to the Corinthians, he enjoins his addressees not to ‘speak into the air.’ To
‘speak in tongues,’ Paul suggests, is how to forget the meaning of one’s words. It is to abandon one’s tongue for ‘tongues’ and ‘obscure expressions,’ such that one
becomes a ‘child in understanding’ and a ‘barbarian … that speaketh unto a barbarian.’ Giorgio Agamben has commented that such speech consists not so much in
the ‘“pure uttering of inarticulate sounds” as in a “speaking in gloss,” that is, in words whose meaning one does not know.’ To hear such sounds is to know they mean
something without knowing exactly what such a ‘something’ might be; in other words, it is to discern an intention to signify that cannot be identified with any particular
signification. Agamben notes that the traditional translations of the Greek text of Paul’s letter fail to capture the full radicalism of the linguistic ‘barbarism’ that it clearly
describes. Whereas the King James version, following the Vulgate, has ‘If I know not the meaning of the voice, I shall be unto he that speaketh a barbarian, and he that
speaketh shall be a barbarian unto me,’ a literal rendition would read otherwise on a single, decisive point: ‘If I know not the meaning of the voice, I shall be unto he that
speaketh a barbarian, and he that speaketh shall be a barbarian in me [en hemoi].’ ‘The text’s en hemoi,’ Agamben writes, ‘can only signify “in me,” and what Paul
means is perfectly clear: if I utter words whose meaning I do not understand, he who speaks in me, the voice that utters them, the very principle of speech in me, will
be something barbarous, something that does not know how to speak and that does not know what it says.’” See Daniel Heller-Roazen, “Glossolalia,” entry for the
English edition of the Vocabulaire.

Part Four
Who Owns My Translation?

I
Marx’s Bovary
In “Thyroid Diary,” one of the stories included in her collection, Lydia Davis, author of a much-acclaimed English translation ofM adame
Bovary, relies on a first-person narrator who happens to be a professional translator:
I have been trying to see if I am thinking more slowly than before. In my translation work, for instance, I see that I sometimes try to find an
equivalent in English before I really understand the French. Then I realize that I don’t understand the French, even after trying several times, and
I cast my eye rather listlessly here and there within the paragraph, hoping the meaning of it will fall into place by itself, which it sometimes does.
But today it does not, and then my mind wanders. I go back to work on it, look in the dictionary again, reading every word of a very long entry, but
there’s nothing in the dictionary that helps me. I want to put something down, anything at all, just to mark the place so that I can go on translating
and come back to the problem later. I need to put down something noticeably wrong, so that later I will see that the spot needs work, but
everything I think of is so poor that it is embarassing. I don’t know why I should be embarassed, if there is no one to see it, but I am embarassed
and will not go on until I put down something decent, though wrong. At least, this morning, as I was studying the dictionary carefully in order to see
if I really wanted to use the word, I learned more about embarassment and its earlier, concrete meaning of “encumbrance” or “obstruction”…
Now, if I do this whole translation, which is an important job, with my mind not working very clearly, then the translation may not be very good
… though I may not know that. And if it isn’t very good, that would really be unfortunate, since some of my future income may depend on it.1
In taking the reader through the arduous relay of dictionary word checking, in analyzing the “listlessness”and “embarassment” that translation
can engender, Davis’s narrator recalls Charles Bovary, introduced in the famous opening scene as a dull-witted translator of Latin sentenced
by his teacher to copy out ridiculus sum twenty times2: “We saw him working conscientiously, looking up all the words in the dictionary and
taking great pains … for while he knew his rules passably well, he had almost no elegance in his constructions.”3 The word “tournure,”
translated by Davis as “construction,” draws attention to the laboriousness of translation. Belaboring the translator’s Aufgabe, Davis seems
to have channeled the spirit of Eleanor Marx, the first recognized translator of Madame Bovary into English.
The story of the English translation of Gustave Flaubert’sM adame Bovary by Eleanor Marx, Karl Marx’s youngest daughter, reads like a
novel worthy of the title “Kapital (The Novel)” or “Marx’s Bovary, Flaubert’s Kapital.” It is the tale of a world literature classic in translation into
whose folds bits of the personal histories of its translators are secretly embedded. Flaubert and Karl Marx have often been written about
together, but I am interested here in approaching their relation on the bias of translation, and through the intermediary of Eleanor Marx. I am
interested in writing the “biography of a translation,” one that in principle would yield the screenplay for a weepie featuring an aspirational
feminist storyline, philandering, betrayed love, and suicide. Yet these familiar plot lines diagram something more than melodramatic
boilerplate; they show the extraordinary place of Eleanor Marx in the history of translation. We begin to see “Marxist translation” not just as
women’s work but also as a theory of what Eleanor herself called “conscientious” translation.
Though apparently not the very first English translation of Flaubert’s masterwork—a preexisting 1881 edition by John Stirling (pen name
of a Mrs. M. Sherwood) appears in the bibliography of D. J. Colewell and an equally intangible “lost” version has been attributed to Juliet
Herbert, the governess of Flaubert’s niece—Eleanor Marx’s translation of Madame Bovary has endured like none other.4 Following its
publication in 1886, it became the basis of many succeeding editions, including the teaching edition by Charles I. Weir Jr. in 1948 (used by
Vladimir Nabokov), Paul de Man’s 1965 Norton edition, and Margaret Cohen’s 2004 Norton edition, all published in the United States5. The
critical attention that this translation has received from the beginning has attached the questions surrounding Flaubert’s style to those of his
translator, and has drawn attention to the parallels between Emma Bovary’s and Eleanor Marx’s suicides in the wake of failed relationships.6
Eleanor Marx’s Madame Bovary gained its greatest notoriety when Vladimir Nabokov referred to it as an exemplary instance of bad
translation. Nabokov used a 1948 Rinehardt edition of her translation, revised by Charles I. Weir Jr. (“Wonder what these were,” Nabokov
wrote next to the “Corrections and Modernization by the editor” credit.) In this text, bequeathed to the New York Public Library, Nabokov
marked up what he considered to be Eleanor’s most egregious errors, many of them pertaining to descriptions of Emma’s appearance. In
emendations to her text and notes to the Madame Bovary chapter of his Lectures on Literature, Nabokov lit into Marx’s rendering of the
imperfect tense—recognized by Proust as Flaubert’s masterful means of conveying temporal unity and continuity—without mentioning her by
name.7
Any projected “biography of a translation” must take stock of Nabokov’s castigations, but just as important is an account of Paul de Man’s
intervention. Why did de Man choose to base the Norton edition on the translation by Eleanor Marx when this version was clearly so dated?
The obvious answer—that the translation was out of copyright and would save money for the publisher and both money and time for the
editors—was not especially convincing even if it remained the primary reason.
In 1962, de Man was recommended by M. H. Abrams for the job of editing Madame Bovary for the recently launched Norton series.
Already a rising star of literary criticism, de Man promised to burnish the new series with intellectual brio and scholarly authority. “The
Substantially New Translation Based on the Version by Eleanor Marx Aveling” appeared with his signature after three years of work on the
translation in 1965, and was reprised in Margaret Cohen’s 2004 Norton edition. Time constraints apparently hindered Cohen from
