undertaking her own translation, part of the interest of which would have been to toy with convention by re-titling the work Mrs. Bovary! But in
her case, as in de Man’s, there was undoubtedly something about the Marx “standard edition” that could not be supplanted or wished away,
as in the case of James Strachey’s translation of Freud.
Paul de Man may have been attracted to the Marx translation because of the name “Marx,” which allowed him to establish his own
placement in a unique genealogy; an executor not only of Flaubert but, through intellectual heredity, of Karl Marx, and by extension, of the
Hegelian tradition of dialectical thinking that challenged the neo-finalism of de Man’s formation. Conceivably, de Man’s work on Madame
Bovary—the only work of French fiction that bears his signature as translator (despite a plan to translate Rousseau)—afforded him insight
into the problem of translation. It was of course Walter Benjamin’s essay “The Task of the Translator” that occasioned theoretical reflection in
his last lecture. It was here that he argued that translation reveals “the pain and suffering of language itself.”8
What was the extent of de Man’s contribution to the Marx translation? Highly limited, if we go with the account given by de Man’s daughter

What was the extent of de Man’s contribution to the Marx translation? Highly limited, if we go with the account given by de Man’s daughter
who was under the impression that her father’s revised copy was lost in transit from Germany. Discouraged and unable to recommence, he
apparently turned the project over to his wife Patricia de Man. This version of the story supports Neil Hertz’s recollection. According to Hertz,
a close friend and one-time colleague of de Man’s at Cornell, it was Patricia who did the bulk of the work and even petitioned Norton after
Paul’s death for double billing on the editorial credits of future reissues (to no avail):
I would guess Paul went over it pretty carefully, so his sensibility would leave traces, no doubt—perhaps even more determinative than Marx’s on
Eleanor Aveling’s translation?! But these men, these men … with their loyal women! Who needs it? Pat died earlier this year, so there’s no way of
checking all this with her, alas.
To treat de Man’s Madame Bovary as the sexist appropriation of a woman’s labor falls into the old trap of reducing woman to the status of
victim, and panders to a cottage industry of de Man-bashing. (Typically, de Man’s personal history of covering up his anti-Semitic wartime
journalism is used to indict deconstruction as a theoretical smokescreen for rank moral relativism.) And yet, even if one is concerned to
avoid such reductionism, de Man’s 1965 preface is a peculiarly passive-aggressive text; one that seems to belie his claim to “extensive”
revision. If one goes back to Marx’s 1886 text, what is most surprising is how faithful the de Man version was to her translation, not the
reverse. Reviewing his preface—with the Marx text in view—one can only conclude that it disavows the extent of his debt to Eleanor’s work
(and behind that, to that of his spouse Patricia). De Man wrote:
The text here reproduced is an extensively revised version of the older translations of Madame Bovary, done by Eleanor Marx Aveling, the
daughter of Karl Marx … the Marx Aveling text has advantages and drawbacks. One of its main virtues is relatively high degree of fidelity in
rendering the cadence of Flaubert’s sentence. Other translators have produced versions that are a great deal more fluent and idiomatic. But
Flaubert himself is neither fluent nor really idiomatic (except in conversations), and by adhering more closely to his rhythm, Mrs. Aveling
sometimes succeeds in conveying Flaubert’s carefully controlled syntax.9
There are several things to note in this preface: First is the instability of naming when referring to Eleanor Marx. De Man passes from
Eleanor Marx Aveling, to the epithet “daughter of Karl,” to the Marx Aveling text, to Mrs. Aveling. Eleanor herself fell prey to a similar instability
of self-naming. She was an inconsistent signatory of letters and manuscripts, and overly conscious of being a literary help-mate: first to
Prosper-Olivier Lissagaray (whose 1876 L’Histoire de la Commune de 1871 she translated into English in 1886), then to Edward Aveling,
and, most constantly, to her father. The “daughtering” and “spousing” of Eleanor goes along with the phrase “adhering more closely to his
rhythm,” which underscores feminine psychic and physiological servitude to a male author. This syndrome seems eerily repeated in the case
of Patricia de Man, but then, one might well ask, who ascertained the “unfluency” of Flaubert’s French? Patricia or Paul? Though it now
seems quite certain that Patricia de Man did the lion’s share of the revisions, that Paul de Man’s principal motive for editing the novel was
financial, and that Eleanor Marx’s translation provided a cheap and efficient means of remitting the commissioned text, these revelations do
not alter the fact that Paul de Man should be credited with the intuition that riches could be wrung from Eleanor Marx’s “fidelity” to Flaubert’s
“unfluent” style.10
The de Man version restored Flaubert’s paragraph structure and narrative units (following consultation of the authoritative Dumesnil,
Conard, and Garnier editions), while generally preserving Eleanor Marx’s punctuation and diacritical alterations. Whether Marx’s punctuation
was as idiosyncratic as it might seem—she inserts question marks, for example, where none appear in the French original text—remains a
matter of debate since Flaubert left his manuscripts notoriously unpunctuated and avowed his hatred of typography and redacted versions of
his books.11 What does seem clear, when one compares Eleanor Marx’s translation to de Man’s revised version, is that de Man modernized
the diction by de-Victorianizing style, drying out sentimentalities, and rationalizing metaphor:
Several misleading inaccuracies and mistranslations have been corrected. Especially in Part II and III of the novel entire pages had, at times, to
be rewritten; Mrs. Aveling often loses track of the meaning in the meditative, inward passages and renders the already obscure original altogether
opaque. One feels about this patching and mending job the way a surgeon must feel about a difficult operation: the patient is by no means as
good as new, but he should at least feel some relief. Students of this text should be a little closer to Flaubert’s original intention than when the
Marx Aveling translation was sprung upon them without warning.12
As a justification of amputation in the name of “cure,” de Man’s “surgical operation” recalls Bovary’s botched operation on Hippolyte’s
clubfoot (resulting in the poor boy’s leg amputation). The surgical metaphor may distantly allude to the profession of Flaubert’s father, and to
the oft-repeated commonplace that Flaubert’s clinical realism was indebted to his early exposure to the operating theater adjoining the family
home. But most important, it pathologizes Marx’s translation, and by extension Eleanor herself, positioning her as “patient”—an hysteric or
détraquée—ready to ambush the reader with an unforeseen attack.
De Man reserved his harshest criticism for Eleanor’s translation of the novel’s “meditative, inward” passages; those famed moments of
subjectivity in parts 2 and 3. And yet it was precisely in one of those passages that one finds evidence of some rather pathological
translating on de Man’s part. Homais has just delivered to Emma the news of her father-in-law’s death and she is in a peculiar state: a death
has been announced; a deadly poison has been revealed; her monotonous, grieving family surrounds her; and she sits, locked in private
reverie, savoring the pleasure of her latest tryst with Léon. It is at this point in the 2004 Marx/de Man/Cohen edition that we read: “She would
have liked to stop hearing and seeing, in order to keep intact the stillness of her love; but, try as she would, the memory would vanish under
the impact of outer sensations.” On first reading, this phrase in the English version, my attention was riveted by the phrase “the stillness of her
love.” “Still” underscores absence of movement and absence of sound; a state of utter acoustic and kinetic quiescence. The phrase acted as
a referential compass for numerous points in the text. It referred to the tension between the barest movement of light and shadow and the
silence of the natural bower at the moment when Emma finally yields to Rodolphe: “Here and there around her, in the leaves or on the ground,
trembled luminous patches … Silence was everywhere.” It recalled the “blue space” of love, an arrested spot of time in which jouissance is
remembered as a sensation of spatial immensity:
She was entering upon a marvelous world where all would be passion, ecstasy, delirium. She felt herself surrounded by an endless rapture. A
blue space surrounded her and ordinary existence appeared only intermittently between these heights, dark and far away beneath her.
The chain of associations leading from “stillness of her love” to the “blue space of bliss” seemed to give directly onto the death-drive. “The
aim of all life is death,” Freud wrote in Beyond the Pleasure Principle in describing the pulsion of “the instinct to return to the inanimate
state.” Emma’s being yields to inertia, syncope, catalepsy, and drugged stupor (“She felt a stupor invading her, as if from opium fumes”), with
each state advancing the pull of the suicide-drive:
Why not end it all? What restrained her? She was free. She advanced, looked at the paving stones, saying to herself, “Jump! jump!”
The ray of light reflected straight from below drew the weight of her body towards the abyss … She was right at the edge, almost hanging,
surrounded by vast space. The blue of the sky invaded her, the air was whirling in her hollow head.13
Emma experiences the sensation of being absorbed by space; a condition in which the outside comes in and occupies the seat of
consciousness, and all traces of an exterior world yield to subjective interiority. The cliché of “a still point in a turning world” comes to mind,
not just because this is a moment when “time stands still” as Emma’s life hangs in the balance but also because Binet’s lathe is turning;

producing a drone that blocks out the reassuring sound of human voices and prompts the programming of self-destruction. The “stillness of
her love,” an arresting trope, thus acquires performative force as the rhetorical hinge between the life and death instincts.
I went back to the French original to see how Flaubert had rendered the phrase “Elle aurait voulu ne rien entendre, ne rien voir, afin de ne
pas déranger le recueillement de son amour qui allait se perdant, quoi qu’elle fit, sous les sensations extérieures.” The phrase was nowhere
to be found! In its place, there was only nothing—rien—substantivized like a thing or noun: “Elle aurait voulu ne rien entendre, ne rien voir.”
What had happened to this nothingness in the Marx/de Man/Cohen translation? The Eleanor Marx text was reassuring on this score: “She
would have liked to hear nothing, to see nothing, so as not to disturb the meditation on her love, that, do what she would, became lost in
external sensations.” Lydia Davis’s translation follows Marx quite closely but renders “receuillement” with “recollection” instead of
“meditation.” “She wished she could hear nothing, see nothing, so as not to disturb the recollection of her love, which was steadily vanishing,
no matter what she did, under external sensations.” Adam Thorpe’s Vintage translation (committed to pre-1857 vocabulary and expressions)
hews like Davis to the original syntax, though he replaces Davis’s stress on memory and sensation with the visually oriented figures of
contemplation and impression: “She would have liked to hear nothing, see nothing, so as not to disturb the contemplation of her love which
would disappear, whatever she did, beneath external impressions.”14 In neither the Marx nor the Davis nor the Thorpe versions is there a
reference to the “stillness of her love,” no false trope. “Nothing” is kept intact, dutifully performing the work of reinforcing Flaubert’s evocation
of “le néant.”15 If “nothing” were regained, there were, however, other losses of meaning. “Nothing” was manifest in the Eleanor Marx version
at the expense of memory, since the effort of Emma’s consciousness to “re-collect” (recueillir) memory-impressions was hardly conveyed by
the word “meditation” (the very word, we recall, that de Man used to signal Eleanor’s translation failures). Also sacrificed was any attempt to
render the phrase “son amour qui allait se perdant,” which, though nicely rendered by Davis as “steadily vanishing,” blunts the economic ratio
of “pay as you go” love, receding as it expands; an image harking back to Balzac’s magical ass’s skin that shrinks life with every erotic wish
fulfillment.
Despite such drawbacks, Marx’s language was an improvement over de Man’s illegitimate figuration. But then, in an odd twist, de Man’s
use of “the stillness of her love,” a trope that was not there, came back in different guises, first in Chapter 3 of Part 3 in a scene of Emma and
Léon’s moonlit lovemaking on a boat, traced by Nabokov (in his marginalia) to the fourth strophe of Lamartine’s Le Lac:
Ils restaient au fond, tous les deux cachés par l’ombre, sans parler. Les avirons carrés sonnaient entre les volets de fer; et cela marquait dans le
silence comme un battement de métronome, tandis qu’à l’arrière de la bauce qui traînait ne discontinuait pas son petit clapotement doux dans
l’eau.
“Stillness” here supports the sentimental cliché of lovers enjoying a speech-free (sex-filled) moment. The Marx translation, which aligns the
lovers’ “silence” with the air’s “stillness,” seems motivated by the desire to exaggerate the irritating racket of the background noise (on the
order of Binet’s lathe):
They sat at the bottom, both hidden by the shade, in silence. The square oars rang in the iron thwarts, and, in the stillness, seemed to mark time,
like the beating of a metronome, while at the stern the rudder that trailed behind never ceased its gentle splash against the water.
Nabokov makes the sound even louder when he submits “ceased its splashy sound” as an improvement. The de Man translation, by contrast,
was concerned less with noise and more with beat. Truer to Flaubert’s “sans parler,” he avoided Eleanor Marx’s play on “silence/stillness,”
opting instead to emphasize the withholding of language, its replacement by the sound of things:
They stayed below, hidden in darkness, without saying a word. The square-tipped oars sounded against the iron oar-locks in the stillness, they
seemed to mark time like the beat of a metronome, while the rope that trailed behind never ceased its gentle splash against the water.16
The ritornelle between “square-tipped oars” and “iron oar-locks” simulates the metronome beat. It assigns a special role to “stillness” as the
signal pause of the musical measure, marking time in the last hours of an idyll.17 Adam Thorpe would pick up on the metronome effect while
enhancing the autonomous agency of the boat, each of whose parts—assigned their technical names—performs like an instrument in a
chamber ensemble: “The square oars rang between the iron tholes, and this marked the silence like the beating of a metronome, while from
the stern the hawser would not leave off its soft little splashing as it trailed through the water.”18
A kind of “stillness of her love” also might be said to rivet Emma’s perception of Rouen, en route to an assignation with Léon:
Then on a sudden the town appeared. Sloping down like an amphitheatre, and drowned in the fog, it widened out beyond the bridges confusedly.
Then the open country spread away with a monotonous movement till it touched in the distance the vague line of the pale sky. Seen thus from
above, the whole landscape looked immovable as a picture; the anchored ships were massed in one corner, the river curved round the foot of the
green hills, and the isles, oblique in shape, lay on the water, like large motionless, black fishes … Sometimes a gust of wind drove the clouds
towards the Saint-Catherine hills, like aerial waves that broke silently against a cliff.
A giddiness seemed to her to detach itself from this mass of existence, and her heart swelled as if the hundred and twenty thousand souls that
palpitated there had all at once sent into it the vapour of the passions she fancied theirs. Her love grew in the presence of this vastness, and
expanded with tumult to the vague murmurings that rose towards her. She poured it out upon the square, on the walks, on the streets, and the
old Norman city outspread before her eyes as an enormous capital, as a Babylon into which she was entering.19
The de Man version reads:
Then all at once, the city came into sight.
Sloping down like an amphitheatre, and drowned in the fog, it overflowed unevenly beyond its bridges. Then the open country mounted again
in a monotonous sweep until it touched in the distance the elusive line of the pale sky. Seen thus from above, the whole landscape seemed
frozen, like a picture; the anchored ships were massed in one corner, the river curved around the foot of the green hills, and the oblong islands
looked like giant fishes lying motionless on the water … From time to time a gust of wind would drive the clouds towards the slopes of Saint
Catherine, like aerial waves breaking silently against a cliff.
Something seemed to emanate from this mass of human lives that left her dizzy; her heart swelled as though the hundred and twenty
thousand souls palpitating there had all at once wafted to her the passions with which her imagination had endowed them. Her love grew in the
presence of this vastness, and filled with the tumult of the vague murmuring which rose from below. She poured it out, onto the squares, the
avenues, the streets; and the old Norman city spread out before her like some incredible capital, a Babylon into which she was about to enter.
Where the boat idyll had played on sound effects, here stillness was hitched to a certain frozenness of painted landscape, its referents
seemingly culled from Romantic pastoral poetry (Wordsworth’s Prelude) and Dutch or British landscape painting. In Lydia Davis’s 2010
translation, Flaubert’s “immobility” grows into the idea that a painting is simply a landscape that does not move: “The entire landscape had
the stillness of a painting.” By way of contrast, Thorpe writes, “The landscape had the motionless look of a painting.” He then uses “still”
adjectivally as if to underscore the effect of motion coming to a standstill: “and the islands, oblong-shaped, looked like great black fish stilled
on the water.” Flaubert’s reference to “anchored ships” is a possible pastiche of Coleridge’s Rime of the Ancient Mariner: “As idle as a
painted ship upon a painted ocean,” and draws attention to the deathly immobility of ships marooned in still waters, of islands walled off from
the worldly fray, of dead fish floating on the surface of the sea. Eleanor Marx’s diction emphasizes the logic of analogy: hills are to isles as
isles are to fish. In de Man’s rendering, the geometry is more precise (“oblong” is Flaubert’s term, not “oblique”), but the syntactically choppy
way in which Eleanor’s islands “lay on the water, like large, motionless black fishes” seems true to the painted-on feeling of Flaubert’s

“semblaient sur l’eau de grands poissons noirs arrêtés.” Flaubert’s homophonic play between arête (meaning “fishbone”) and arrêté aurally
complements the visual “arrest” created by that daub of black. It is odd that de Man’s rendering—they “looked like giant fishes lying
motionless on the water”—is colorless, neutral. In the place of color we have an awkward alliterative construct—“looked like … lying”—which
bizarrely recalls de Man’s commentary on euphony in a chapter of Blindness and Insight called “Literature and Language.” Referring to
Roman Jakobson’s analysis of the slogan “I like Ike,” de Man zeroes in on the way euphony seeds confusion between literal and figural
representation, drawing attention to the “infinitely deceiving epistemology of representation” and to “the innumerable ways in which it is
possible to confuse images and things.” De Man is trying to identify what ascribes literarity to literature, the quantum in which the figural
status of language resides. But he soon comes up against the problem of sliding degrees of figuralism, the impossibility of clearly
distinguishing tropes from figures, metaphor from metonymy. Figural language is captured by an “apt water-metaphor,” he argues, quoting
Heinrich Lausberg to the effect that “the transition (of one figure to another, in this case, from metaphor to metonymy) is fluid.” “Rhetoric,” de
Man concludes in a familiar turn of argument, “implies the persistent threat of misreading.”20 Perhaps the encrypted “looked like lying”—
placed uncannily in a painted scene of watery stillness—may be read as a caveat against the desire to make language, or life, stand still.21
Such a reading calls for a full reckoning of the inertia of mass crucial to the representation of the death-drive, which is depicted as the
heaviness of embodiment emphasized by Flaubert’s image of “les existences amassés,” and rendered existentially by Eleanor Marx’s
“mass of existence.” Marx’s turn of phrase rings true to the famous line “Elle n’existait plus,” by which Flaubert would mark Emma’s passing,
or to the portrait of dead Emma covered by a mortuary sheet: “Le drap se creusait depuis ses seins jusqu’à ses genoux, se relevant ensuite
à la pointe des orteils; et il semblait à Charles que des masses infinies, qu’un poids énorme pesait sur elle.” (“The sheet sunk in from her
breast to her knees, and then rose at the tips of her toes, and it seemed to Charles that infinite masses, an enormous load, were weighing
upon her.”) The weight of “infinite masses, an enormous load” foreshadows the immense heaviness of Emma’s coffin, transferring the pull of
gravity to the measured exhaustion of the pallbearers: “The tired bearers walked more slowly, and it advanced with constant jerks, like a boat
that pitches with every wave.” “Weariness,” for Roland Barthes, a key term of the Neutral, relates lassitude (lassitudo, fatigatio) to labor
(labo, “to fall,” “painful rural work”); it enters a sequence including mental exhaustion, chronic fatigue, depression, and the “work” of
mourning.22 This state of tropological standstill inheres in the cliché—itself an operator of dead or copied speech.
Like the cliché, Marx’s translation stands as a plea for unoriginal textuality. Deauthored, neutered, deowned—“suicided,” if you will—the
translation, like the Flaubertian cliché, is cast as an unusual form of literary expression that, counter to Romantic values and myths of avant-
garde originality, flaunts its derivativeness and proudly wears the lead weight of predication on literary antecedent. In what is perhaps a
singular case of literature as authorized plagiarism or legalized appropriation, the translation reproduces the original with as much exactitude
as possible, with no risk of copyright infringement or allegations of forgery. It is granted this extraordinary license because it claims to be of
the original—that is to say, possessed of no autonomous textual identity. Critically framed in these terms, Eleanor Marx’s biography is no
longer reducible to the plot of her mistreatment at the hands of a dastardly partner. The focus shifts instead to “Marx, the translation,” and its
status in textual history.
As has been often noted, Gustave Flaubert and Karl Marx (whose respective dates of birth, 1818 and 1821, placed them in a common
chronotope) shared a landscape dominated by the long historical shadow of 1848, the society of bourgeois sham and speculation that
asserted its ascendancy under the Second Empire of Napoleon III, the commercialization of discourse that resulted from Napoleon’s
manipulation of the mass press for propaganda purposes, and the glitter and shine of the commodity arcade that cut its swath through
midcentury Europe.23 February 1848 was the month in which revolution erupted in Paris, a mere two weeks after Marx and Engels published
their Communist Manifesto, establishing it, to borrow Martin Puchner’s words, as a text “arising from the revolution even as it sought to
trigger the revolution.” Though a study of “provincial manners” far-flung from the political agitations that would sweep mid-century Europe,
Madame Bovary superimposes a post-1848 sensibility onto a pre-1848 historical setting, registered most palpably in Emma’s dislocated
idealism or the ideological grain of Homais’s morally compromised progressivism. The way in which Marx charted how the revolution
telescoped epochal shifts created a sense of time out of joint, and disturbed the temporal consistency of the present that has struck many
critics as compatible with a Flaubertian sense of the aesthetic laws of parody and the ironies of historical repetition. In the history of their
reception, Madame Bovary and Das Kapital also line up as texts that fit no single genre: the former outraged readers by introducing unfixed
narrative perspective (style indirect libre) and a psychological realism clouded by authorial irony, while the latter taxed its audience by
confronting them with what Isaiah Berlin referred to as “a curious amalgam of economic theory, history, sociology and propaganda which fits
none of the accepted categories.”24
It is not hard to imagine how Eleanor Marx might have filtered Flaubert’s novel through the lens of Marxism. George Steiner has argued
that
George Moore had been instrumental in the project, but Eleanor Marx was principally inspired by what she took to be the radical posture of
Flaubert’s book. Here was a statement of the condition of women under the suffocating regime of bourgeois hypocrisy and mercantile ideals …
The book had been prosecuted for obscenity in the courts of Napoleon III. Eleanor Marx saw in this prosecution a nakedly political attempt to
silence an artist who, by sheer honesty of vision, had laid bare the cant and corruption of life in the Second Empire. Thus the translator brought
to her task an explicit programmatic “set.” She approached the text almost entirely via context, via what she felt to be a shared sphere of moral-
political intention.25
An interest in commodity fetishism seems to have been part of the “programmatic ‘set’” that Eleanor Marx brought to bear on the text.
Flaubert arguably wrote his way, through the art of the detail, into what would become Karl Marx’s theory of commodity fetishism. In the
passage describing the porcelain sheen and glossy veneer calqued onto the gentlemen’s faces and bodies at the Vaubyessard Ball, there
would seem to be an intimation of Marx’s celebrated formula of fetishism whereby social relations between men have been displaced by “the
fantastic form of a relation between things”:
Their clothes, better made, seemed of finer cloth, and their hair, brought forward in curls towards the temples, glossy with more delicate pomades
[the French “lustrés par des pommades” accentuates the gleam of light]. They had the complexion of wealth—that clear complexion that is
heightened by the pallor of porcelain, the shimmer of satin, the veneer of old furniture [“ils avaient le teint de la richesse, ce teint blanc que
rehaussent la pâleur des porcelaines, les moires du satin, le vernis des beaux meubles”], and that an ordered regimen of exquisite nurture
maintains at its best.
Eleanor Marx’s consistent rendering of riches as wealth enhances the latent critique of wealth in Flaubert, and it is interesting in this regard to
note that Lydia Davis, in her strong translation, preserves “the complexion of wealth,” as does Adam Thorpe. Throughout Madame Bovary,
the desire for wealth is contracted like an incurable disease, as when the satin shoes stored by Emma after the ball exceed the function of
fetishistic memento mori to become objects of wealth-transference: “Her heart was like these. In its friction against wealth something had
come over it that could not be effaced.” Alan Russell used “riches” in his 1952 Penguin translation: “Her heart was like that. Contact with
riches had left upon it a coating that would never wear off.”26 In preferring the term wealth to riches, it was as if Eleanor Marx were intent on
not letting Anglophone readers forget that luxury items—clothing, jewels, aristocratic refinements—were dressed-up versions of money,
hardened into congealed capital.27 Flaubert’s luxury passages, in the hands of Eleanor Marx, suddenly appear like a gift to Karl Marx,
providing grist for his theory.

It is of course a stretch to cast Flaubert as a Marxist. A member of the rentier class who prided himself on just how uneconomic his
writing was, Flaubert’s depiction of wage-labor in Madame Bovary was confined to the personage of Berthe, Madame Bovary’s hapless
daughter, consigned at the novel’s end to factory work. (“It was an aunt who took charge of her. She is poor, and sends her to a cotton-mill to
earn a living.”)28 Compared to Zola, the Goncourt brothers, or early Huysmans—each of whom documented the exigencies of wage-labor,
piece work, domestic work, and sex work in the diverse arenas of the slaughterhouse, the department store, the laundry, the home, or the
brothel—Flaubert rates as a pale chronicler of the workingwoman’s everyday.29 At the Vaubyessard Ball, for example, servants are treated
as utility figures, allowed to assist with narrative action and occupy character-space but scarcely dignified with names or dialogue. (A
steward resplendent in knee breeches and white cravat is flattened and objectified when glimpsed bobbing amid a sea of platters while
another servant, similarly attenuated, shouts the names of dishes into the Duc de Laverdière’s ear.) Characterologically speaking, Flaubert
assigned space for such liveried domestics on the narrative subfloor of minor characters.30
No Marxist then, in his class sympathies, Flaubert nonetheless qualifies as a Marxist of sorts insofar as he can be seen as an intellectual
worker or laborer of the “real” of realism. To read Flaubert this way is to home in on the material origins of his images, and the importance of
the art of illustration to his literary techne. In Madame Bovary, Flaubert modernized the classical procedures of ut pictura poesis
(ponderously deployed later on in the overly historicist Salammbô [1862]) by linking them to modern media and the condition of mechanical
reproducibility. His use of caricature, satirical illustrations, maps, portraits, fashion plates and optical contrivances (spectacles, lorgnons,
microscopes) as devices for constructing visual set pieces lent historical density and phantasmagorical surplus-value to commodity
spectacle. This effect is especially striking in the novel’s attention to fashion, brought out in the Claude Chabrol film version with its exquisite
reconstruction of period ensembles and colors coordinated to Emma’s moods. Margaret Cohen’s 2004 edition (which, in addition to
preserving the revised Marx translation is heavy on fashion plates) underscores the importance of sartorial templates to Flaubert’s realism.
They include: an Amazon in a riding costume similar to the one that Madame Bovary acquires when she arranges riding excursions with
Rodolphe; two stevedore costumes that reference those donned by Emma when she attends a masked ball in Rouen; two Devéria prints
from an 1840 collection called Les Heures de la Parisienne showing what a fashionable woman wears while writing letters or while asleep
on the divan after a night of reveling; and a plate of two women in ball gowns from an 1846 German edition of La Mode that Cohen matches
up with outfits worn by Emma and other ladies at the Vaubyessard Ball: “She wore a gown of pale saffron trimmed with bouquets of pompom
roses mixed with green … Lace trimmings, diamond brooches, medallion bracelets, trembled on blouses, gleamed on breasts, clinked on
bare arms.”31 Cohen’s illustration-embellished edition prompts further reflection on the labor of literary visualization.
Flaubert’s transmogrification of the object could also be said to prefigure what Marx would do in Capital with a table or a coat in his
theories of exchange-value and commodity fetishism (his own overcoat went in and out of pawn throughout the 1850s and ’60s, determining
whether he would be allowed into the British Library). As Peter Stallybrass observes:
In Capital, Marx wrote about a coat as a commodity—as the abstract “cell-form” of capitalism. He traced the value of that cell-form to the
appropriated body of alienated labor. In the process of production, he argued, the commodity takes on an exotic life, even as the body of the
worker is reduced to an abstraction.32
Though Stallybrass wants to insist on the fact that Marx’s coat, like the coats of most workers, “were anything but abstractions,” he
acknowledges the power of Marx’s dematerialization of things in his idea of the commodity fetish. “As a commodity, the coat achieves its
destiny as an equivalence: as 20 yards of linen, 10 lbs of tea, 40 lbs of coffee.” Marx’s commodity fetish, in Stallybrass’s ascription,
“inscribes immateriality as the defining feature of capitalism.”33 One could say that this immaterialized materialism also defines Flaubertian
realism, which emerges as a “Marxist” genre in its obsession with the mystificatory capabilities of objects. This Marxist Flaubert is only
underscored by the addition of Eleanor Marx’s signature to the novel’s English translation.
The Marx-Flaubert-Marx trifecta, crucial to the “Englishing” of French realism’s language of class, luxury, and commodity fetishism, may have
also contributed to the idea of the “work” of art grounded in man-hours, productive outcomes, and the forms assumed by intellectual labor.
Arguably, there is a discernible quilting point where Flaubert, the self-designated “homme-plume,” who left two thousand pages of polished
writing and over twenty thousand pages of notes and drafts, joins Eleanor Marx, the worker bee of translation, whose corpus went beyond
Flaubert’s Madame Bovary, Marx’s Capital, and Prosper-Olivier Lissagaray’s History of the Paris Commune of 1871 to include Nikolaus
Delius’s The Epic Elements in Shakespeare’s Dramas and Ibsen’s plays.34 One could even say that Eleanor’s concept of “conscientious
translation,” expounded in her preface to the first-print runs of her translation, would not have been possible without the example of Flaubert’s
labored prose. Eleanor wrote:
There are three possible methods of translation. The first is that of the genius, who, literally re-creates a work in his own language. Schlegel has
done this for Shakespeare, Baudelaire for Edgar Poe. But there are few geniuses in the world, and those we have do not, for the most part,
devote themselves to the thankless task of translating. Next, there is the hack translator, who, armed with dictionary, rushes in where his betters
fear to tread. He it is who has earned for the translator the epithet of tradittore, and his work is but too often a perversion, not a rendering. Finally
there is the conscientious worker. He cannot if he would belong to the first category of translators. He would not if he could belong to the second.
He can but strive to do his best; to be honest, earnest. To this last category I claim to belong. Certainly no critic can be more painfully aware than
I am of the weaknesses, shortcomings, the failures of my work; but at least the translation is faithful. I have neither suppressed nor added a line, a
word … My work, then, I know is faulty. It is pale and feeble by the side of its original. Yet, if it induces some readers to go to that original, if it
helps to make known to those who cannot thus study this work of the greatest of French novelists after Balzac, I am content. At any rate, I cannot
believe James Thomson was right when he prophesied that “if any one dared to publish an English version [of Madame Bovary], he and it would
be overwhelmed with execrations.” But whether thus “overwhelmed” or not, I do not regret having done this work; it is the best I could do.35
While Eleanor reveals a curious lack of solidarity with the hack translator who is arguably the real proletariat of translation, her self-
identification as a translator of the worker stripe was surely not unrelated to the fact that she herself translated for money and saw financial
independence as the key to female emancipation from property marriage. The modesty of the translation’s textual ideal seems here to
conform to the personal modesty of a woman whose literary talents were substantial but whose personal claim to the literary vocation was
adjusted to the “class” of the writer manqué—that is to say, of the translator. And yet, rather than stop at an interpretation that reinforces the
idea of the translator as a second-class citizen, or pays tribute to the feminist translator as redeemer of devalorized forms of work, I am more
interested in extracting from Eleanor Marx’s preface and practice of translation a singular model of unalienated literary labor.
As S. S. Prawer reminds us, literary labor fell into a peculiar category in Marxist thought. Literature “was valuable to Marx not least
because it resisted crude ‘utility’—and ‘consumption’—thinking.” There is, he continues,
something distinctly odd about Marx’s humorous use of the labels “productive” and “unproductive” in a literary context. If the distinguishing mark
of “productive labour” is that it serves to increase capital and bring profit to the owners of capital, then the fact that Milton only received 5 pounds
for Paradise Lost proves no more than that he was himself no capitalist … But the general point Marx is making is clear enough. The true poet’s
labour could remain—in Milton’s time at least—unalienated to the extent that he took no account of market value. Such a poet writes what he has
to, from the center of his being, and leaves it to others to convert his poem into a profit-bearing commodity. He thus prefigures that “realm of

freedom” which has inspired the most eloquent and justly famous passage of Capital III.
Where Prawer focuses on how literature serves Marx as a model of unalienated labor, Werner Hamacher sees capitalism itself leading, in
Marx’s scheme, to the “end of labor, more precisely, of forced and commodity-producing labor.” Hamacher also homes in on the “famous
passage” of chapter 48 of the third volume of Capital in which Marx wrote:
In fact the realm of freedom actually begins only where labor which is determined by necessity and mundane considerations ceases; thus, in the
very nature of things, it lies beyond the sphere of actual material production … Beyond it [the realm of necessity] begins that development of
human energy which is an end in itself, the true realm of freedom, which, however, can blossom forth only with the realm of necessity as its basis.
The shortening of the work day is its basic prerequisite.
For Hamacher, what Marx promises here is “not so much the liberation from labor as the liberation to it.” The end of labor is structurally
registered in a whole new language: “a language other than commodity-language … that speaks beyond—but also in—all ‘objective thought-
forms’ of commodity-language, opening up its syntactic arrangement and its meaning to something else beyond any conceivable form.”36
Literary or intellectual labor, identified respectively by Prawer and Hamacher as the place where Marx seeded an idea of unalienated
labor—and from there, perhaps, the foundational principle of an “International of letters”—provides a different optic on the well-worn
canonical reading of Flaubert as laborer of the mot juste. Frances Ferguson reminds us that the symbolic capital of Flaubert’s work ethic
was drawn on by his lawyer Jules Senard to bolster his character defense at the obscenity trial, and that the acquittal was taken by Flaubert
as a validation of his commitment to literary technique (“technique,” says Ferguson, “had won the privilege of the technical”). This profile of
Flaubert as master technician informs Gilles Philippe’s study of the grammaticalization of French literature between 1890 and 1940, a
phenomenon attributed in large part to the enshrining of the Flaubertian “worked” phrase in literary pedagogy. Philippe Hamon had already
established the terms for analyzing Flaubert’s art of description (sometimes qualified as ekphrasis) as labor-intensive stylistics, contending
in his Introduction à l’analyse du descriptif that description
is the site where work is manifest in three ways: as the application of a professional lexicon (used say, by an architect to describe a house), as
work on the lexicon itself (ostentatious use of stylistic savoir-faire, rarified epithets, descriptive metaphors), and as site of generic study of the
world, of knowledge, of classification, of Mathesis.37
Julian Barnes, reviewing Pierre-Marc de Biasi’s critical edition of Flaubert’sC arnets de travail expatiates on the labor of research:
“Research for Flaubert is not preliminary but central; not a matter of ‘checking’ but part of the writing process.”38
The research model of writing, what Barnes dubs “conscientious literature,” puts translation theory into dialogue with the French field of
manuscript scholarship known as la critique génétique in which the final published version is effectively unspooled in an archival field of
drafts and sketches.39 Such an approach is especially suited to authors like Flaubert or Hölderlin, the latter notorious for never leaving a final
version of his poems untouched. As J. M. Coetzee has recently noted:
In his last productive years Hölderlin seems to have abandoned the notion of the definitive and to have regarded each seemingly completed
poem as merely a stopping place, a base from which to conduct further raids into the unsaid. Hence his habit of breaking open a perfectly good
poem, not in order to improve it but to rebuild it from the ground up. In such a case, which is the definitive text, which the variant, particularly when
the rebuilding is broken off and not resumed? Are apparently unfinished reworkings to be regarded as abandoned projects, or might Hölderlin
have been feeling his way toward a new aesthetics of the fragmentary, and an accompanying poetic epistemology of the flashing insight or
vision?40
Provisionality rather than finish becomes the norm in this “new aesthetics of the fragmentary and accompanying poetic epistemology”; in
Hölderlin’s case practiced by himself as well as by the généticiens who labored posthumously to determine a definitive edition.
Translation, like genetic criticism, effects a subtle generic shift in how we view the literary text. The work becomes something on the order
of what I would call l’oeuvre oeuvrée, the worked and working text. No longer viewed as a stable object owned by a single author, it emerges
as a site of translational or editorial labor. Though génétique can have the effect of enhancing the patrimonial prestige of a national author,
its working method of scholarly teams dedicated to transcription, collation, and chartist authentification passes the singular author to the
collective interpretive community, attenuating the writer’s exclusive literary title. Jacques Derrida characterized this singular/plural textual
identity as “the genius of the genius” that “enjoins us to think how an absolute singularity subtracts itself from the community of the common,
from the generality or the genericness of the genre and thus from the shareable.”41 Derrida grounds his insistence on the “genericity of all
genre” in a way that makes it compatible with the pragmatism of literary genetics, which, by dissolving the unitary integral into a textual
commons of epitexts and variants, creates a kind of generic literary field. Jean-Michel Rabaté, in conversation with Derrida, ascribes this
genericity to the functionalist archive such that manuscripts, made subject to the analytic methods of informatique and classified as research
objects—are effectively “ITEM-ized” (ITEM being the initials forL ’Institut des textes et manuscrits modernes [the Institute of Modern Texts
and Manuscripts]).42 Mediated by unseen editorial hands, the text bears the imprimatur of anonymous signatories whose alterity redounds
against the unregistered identities of the work’s all-inclusive readership. Translation is similar in this respect: the translator remains below
the radar interceding as unobtrusively as possible. And like génétique, translation operates in the domain of literary techne, working through
linguistic medium and milieu.
The terms “medium” and “milieu” are paired by Antoine Berman to approximate what Walter Benjamin intended by “task” in his famous
1923 preface to his translation of Baudelaire’s Tableaux Parisiens titled Die Aufgabe des Ubersetzers. Berman suggests that for Benjamin
language was a medium comprised of differential densities, while translation implied the act of moving among zones of greater or lesser
density.43 Berman offers the interesting observation that Benjamin’s essay “The Task of the Translator” should by rights be titled “the task of
translation” given that Benjamin says virtually nothing about the translator in the essay. Traditionally, Berman argues,
translators’ prologues—even if they seek to generalize—speak of the translation being introduced. It’s the law of the genre: think of Humboldt’s
preface to Aeschylus’s Agamemnon, of Stefan George’s to Les Fleurs du Mal, of Bonnefoy’s to Hamlet, of Meschonnic’s to the Bible. Whenever a
translator prefaces his work, he always speaks of his translation, or at least of the work he has translated. Nothing of the sort with Benjamin. This
is too unusual not to be deliberate and indicates Benjamin’s intent to produce an alternative kind of discourse on translation.44
If a Benjaminian parenthesis on the task of translation has been opened at this juncture, it is because Benjamin’s notion of task provides a
critical framework for analyzing the conscientiousness of Flaubert’s text and Eleanor Marx’s translation in terms of literary technics. Of
special interest in this regard is Benjamin’s understanding of translation failure. A profound sense of technical failure informs his theory of
translation and grounds his notion of Aufgabe. According to Berman, Benjamin lacked the “translation drive” (pulsion-de-traduire) essential
to the “true” translator. Translation did not come easily to him, and he reserved harsh judgment for his own translation of Baudelaire, which
initially he hoped would rival that of Stefan George. In his own estimation, his Baudelaire translations fell woefully short of metric ingenuity or
a style equivalent to Baudelaire’s aesthetically fascinating “baroque of banality.” In a letter to Hugo von Hofmannsthal cited by Berman,
Benjamin wrote:
I think I have come to understand that every translation that is not undertaken for the highest and most urgent practical goals (like Biblical

translation, as a prime example) or for the sake of purely philological research must have something absurd about it.
As Berman surmises, as soon as Benjamin engaged in the actual work of translation, a hiatus opened up between translation theory and the
practice of translation:
As soon as we posit translation as involving work, say, on the signifier, a gap opens between the discourse on translation and the experience of
it. From this point on, this discourse can neither preside over translation nor fully analyze it. One can certainly formulate principles that might
govern the translation of the “letter,” but these principles will never achieve the status of a methodology. Between such principles and the act of
translation, there emerges an obscure space where the subjectivity and the unconscious of the translator intervene … The discourse on
translation can never rejoin the actuality of translation.45
The worst translation experience for Benjamin occurred when he took on Proust, a project begun in 1926 in collaboration with his friend
Franz Hessel. Only the first two volumes ever saw the light of day: A l’ombre des jeunes filles en fleurs and Le côté de Guermantes. The
completed manuscript of Sodome et Gomorrhe was lost and the partially completed text of La Prisonnière abandoned in midstream. To
Gershom Scholem, Benjamin confessed that the Proust work was killing him. The only way to redeem the pain was to use the difficulties he
was encountering with the Proust translation as the basis for a projected article titled “On Translating Proust.” The article was never written,
and together with the missing translation of Sodome et Gomorrhe, it forms a monument to the impossible task of translation; or, alternately,
to a theory of translation as task. Though the slide from labor to task, and from task to translation, may be not entirely true to the spirit of
Benjamin’s and Berman’s efforts to “demetaphorize” translation (Richard Sieburth, himself their common translator, maintains that Benjamin
“tropes translation away from its etymological roots in metaphor and into metonymy”), I would insist that metaphoric and metonymic
modalities are in fact combined in Berman’s adumbration of the Benjaminian concept of task. Mining the shared connotations of Aufgabe
and Auflösung, the latter a favored term of German romanticism whose cognate is “solution,” Berman offers a rich rereading of “task” as:
•the logical solution (of a problem)
•the chemical (dis)solution (of a substance)
•the mathematical (re)solution (of an equation)
•the musical (re)solution (of a chord)
And, as a solvent of the foreign, as in Novalis’s “Poetry dissolves (auflöst) the foreign in its native essence,”46 Berman’s concept of
translation as trial, or test, or experience of the foreign (l’épreuve de l’étranger) implies taxing language with the search for a solution to
dissonance. The Aufgabe as Auflösung involves laboring the native tongue while assuming the risk of capitulation (aufgeben) to the
stranger.
Berman’s desire to underwrite Benjamin’s translation practicum by glossing the concept of task ends up placing renewed emphasis on
intellectual labor as a literary technics. And it is this paradigm of textual critique—relevant to translation theory and génétique alike—that can
be adduced from Eleanor Marx’s labor theory of translation and Paul/Patricia de Man’s revisions to her translation ofM adame Bovary. The
task of translation, in these terms, involves working on solutions to linguistic Untranslatables, while mining the text’s fluctuating mobile status
as material and editorial object.
“Marx’s Bovary”—that is, the “Marxist” Bovary encrypted in the English translation by Karl Marx’s daughter—affords a glimpse of a
language of labor released from a transcendental, capitalist logic of equivalence, exchange, project and credit. Such a language, in Werner
Hamacher’s view, is what Derrida was after in Specters of Marx. Derrida’s idea of “hauntology,” Hamacher argues, implies
that language does not belong to the system of capital, nor to that of labor, that language does not define itself as commodity-language; that it
only assumes the character of productive or reproductive labor when the equivalence form is generalized and has repressed the credit-character
of capital as well as the project-character of labor.47
A non-capitalized theory of language, an unalienated form of labor, a form of literature (translation) released from the burden of possession
by a single authorial signature, and the conscription of these objectives for a new “international of letters” (or refurbished Weltliteratur) that
would sustain the internationalism of The Communist Manifesto—these immense stakes of aesthetic politics are thus coaxed out of
Eleanor Marx’s reflections on the task of the conscientious translator.48 It is no small irony that this labor theory of translation—a kind of last
will and testament tucked into an ephemeral preface to Flaubert’s masterpiece—was lost to literary history even as the translation itself was
reborn in the most recent Norton edition.

19 Flaubert, Madame Bovary, trans. Marx Aveling, 289–90; (original French text: “Puis, d’un seul coup d’oeil, la ville apparaissait. Descendant tout en amphithéâtre
et noyée dans le brouillard, elle s’élargissait au-delà des ponts, confusément. La pleine campagne remontait ensuite d’un mouvement monotone, jusqu’à toucher au
loin la base indécise du ciel pâle. Ainsi vu d’en haut, le paysage tout entier avait l’air immobile comme une peinture; les navires à l’ancre se tassaient dans un coin; le
fleuve arrondissait sa courbe au pied des collines vertes, et des îles, de forme oblongue, semblaient sur l’eau de grands poissons noirs arrêtés … Parfois un coup de
vent emportait les nuages vers la côte Sainte-Catherine, comme des flots aériens qui se brisaient en silence contre une falaise. Quelque chose de vertigineux se
dégageait pour elle de ces existences amassées, et son coeur s’en gonflait abondamment, comme si les cent vingt mille âmes qui palpitaient là eussent envoyé
toutes à la fois la vapeur des passions qu’elle leur supposait. Son amour s’agrandissait devant l’espace, et s’emplissait de tumulte aux bourdonnements vagues qui
montaient. Elle le reversait au-dehors, sur les places, sur les promenades, sur les rues, et la vieille cité normande s’étalait à ses yeux comme une capitale
démesurée, comme une Babylone où elle entrait.” Flaubert, Madame Bovary, Garnier-Flammarion, 335–6).
20 Flaubert, Madame Bovary, trans. de Man, 207; Flaubert, Madame Bovary, trans. Davis, 233; Flaubert, Madame Bovary, trans. Thorpe, 251; Paul de Man,
“Literature and Language: A Commentary,” included as appendix B in Blindness and Insight, Minneapolis: University of Minnesota Press, 1983, 283; De Man,
“Literature and Language,” 284, 285.
21 The phrase “Seen thus from above, the whole landscape seemed frozen, like a picture; the anchored ships were massed in one corner, the river curved around
the foot of the green hills, and the oblong islands looked like giant fishes lying motionless on the water” unfurls as a memory-trace leading to illustrated tales read in
childhood of shipwrecks and buccaneers. In a letter written to Louise Colet in March of 1852 describing his arduous progress on Madame Bovary, Flaubert mentions
rereading these tales “for his novel” and stumbling on a cache of engravings that he had colored as a child of seven or eight: “There are rocks painted blue and trees
painted green. At the sight of some of them (for instance a scene showing people stranded on ice floes) I re-experienced feelings of terror that I had as a child. I should
like something that would put it out of my mind; I am almost afraid to go to bed. There is a story of Dutch sailors in ice-bound waters, with bears attacking them in their
hut (this picture used to keep me awake) … My travels and my childhood memories color off from each other, fuse, whirl dazzlingly before my eyes, and rise up in a
spiral” (“Letters about Madame Bovary” in the de Man Norton edition, 302). Flaubert was certainly far from being the only nineteenth-century author to sublimate the
terrifying imprint of an engraving into literature, or, for that matter, to have fun with the arch-rivalry between visual and verbal mediums. Many experimented with virtuoso
executions of ut pitura poesis in an effort to enable literature to best the pleasures of the eye; pleasures optically delivered so easily by painting, drawing and
photography. J. K. Huysmans’s decadent prose rendition of Gustave Moreau’s Salomé paintings in À rebours stands as a classic example of such competitive écriture
artiste. But Flaubert’s use of illustration as a literary device is distinctly associated with the techne of intermedial reproducibility and as such carries interesting
implications for contemporary translation theories that focus on transcoding.
22 Madame Bovary, Garnier-Flammarion, 401, 404–5; English translation: Flaubert, Madame Bovary, trans. Marx Aveling, 361; “The weight of infinite masses”:
Flaubert, Madame Bovary, trans. Marx Aveling, 370; (“Les porteurs fatigués se ralentissaient; et elle avançait par saccades continues, comme une chaloupe qui tangue
à chaque flot.”); Madame Bovary, Garnier-Flammarion, 413; Roland Barthes, The Neutral, trans. Rosalind Krauss and Denis Hollier, New York: Columbia University
Press, 2005, 16.
23 Richard Terdiman writes, “Beyond such chronological parallels, the two [Flaubert and Marx] came together in their object, as Flaubert put it, of sinking their
civilization. They seem not to have heard of each other, at least no trace of any mutual recognition has been found. All the better: investigation of their respective
compendia of the stupidity, cupidity, and morbidity in which they felt their period was drowning them can proceed without fear that the convergences in their tactics
might have had a merely anecdotic source. On the contrary, their intertextualities signify. And their peculiar castigating humor stands as a central sign of such
signification.” Richard Terdiman, Discourse/ Counter-Discourse: The Theory and Practice of Symbolic Resistance in Nineteenth-Century France, Ithaca, NY: Cornell
University Press, 1985, 199–200. On Marx/Flaubert parallels, Edward Ahearn has written: “Parcelization of land, the persistence of the peasant class, the exploitation
of it and elements of the lower middle class through taxation and through ‘secondary forms’ of capitalist expropriation, features studied by Marx in his analyses of
events in mid-century France, are strikingly evident in Madame Bovary.” Edward Ahearn, Marx and Modern Fiction, New Haven, CT: Yale University Press, 1989, 7.
24 Martin Puchner, Poetry of the Revolution: Marx, Manifestos, and the Avant-Gardes, Princeton, NJ: Princeton University Press, 2006, 33; Karl Marx, The

25 George Steiner, After Babel: Aspects of Language and Translation, New York: Oxford University Press, 1975, 376. Steiner compares the Marx translation to
Gerard Hopkins’s 1948 translation, deemed an improvement on grounds of accuracy even if it caters to the “world classics” model by naturalizing the language.
26 Flaubert, Madame Bovary, trans. Marx Aveling, 55–6; Flaubert, Madame Bovary, trans. Davis, 44; Flaubert, Madame Bovary, trans. Thorpe, 47; “Her heart was
like these …”: Flaubert, Madame Bovary, trans. Marx Aveling, 61 (“Son coeur était comme eux: au frottement de la richesse, il s’était placé dessus quelque chose qui
