27 Eleanor Marx undoubtedly appreciated the etymological proximity of luxury to luxure (lasciviousness, sex addiction) prominent in the blasphemous scene of
extreme unction where the priest touches the crucifix to Emma’s mouth “qui avait gémi d’orgeuil et crié dans la luxure …” Flaubert, Madame Bovary, 1857, Paris:
Garnier-Flammarion, 1986, 399; Flaubert, Madame Bovary, trans. Marx Aveling; in the Marx version, we read: “that had curled with pride and cried out in lewdness”
(356); Marx translated luxure as “lewdness,” in syntactic alignment with the “‘worldly pomp’ (somptuosités terrestres) coveted by Emma’s eyes” (355).
28 “Ce fut une tante qui s’en chargea. Elle est pauvre et l’envoie pour gagner sa vie, dans une filtature de coton”, Flaubert, Madame Bovary, trans. de Man, 275.
29 In this excerpt cited by Henri Mittérand, one sees very clearly how Zola mulled over different narrative strategies for making Germinal an affecting and politically
effective novel about labor and capital: “Le roman est le soulèvement des salaries, le coup d’épaule donné à la société, qui craque un instant: en un mot la lutte du
capital et du travail. C’est là qu’est l’importance du livre. Je le veux prédisant l’avenir, posant la question qui sera la question la plus importante du XXe siècle. Donc,
pour établir cette lutte, qui est mon noeud, il faut que je montre d’une part le travail, les houilleurs dans la mine, et de l’autre le capital, la direction, le patron, enfin ce
qui est à la tête. Mais deux cas se présentent: prendrai-je un patron qui personnifie en lui-même le capital, ce qui rendrait la lutte plus directe et peut-être plus
dramatique? Ou prendrai-je une société anonyme, des actionnaires, enfin le monde de la grande industrie, la mine dirigée par un directeur appointé, avec tout un
personnel, et ayant derrière lui l’actionnaire oisif, le vrai capital?” Extracts from this preparatory dossier published in La fabrique de Germinal, ed. Colette Becker,
Paris: Sedes-PUL, 1986, 56, and cited by Henri Mittérand in Le roman à l’oeuvre: Genèse et valeurs, Paris: Presses Universitaires de France, 1998, 70.
30 Alex Woloch locates “the minor character at the junction between implied person and narrative form.” He maintains that “the minor character is always drowned
out within the totality of the narrative; and what we remember of the character is never detached from how the text, for the most part, makes us forget him … The
strange significance of minor characters, in other words, resides largely in the way that the character disappears, and in the tension or relief that results from this
vanishing.” See Woloch, The One vs. the Many: Minor Characters and the Space of the Protagonist in the Novel, Princeton, NJ: Princeton University Press, 2003, 38.
31 Flaubert, Madame Bovary, trans. de Man, 44.
32 Peter Stallybrass, “Marx’s Coat,” in Border Fetishisms: Material Objects in Unstable Spaces, ed. Patricia Spyer, London: Routledge, 202.
33 William Pietz confirms this formulation, arguing that the value of commodity fetishes derives not from their physical existence, but from their exchange-value and

masked social character. William Pietz, “Fetishism and Materialism,” in Fetishism as Cultural Discourse, eds. Emily Apter and William Pietz, Ithaca, NY: Cornell
University Press, 1993, 129.
34 See the Avant-Propos of Pierre-Marc de Biasi, in Carnets de travail de Gustave Flaubert, Cher: Balland, 1988, 9. On Eleanor’s translations of Ibsen see Sally
Ledger’s “Eleanor Marx and Henrik Ibsen,” in Eleanor Marx (1855–1898): Life, Work, Contacts, ed. John Stokes, Burlington, VT: Ashgate, 2000, 53–68.
35 Eleanor Marx Aveling, Introduction, Madame Bovary, xxi–xxii.
36 S. S. Prawer, Karl Marx and World Literature, London: Oxford University Press, 1976, 418; S. S. Prawer, Karl Marx and World Literature, 311–2; Werner
Hamacher, “Lingua Amissa: The Messianism of Commodity-Language and Derrida’s Specters of Marx,” in Ghostly Demarcations: A Symposium on Jacques Derrida’s
Specters of Marx, ed. Michael Sprinker, London: Verso, 1999, 179; ibid., 179, 180.
37 Frances Ferguson, Pornography, the Theory: What Utilitarianism Did to Action, (Chicago: University of Chicago Press, 2005), 104 and 101, respectively; Gilles
Philippe, in Sujet, verbe, complément: Le moment grammatical de la littérature française 1890–1940, Paris: Gallimard, 2002; Philippe Hamon, Introduction à l’analyse
du descriptif, Paris: Hachette, 1981, 26. The translation/paraphrase is my own.
38 Barnes writes: “Research means jotting down possibly useful names (he likes the sound of Cahours as a place; Tardival and Vaudichon as surnames; relishes a
prostitute who calls herself ‘Crucifix’). It means tramping the countryside until you find the right cliff for Bouvard and Pécuchet to be terrified (he enlists Maupassant’s
help on this quest), and the correct plateau stupide on which to site their village. It means reading enormous quantities of books, and even—if we interpret correctly a
couple of brief notes—relying on the loathed craft of photography (‘which is never what one has actually seen’…)” (242). Julian Barnes, Something to Declare: Essays
on France, New York: Alfred A. Knopf, 2002, 243–4.) “Flaubert, in his letter scorning Goncourt’s skip-hunting type of research, states very clearly that ‘I regard
technical and local details—the precise, historical side of things—as very much of secondary importance.’ If Goncourt found satisfaction in picking up a tasty word,
Flaubert found it, by contrast, ‘when I have written a page which avoids assonance and repetition.’ This ought to be clear enough, and indeed Flaubert’s denials of
realistic intent are frequent: he sought only beauty. But writers’ declarations of intent and writers’ practices don’t always match up.”
39 The bibliography on this strain of French genetic criticism is quite vast, and has been particularly rich in the field of Flaubert studies. I will restrict myself to
signaling the work of Jacques Neefs, Pierre-Marc de Biasi, Ann Herschberg Pierrot, Yann Leclerc, Raymonde Debray-Genette, and Jean Bellemin-Noël.
40 J. M. Coetzee, review of Michael Hamburger’s translation of Friedrich Hölderlin’s Poems and Fragments, in The New York Review of Books 53: 16, October
2006, 70.
41 Jacques Derrida, Geneses, Genealogies, Genres and Genius: The Secrets of the Archive, trans. Beverly Bie Brahic, New York: Columbia University Press,
2006, 1.
42 See Pourquoi la critique génétique? Méthodes, théories, ed. Pierre-Marc de Biasi et al., Paris: CNRS Editions, 1998, 197.
43 Antoine Berman, “L’Age de la traduction: ‘La tâche du traducteur de Walter Benjamin, un commentaire,” in La traduction-poésie à Antoine Berman ed. Martine
Broda, Strasbourg: Presses de l’Université de Strasbourg, 1999, 17. All English translations from this work are by Richard Sieburth. See Richard Sieburth, Antoine
Berman: The Benjamin Seminar and Other Writings on the History and Theory of Translation, ed. and trans. Richard Sieburth, unpublished manuscript.
44 Berman, “L’Age de la traduction,” 31–3.
45 Ibid., 30, 28, 30, 35.
46 Berman, “L’Âge de la traduction,” 29. See “Chronology” for 1926 in Walter Benjamin: Selected Writings, vol. 1, 1913–1926, ed. Michael W. Jennings, Cambridge,
MA: Harvard University Press, 1996, 513; Richard Sieburth, Antoine Berman; Berman, “L’Age de la traduction,” 36.
47 Hamacher, “Lingua Amissa,” 192.
48 See the chapter “The Geography of the Communist Manifesto” in Martin Puchner’s Poetry of the Revolution: Marx, Manifestos, and the Avant-Gardes, Princeton,
NJ: Princeton University Press, 2006, 47–68. According to Puchner, Marx’s conception of World Literature—an updated Goethean model emphasizing the circulation
of difference rather than sameness—is a global one in the contemporary sense. Puchner clarifies how translation functions in Marx’s work as an analogue to value
general-equivalence: “Borrowing from Das Kapital, we can say that translation or translatability provides what Marx calls the ‘form of equivalence’ (Aquivalentform),
which is the condition and form of exchangeability. World literature, in this view, sets the value of a given translation not according to its relation to the original text (use
value) but according to the market of translation (exchange). Where Goethe leaves the production of literature to the national literatures and restricts world literature to
their translation and exchange, for Marx this world of exchange and translation begins to encroach on production as well” (51). Puchner’s interpolation of Marx’s
“translatability without enslavement to capitalism” (I paraphrase Puchner’s phrase) is conditional on the Manifesto’s circulation in all the languages of the world (57).

II
What Is Yours, Ours, and Mine
Item: New York City, June 28, 2009. The streets are blaring “Thriller” and are full of people “being” Michael Jackson. What’s the ownership
stake of Michael impersonators in his image? Do Jackson impersonators who make their living by copying his dance moves and songs
(e.g., Joby Rogers or Britain’s NAVI) infringe on the star’s legal title to his self- and creative property? Are there conditions under which
impersonation qualifies under the law as a form of identity theft or celebrity plagiarism?
Item: From the New York Times, June 29, 2009: “Durango, Colo.—For the first time since territorial days, rain will be free for the catching
here … Precipitation, every last drop or flake, was assigned ownership from the moment it fell in many Western states, making scofflaws of
people who scooped rainfall from their own gutters … Who owns the sky anyway? In most of the country, that is a question for philosophy
class or bad poetry. In the West, lawyers parse it with straight faces and serious intent.”1
Item: June 2, 2009: J. D. Salinger, the reclusive ninety-year-old author of Catcher in the Rye files suit in a Manhattan federal court to stop the
publication, sale and advertisement of 60 Years Later: Coming Through the Rye, a parodic canular written by a Swedish author going by
the name J. D. California. The suit affirms that “the Sequel infringes Salinger’s copyright rights in both his novel and the character Holden
Caulfield, who is the narrator and essence of that novel.”
Each of these “items,” pulled out at random from media coverage in June of 2009, exposes the fungibility of boundaries between free
and private property, individual and government ownership, self and creative properties. As Stephen M. Best observes in his book on the
fugitive slave (cast as “equal parts pilfered property and indebted person”), there is something within slave law and intellectual property law
that is “eccentric to the law of real property.” Deferring to Aristotle’s figure of “thinking property,” Best sheds light on historical connections
between antebellum law and intellectual property law, unearthing obscured reasons for the intuitive sense that there is something inherently
wrong with “property that behaves like a person.”2 Though the items above are frivolous by comparison with the example of slavery’s legacy
in American property law—they qualify nonetheless as similarly “eccentric” cases insofar as they also examine the limits of legal theory in
dealing with ambiguities of “thinking property.”
The established and robust field of “law and literature” has a long history spanning the 1710 Statute of Anne,3 Benjamin Cardozo’s 1925
essay “Law and Literature,” and the 1971 amendment of the the Berne Convention for the Protection of Literary and Artistic Works
(unrecognized by U.S. Law), which affirms the droit moral d’artistes (moral rights of the author).4 As an academic specialization straddling
legal studies and the humanities, “literature and law” traces back to James Boyd White’s 1973 The Legal Imagination, a textbook designed
to refine understanding of the language of law, its professional rhetoric, the imagination of the lawyer, the judicial opinion and the poem, and
legalistic versus literary approaches to language control. Nourished by a triennially published journal (Literature and Law published by the
Cardozo Law School), the huge gamut of topics addressed includes inheritance law, music plagiarism, deception and art forgery, and the
relationship between property and selfhood in the work of specific authors.5 Much of this field has been shaped by “eccentric” cases that
parse the shifting, evolving, overlapping criteria applied to fair use (traditionally governed by a rule of access without permission for citations
under four hundred words); copyright (which protects exact expression, not ideas); plagiarism (the non-transformative, derivative
appropriation of “original” material); and intellectual property title (a vast catchall covering artistic work, ideas, patents, discoveries, phrases,
symbols, trade secrets, designs and trademarks).6 Cases involving the harsher treatment of satire than of parody by the courts (the former in
its dependency on the original deemed to be harmful to the market of the original; the latter judged to be more of a product in its own right)
are especially interesting not just because they demonstrate how legal scholars must perform like literary critics in the application of
hermeneutical techniques of close reading but also because they extend questions of ownership beyond authorship into matters of form,
genre and expressive medium.
In an era in which lawsuits abound involving contentions of creative property theft, plagiarism, and infringements of fair use, questions that
formerly might have seemed frivolous or peripheral to aesthetic theory are thus increasingly freighted with juridical consequence: Who owns
your life story? This question supplied the satirical conceit of a 2006 Hollywood movie Stranger than Fiction, in which the protagonist
discovers that he has become the main character in a work of fiction, subject to the author’s manipulation, and thus he is no longer in
possession of his own biographical trajectory. A similar concern was raised by an actual lawsuit brought by original cast members of the
musical A Chorus Line, convinced that producer Michael Bennett had insufficiently remunerated them for a script built on their oral histories
of childhood abuse, marital woes, and professional struggles. Should writers be held liable if they lift episodes from the lives of others (at
issue in the controversies surrounding Ian McEwan’s Atonement, alleged to have plagiarized Lucilla Andrews’s 1977 memoir No Time for
Romance, or Bryony Lavery’s play Frozen, reliant on Malcolm Gladwell’s article in the New Yorker that was in turn indebted to a
psychiatrist’s account of her work with serial killers)?7 Would literature be possible if writers were proscribed from “translating” reality-based
experiences and real-life human interest stories into fiction? This last query was answered vehemently in the negative by writers polled on the
McEwan case: Zadie Smith, Margaret Atwood, Colm Tóibín, and Kazuo Ishiguro. Can readers stake a legal claim to an author’s life story?
This question animated the controversial case of James Frey when it was revealed that he had fictionalized portions of his drug recovery
memoir, A Million Little Pieces (2003). Readers did not stop at outrage over Frey’s violation of what critic Philippe Lejeune has dubbed “the
autobiographical pact” (grounded in the presumed credibility of the auto-text and the guaranteed match of proper name to authorial subject);
they sought legal damages for consumer fraud. Class action suits in California, Washington and Illinois were brought against Random House
alleging breach of contract, and in 2007 a settlement was reached whereby refunds would be paid to any reader who had purchased the
book prior to Frey’s disclosure. In this instance, well-rehearsed debates over acceptable quotients of literary fabrication in a documentary
text, or what Lejeune calls the reciprocal “interference” of writing and reality, were upstaged by legal ones.8 The plaintiffs brought to bear on
literature the regulative logic of the “lemon law” stipulating that the reader should be protected against specious life stories much like the
buyer of a faulty car.9

The proliferation of lawsuits by individuals suing writers for their stories, or readers suing writers for marketing fake memoirs, has been
complemented by class-action suits staking claims to collective culture (as in a case filed retroactively after China introduced new copyright
laws in 1991, brought by a Chinese national minority community against a filmmaker who had used their folktales in a popular 1961 film).10
The new litigiousness of individuals and groups, and the extension of copyright restrictions in an era of heightened media access to text and
image, compounds the need for new parameters of fair use.11 And as this agenda presses, the legal field of intellectual property law comes
ever closer to poststructuralist theory, with its long history of engagement with aesthetic appropriation, plagiarism, pseudo-translation,
copyright, signature, and authorial possession.12
Translation offers a particularly rich focus for discussions of creative property and the limits of ownership because it is a peculiar genre; one
that, counter to Romantic values and myths of avant-garde originality, exalts the art of the copy, flaunts its derivativeness, and proudly bears
the lead weight of predication on literary antecedent.13 In what is perhaps a unique case of art as authorized plagiarism or legal
appropriationism, the translation is encouraged to pilfer the original with no risk of copyright infringement or allegations of forgery. It is
granted this license because it implicitly claims to be of the original; that is to say, possessed of no autonomous textual identity. Translation
thus challenges legalistic norms of ownable intellectual property. The terms in which Judith Butler has interrogated gender possession are
applicable here:
What I call my “own” gender, [she writes,] appears perhaps at times as something that I author or, indeed, own. But the terms that make up one’s
own gender are, from the start, outside oneself, beyond oneself in a sociality that has no single author (and that radically contests the notion of
authorship itself).14
Implicit in Butler’s language is the detachability of gendered properties from possessive persons, a condition that throws the whole
assumption that individual persons can “have” sexual identities into arrears. Similarly, translation throws into arrears the whole idea that
authors of “originals” are the sole owners of their literary property.
Authorial ownership, even if it is considered without the benefit of Butler’s heuristic, is a complex problem that has been approached in
multiple ways by historians of book and print culture. Robert Darnton (The Business of Enlightenment), Mark Rose (Authors and Owners),
Meredith McGill (American Literature and the Culture of Reprinting), Jody Greene (The Trouble with Ownership), and Adrian Johns (Piracy:
The Intellectual Property Wars from Gutenberg to Gates) are among those who have brought renewed focus to the shifting legal
determinations of literary property, emphasizing how in Britain and France it was often publishers or booksellers rather than authors who
were held liable in cases involving literary censorship.15 The fact that Flaubert, and not La Revue de Paris (which had published Madame
Bovary in serial form in 1856), stood trial for obscenity is significant in this regard. The trial became so integral to the novel’s reception that
most editions included its transcript. In these terms, to own a text is to own up to it under fire, to assume liability for it under adverse legal
circumstances. But what about a translation? Where does it sit legally between publisher and author(s)? Who lays ultimate authorial claim to
it? As Jody Greene notes in The Trouble with Ownership: Literary Property and Authorial Liability in England, 1660–1730, the watershed
case in England over author/publisher liability involved, interestingly enough, a translation. In Burnet v. Chetwood, the estate of the author
George Burnet (author of a work on natural history and theology titled Archaeologiae Philosophicae that was published in Latin in 1692)
filed for an injunction against William Chetwood, a representative of a group of English publishers who wanted to publish an English
translation of the work. Greene observes:
The first case brought on behalf of an author under the Copyright Act thus involved the difficult question of whether a translation constitutes a
new work or simply a version of an existing one.
The booksellers argued in court that a translation “may be called a different book, and the translator may be said to be the author.” On this
textual argument, they grounded their case that the injunction against them should not be granted. The presiding judge, the Lord Chancellor,
Lord Macclesfield, concurred with them, arguing that “a translation might not be the same with the reprinting of the original, on account that the
translator has bestowed his care and pains upon it.” Macclesfield thus made the Lockean argument that the translators had mixed their labor with
the text in question and thus could be said at least in some degree to be the new work’s proprietors.16
By this Lockean principle, Eleanor Marx (daughter of Karl Marx and the first recognized English translator ofM adame Bovary in 1886,
discussed in the previous chapter), having mixed her labor with the text of Madame Bovary, becomes in effect the proprietor of the text’s
English translation; itself accorded the copyright status of a new or separate work.17 But to make this argument is to fly in the face of Eleanor
Marx’s very un-Lockean attitudes toward ownership and private property. A good Marxist, she coauthored an essay titled The Woman
Question (1886) with her common-law husband Edward Aveling condemning the commercialization of kinship in capitalist society and
militating against the legal treatment of women as a form of private property. This opposition to women-as-property, and to property as the
general equivalent for transacting abiding connubial bonds, may be carried over to the Marx-Aveling joint signature, which reinforced an ideal
of deowned intellectual property in its practice of collaborative authorship. Here, the anti-Lockean, Marxist position disputes the whole idea
that textual property of any kind—authored or translated—can or should be owned.
The concept of textual de-owning has no obvious source but may be grafted from Jacques Derrida’s Specters of Marx, in which he
writes, “The living body, the ‘mine,’ ‘my property’ returns by annulling or taking back into it the phantomatic projections, the ideal
prostheses.”18 Derrida imposes a logic of subtraction and partition onto the concept of signature. Texts are thus imagined within a mass of
“readings” and tenuously attached to the supports or supplements of proper names. Mallarmé, Ponge, and Freud are “authors” for Derrida
only insofar as they are markers of endless citationality and iterability. Their names are posed as the problem of copyright itself.
For Derrida, language maintains copyright on all originals, rendering words that are fundamentally untenable by any author. Jacques
Lacan made a similar argument in his seminar on Edgar Allan Poe’s “The Purloined Letter.” As Fritz Gutbrodt has commented:
While the “Seminar” leaves little doubt that the letter, as an object, cannot be taken possession of, Lacan tries hard to present the struggle for its
possession among the figures in the narrative as the function of an imaginary desire. His question is to be addressed to both Poe as the author
of the original and Baudelaire as its translator: “To whom does a letter belong?” … In a more acute sense than perhaps any other form of
language, translation enacts a longing in language to belong to language.19
Here, translation is defined as the disavowal of linguistic dispossession.20 As with plagiarism, it marshals a theft narrative to sustain the
illusion that literary property is ownable in the first place.21 According to this view, there can be no such thing as artistic robbery since all texts
are essentially thievable pieces of language.
Poe was and remains at the roiling center of property disputes that have shaped literary history in dissolving the lines between literary-
and self-properties. Rumors that Poe had stolen “The Raven” (poem) were rife even during his lifetime2.2 Eliza Richards has tracked the
plagiarism allegations that beset the poem’s reception, ranging from those of well-known English writers (Samuel Taylor Coleridge, Charles
Dickens, Alfred Lord Tennyson, Elizabeth Barrett) to obscure Italian, Persian and Chinese sources that Poe putatively translated into
English. She makes the interesting observation that “The Raven” cultivates its reception as stolen or “borrowed” words2.3 The raven, after all,
mechanically repeats the word of an unknown person to profound effect.24 The charge of plagiarism was so contagious, it seems to have
infected Charles Baudelaire, who wrote in a letter to his friend Théophile Thoré that he often felt that Poe had stolen from him!

I myself am accused of imitating Edgar Poe! Do you know why I’ve translated Poe so patiently? Because he was like me. The first time I opened
one of his books, I saw, with horror and delight, not only topics I had dreamed of, but sentences I had thought of, and that he had written 20 years
before.25
Baudelaire’s plagiarism pleasantry confirms his proprietary stake in “French Poe.” As is well known, his biographical portrait of Poe and
translations of his stories and poems launched the myth that the Americans were unable to appreciate a literary genius in their midst and
required the French to recognize their patrimony. Meredith McGill argues that the traits considered defects by the American critics—Poe’s
“estrangement” from his own work, his “exaggerated awareness of cultural borrowing,” and his “inauthentic relation to literary tradition” were
precisely those that commended him to Baudelaire, Mallarmé and Valéry. Taking her cue from Jonathan Elmer, McGill maintains that the
mere prospect of reprinting his oeuvre in one large volume created an uncanny sense of disinheritance from his own literary property. Poe,
she writes, was “forced to abandon the position of author and to regard his work from a distance ‘as the composition of another.’”26 This
distance seems to account, at least in part, for the complex history of disputed attributions, print-mimetic manuscript copy, autographic
ruses, plagiarism charges, and all manner of literary fake-outs that dogged his career as both writer and editor.27
French translations of “The Raven” by Baudelaire and Stéphane Mallarmé add layers of complexity to strictly legalistic accounts of
authorial ownership, authorship, and intellectual property more generally. Mallarmé, perhaps even more than Baudelaire, stamped his
signature translation on “The Raven.” The poem’s famous final word, “Nevermore,” translated asj amais or jamais plus, was taken over as
creative property by Mallarmé when he made the typographically enlarged word “JAMAIS” the pivotal word of the title phrase of his most
famous poem “Un coup de dés jamais n’abolira le hasard” (“A Throw of the Dice Will Never Abolish Chance” [1897]): As if leapfrogging over
Baudelaire to establish his own privileged connection to Poe, Mallarmé sutured his name to Poe’s with his verbal fixation on “Never.” He
further cemented the connection with a consecrating sonnet that mentioned Poe by name in its title: “Le Tombeau d’Edgar Poe” (“The Tomb
of Edgar Poe” [1877]). Earlier in his career, Mallarmé had also encroached on Baudelaire’s exclusive “French Poe” territory by producing an
1875 luxury edition of “Le Corbeau,” illustrated by Édouard Manet. Tempting though it may be to regard these details as evidence of a
familiar pattern of poetic rivalry, I think that what Mallarmé really proved was that nobody—including, as we have seen, Poe himself—could
fully own “The Raven”; a text whose literary history over time became synonymous with the idea of a translation machine that slips out of the
grasp of each and any given writer.
“The Raven” tests the limits of textual ownership not least because as a work of genius, it was regarded as there for the taking by an
international confederacy of “stellar” poets. By Poe’s own logic, an elite class of poets was thus entitled to the property of another poetic
genius. Baudelaire’s Poe and Mallarmé’s Poe fall, according to this criterion, under the same entitlement that governed Chapman’s Homer,
Goethe’s Diderot, Stefan George’s Baudelaire, Valéry Larbaud’s Joyce, Ezra Pound’s Li Po, or Paul Auster’s Mallarmé. The compound
signature guarantees value-added literary capital, a joint-stock interest, or co-ownership of creative property.
The example of Poe-in-translation points to the fungible boundaries between translation and plagiarism. Translation, as we have already
suggested, is in many respects the legal form of plagiarism. In Michael Henry Heim’s succinct formulation: “Translation is imitation to begin
with—legal and highly necessary imitation, but imitation nonetheless.” This stigma of imitation haunts translation. Despite its legal sanction,
despite its special dispensation as a textual practice that discloses an indexical relation to the original, translation is in the end a plagiary.
And as such, as Nick Groom would have it, it arouses fears of “body-snatching” and “occult reanimation.” Classed as a soft crime,
plagiarism’s threat stems from its ostensible perversion of authenticity. Groom observes that plagiarism is often treated “like a virus that
debilitates culture, a crash than can bankrupt cultural value.”28
Critics have coined the term “translation plagiarism” in an attempt to keep translation clean of the plagiarism taint. The term is routinely
applied to examples of textual theft that can rarely be condoned. M. H. Shakir’s translation of the Qur’an, determined to have been
plagiarized from Maulana Muhammad Ali’s 1917 English version is a frequently cited example.29 Punishment for translation plagiarism
(which is often harder to detect than same-language thefts) can be severe: South African poet Melanie Grobler had her Eugène Marais
award revoked after it was revealed that her poem “Stad,” composed in Afrikaans, was a word-for-word translation of Anne Michael’s 1999
poem “There Is No City That Does Not Dream.3”0 More run-of-the mill cases involve the failure of professional translators to acknowledge the
footwork that has come before. “In what ways,” Lauren Leighton asks, “may a translator legitimately use a predecessor’s work? And when
does the use of an existing translation become plagiarism?” After all, as Terry Eagleton points out, “As the business of looking things up in
the dictionary suggests, all words are stand-ins for other words, and all our language is filched and forged, reach-me-down rather than
bespoke.”31 Where then does one draw the line? As these questions attest, the technical legal definition of translation plagiarism is a matter
of pressing concern for writers, translators and publishers operating in the “real world” of signature marketing. But the questions themselves
are problematic insofar as they foreclose a discussion of whether literary property or, for that matter, self-properties, should be treated
analogously to other forms of private property. As long as they are, we will be mired in a strange psychic economy whose cardinal principle
is: “I am therefore I am owed.”32 Put to everyday use, this principle translates as: How much is my life-story worth? Virtually everyone could
become either the subject or object of a legal shakedown under these terms. The commercialization of self literalizes the fiduciary
accounting of artistic debt, forcing writers and artists to face the prospect of ballooning remittances and unending copyright fines.33
Contesting the privatization of property in all its guises, a number of artists and writers have availed themselves of translation plagiarism,
or plagiarism tout court, in the interests of a piratical economy. In this spirit, Kathy Acker famously lifted fifteen hundred words from Harold
Robbins’s 1974 novel The Pirate for her 1975 book The Adult Life of Toulouse Lautrec by Henri Toulouse Lautrec. Yambo Ouologuem’s
1968 Le devoir de violence (Bound to Violence)—a major novel of African independence that contained French versions of verbatim
English text drawn from Emily Dickinson, Graham Greene, T. S. Eliot, and others, as well as Francophone authors—was thought to have
conscripted plagiarism in the cause of deconsecrating the Western canon with its enshrined values of authorial originality.34
Ricardo Piglia, an Argentine Maoist at the time he wrote the 1975 novella “Homenaje a Roberto Arlt,” also mobilized translation and
plagiarism to such ends. The novella’s narrator, “Ricardo Piglia,” is a sleuth on the trail of an unpublished manuscript authored by the real-life
working-class Argentine novelist Roberto Arlt, most famous for Los siete locos (Seven Madmen), a novel of 1929. After paying top dollar for
the unpublished excerpt to his friend Kostia, a disreputable fellow writer, the narrator secures his prize only to find himself in a strange double
bind—textual ownership holds him hostage to his desire to plagiarize: “I was trapped by a strange feeling of possession, as if the text were
mine and I had written it.” This onus of ownership is soon dispelled, however, when he learns that the untrustworthy Kostia has published the
Arlt fragment under his own name (or claims he has; Piglia isn’t entirely convinced that Kostia is not himself the author). Kostia insists that
since the manuscript was flawed, he was merely sparing Arlt a blot on his literary reputation. Then he drops the pretense: “You and I are
thieves.” According to Bruno Bosteels’s astute reading, this story articulates “a challenge to literary property in the name of a new ‘aesthetic’
of plagiarism or of intertextuality.” In reference to Piglia’s practice of plagiarizing Borges—Borges himself being a writer who famously built
up fictions from translation plagiarism and played up the plagiaristic fundaments of all literature to satirical effect—Bosteels discerns a
militant purpose traceable to Alain Badiou, the philosopher whose work Piglia was steeped in at the time: “By critically belaboring the idea of
plagiarism,” Bosteels maintains,
the story coins a series of concepts that, once they are extracted from the domain in which they operate in a privileged if not exclusive manner,
are also capable of reconfiguring the modes of being of politics in what we might call its Maoist, or post-Leninist, sequence.35
This new mode of politics stakes an alternate economy on expropriated intellectual property. Access to beauty must no longer be blocked by

This new mode of politics stakes an alternate economy on expropriated intellectual property. Access to beauty must no longer be blocked by
price or by authorial proprietary right; beauty must be turned over to the commons via militant acts of plagiarism. All forms of artistic theft—
translation included—are justified as weapons against the corporate policing of creative property.
Though far less radical than the early Piglia, the novelist Jonathan Lethem drafted a manifesto for the commons titled “The Ecstasy of
Influence: A Plagiarism” (2007). Lethem inveighs against Disney’s outrageous landgrab of intellectual property:
The Walt Disney Company has drawn an astonishing catalogue from the work of others: Snow White and the Seven Dwarfs, Fantasia, Pinocchio
… a legacy of cultural sampling that Shakespeare, or De La Soul, could get behind. Yet Disney’s protectorate of lobbyists has policed the
resulting cache of cultural materials as vigilantly as if it were Fort Knox—threatening legal action, for instance, against the artist Denis Oppenheim
for the use of Disney characters in a sculpture, and prohibiting the scholar Holly Crawford from using any Disney-related images—including
artwork by Lichtenstein, Warhol, Oldenburg, and others—in her monograph Attached to the Mouse: Disney and Contemporary Art.
This peculiar and specific act—the enclosure of commonwealth culture for the benefit for a sole or corporate owner is close kin to what could
be called imperial plagiarism, the free use of Third World or “primitive” artworks and styles by more privileged (and better paid) artists.
While not prepared to relinquish the principle of authorial copyright, Lethem comes down hard on the abuse of copyright, trademark and
patent law:
The case for perpetual copyright is a denial of the essential gift-aspect of the creative act. Any text is woven entirely with citations, references,
echoes, cultural languages, which cut across it through and through in a vast stereophony… The kernel, the soul—let us go further and say the
substance, the bulk, the actual and valuable material of all human utterances—is plagiarism … Don’t pirate my editions; do plunder my visions.
The name of the game is Give All.36
Lethem’s plunderer is not unlike McKenzie Wark’s hacker. In his Hacker Manifesto (2004), Wark endorsed an Internet commons, an open-
source utopia, free intellectual property, and authorial deownership. He also put his theory to work by writing a book on the Internet whose
contents evolve according to the collaboration of online readers (Gamer Theory), and by performing in a piece called This Situation.
Orchestrated by the Berlin-based artist Tino Seghal, the work’s running theme at the January 2007 session I attended at the Marian
Goodman Gallery in New York was the critique of private property. As the visitor enters the bare gallery room, she is greeted by the words
“Welcome to this situation.” The “situation” resembles a seminar in which spontaneous dialogue is generated from collective responses to
quotations of unspecified provenance. The deattributed citations thus activate an experiment in making communal intellectual property or a
site that resists becoming private property (the site comes to exist, rather, in the spirit of what Lawrence Weiner identified in a project of
1968 called Statements, as “the public freehold domain”).37 The work’s repudiation of capitalization and commodification is further bolstered
by Seghal’s ban on pictures and press kits. Though This Situation can be sold, the buyer obtains only the right to re-perform the piece
(hence the title of Anne Midgette’s New York Times review “You Can’t Hold It, But You Can Own It”)3.8 Though it remains unclear whether the
performers maintain collective copyright or whether there is a patented concept attached to the name Tino Seghal, at the very least one can
say that Seghal’s project interrogates, in a strategy that has been familiar since Duchamp signed the urinal, what kind of property an artist
can put a signature to; what is the limit case of the signable. Gordon Matta-Clark extended these questions to whether the commons could
be signed in Fake Estates. He purchased tiny parcels of deaccessioned city property—curbs, sidewalks, alley-ways—and returned them to
the public domain in the form of an art piece. More recently, Guy Ben-Ner appended his name to an act of spatial appropriationism in the
video “Stealing Beauty” (2007). Here, the artist and his family are shown occupying various showroom floors of the Swedish furniture and
household product chain IKEA. Sleeping, eating, masturbating, arguing, showering and reading in the store’s publicly displayed yet privately
owned commercial galleries, the family essentially squats in full view as customers walk by. “Stealing Beauty” challenges the codes of
decorum that reinforce the boundaries demarcating public and private space while pushing the envelope of IKEA’s legal tolerance for the
despoiling of store property. As the family engages in domestic interactions among a sea of objects with their price tags dangling down like
a kind of capitalist garniture, ordinary living becomes an expropriative gesture, a truculent performative of Proudhon’s maxim “Property is
theft” (underscored by the father’s proclamation: “Property is like a ghost. You can possess property without being possessed by it”).
If I have incrementally shifted from translation as a peculiar form of authorized plagiarism to plagiarism as one of many effective tactics
aimed at radical deownership, it is because I think this shift itself points to an increasingly fraught condition of confusion over what is “yours,”
“ours,” and “mine” in contemporary culture. As Pierre Bayard has argued:
That the parts of ourselves, gestures, words, thoughts, phantasms (not counting the body … ), are not our property, that we are never entirely
separated from others—prior, parallel or posterior—is an obvious point that psychoanalysis would not be alone in admitting. We constitute
ourselves as autonomous subjects, but we continue to carry within us fragments that do not belong to us.39
Bayard articulates the psychic ironies of possessiveness: “mine” is a state of being badly separated from others; or worse, a state of mind
that disavows the subject’s theft of others’ self-properties.
Bayard’s remarks on possession occur in the context of a short study of Romain Gary, a writer hoisted on the petard of his own
plagiarism hoax. A Lithuanian Jew brought up in France, whose career included the roles of resistance fighter, Gallimard author, and French
attaché in Los Angeles, Gary received the Goncourt Prize in 1958 and published nineteen novels by the age of seventy-three. At the moment
when his career was on the wane, Gary published four novels under the pseudonym Emile Ajar, one of which—La vie devant soi—garnered
the Goncourt Prize (which legally cannot be accorded the same author twice). Gary persuaded his nephew Paul Pavlovitch to impersonate
Ajar. Pavlovitch became so enamored of his role that he tried to blackmail his uncle into writing more Ajar books so that he could bask in the
public limelight indefinitely. This was a case of literary supercherie gone terribly wrong; Ajar had essentially usurped Gary’s copyright. The
nadir arrived when Gary, who had self-plagiarized in several Ajar novels, found himself in the ludicrous position of having to defend himself
against the charge that he was plagiarizing Ajar. What had begun as an amusing literary exercise to get past a depressive phase of
boredom with being himself, ended in the unnerving experience of self-dispossession. In 1979, shortly before committing suicide (and in the
spirit of his mother’s arrangement to have her letters posthumously dispatched to her son so as to sustain the illusion that she was still living
during his time in the Resistance), Gary wrote a literary testament that appeared after his death under the title Vie et mort d’Emile Ajar
(1981). Here, Gary exposed the Ajar imposture and spoke of a “reckoning with myself” that in essence justified his suicide as a murder of
Ajar.40 In converting the ultimate piratical act—self-theft—into a ritual of authorial self-repossession, Gary demonstrated the persistence of
the ownership model of authorial identity.
The late Chilean writer Roberto Bolaño satirized the ownership model of authorship in a number of stories built around Gary-like
characters, both victims and victors in the plagiarism game. “Alvaro Rousselot’s Journey” (published in the New Yorker in 2007) features an
Argentine writer whose unsuccessful novel Solitude (“about daily life in a remote Patagonian penitentiary”) gains attention only after it
appears in French translation as Nights on the Pampas. This gain converts to a loss, however, when the author discovers that the translation
has been plagiarized in a French film ironically titled Lost Voices. The final blow comes when Rousselot’s friends dissuade him from bringing
a lawsuit against the filmmaker on the grounds that there is something inherently pathetic about writers obsessed with the belief that their
creative property has been stolen. In Bolaño’s Nazi Literature in the Americas [La literatura nazi en América] (built around fictitious author
“bios”, Max Mirebalais is an Aryan-obsessed aspiring writer who is professionally rewarded only after becoming a plagiarist of the masters

of négritude literature from Aimé Césaire, to René Depestre, to Édouard Glissant: “With the passage of time he became expert in the art of
breaking down the work of another poet in order to make it his own.”41 The language of “breaking down” and “making it his own” underscores
the more sinister recesses of plagiarism; its affinities with enslavement, sadism, torture, and a violent appropriationism that expunges the
name of the original author from cultural memory.
With mixed degrees of humor and pathos, Gary and Bolaño deal with the theft of literary property as a matter of legal damages and
property restitution. While both satirize the rituals of redress to injured authorial egos, their texts point more symptomatically to the critical
problem of whether art, ideas, and personhood can be free if they remain tethered to a private property paradigm of individual ownership,
authorship and copyright. As long as they do, the following questions will be increasingly frontloaded as the subject matter of artistic practice,
critical theory and case law: Is free property definable only as freedom from the legalistic strictures that privatize and corporatize signature?
Is creative work thinkable outside the order of capitalization or is the aesthetic simply a disavowal of “the price of everything”?42 Do you own
yourself or your life properties (from DNA to biographical datum)? How does one deal legally with the ephemeral ownability of language and
creative property? What are the ethical and political consequences attendant on the border policing of the creative commons by copyright
and patent holders? How will knowledge production be affected by the shrinking field of free intellectual property at the very moment when
planetary access to the open source has expanded and when apparitions of anonymous, deowned, collective intellectual property (blogs,
Wikipedia, group art) are legion? Should life stories be considered plagiarizable creative property given that they are not copyrightable
forms of exact expression? To what extent is the notion of authorial property, particularly as it is defined by international copyright law in
matters pertaining to plagiarism cases, a highly culture-bound, Eurocentric concept predicated on Western notions of owership? And, finally,
can stealing from oneself or self-plagiary (defined in Stirnerian terms as a form of egoic divestiture) provide a way to undo the possessive
individualism that subtends the trademark status of signature and authorial title? If you subscribe as I do to Étienne Balibar’s view that
possessive individualism is a setback to the “collective and communitarian dimensions of life,” then opposition to ownership models based
on limiting property access or “disfruting” the other’s right to enjoyment become critical.43 As never before, the commodity nature of the
aesthetic artifact has become paramount: the work of art is (that is to say, exists or has a specific status or identity as art), insofar as it is
possessable; insofar as its signature brand can be trademarked and legally protected; insofar as shares of it belong to an author, a reader,
a publishing house, a distributor, or an Internet domain.44 As intellectual property has become increasingly the default paradigm for all art,
literature has followed the lead of film, music and visual media in allowing itself to be defined as “content” for the purposes of monitoring
copyright infringement. By extending the notion of untranslatability to include withheld, subtracted intellectual property, there is not only the
potential for activist deowning as a critical practice but also a case to be made for orienting World Literature around the problem of having or
not having properties.

rights, the author shall have the right to claim authorship of the work and to object to any distortion, mutilation or other modification of, or other derogatory action in
relation to, the said work, which would be prejudicial to his honor or reputation.”

then the literary works become legally vulnerable to “truth-in-advertising” regulations. What is starkly revealed is the extent to which signature and genre both serve to
“propertize” literature. On the problem of what trademark guarantees as intellectual property and the expanded field of its applications, see Rob Walker, “Can a Dead
Brand Live Again?,” New York Times Magazine, May 18, 2008, 50–3.

13 Jack Lynch reminds us that “it was only during the eighteenth century that ‘originality’ in the modern sense became an ideal.” An important milestone is Edward
Young’s Conjectures Concerning Original Composition, which appeared in London in 1759. There, Young celebrates novelty and attacks imitation: “Originals are, and
ought to be, great Favourites, for they are great Benefactors; they extend the Republic of Letters, and add a new province to its dominion: Imitators only give us a sort
of Duplicates of what we had, possibly much better, before.” Good authors are original, bad authors copy, and copying is no better than “sordid theft.” See Jack
Lynch’s “The Perfectly Acceptable Practice of Literary Theft: Plagiarism, Copyright, and the Eighteenth Century,” in Colonial Williamsburg: The Journal of the Colonial
Williamsburg Foundation 24: 4, Winter 2002–2003, 51–4.

as unownable.

we must address the seemingly contradictory and no less amazing tendency of his writing to usurp or incorporate without acknowledgment texts, images, props and
properties that seem to belong to other authors or sources. To Poe, authorship and originality are some form of outsourcing.” Fritz Gutbrodt, Joint Ventures:

raven is set up as a medium through which language speaks itself, mysteriously.” Gutbrodt, Joint Ventures, 251.

away from strict historical and legal referentialism as she evokes “the subjection of authors to propertyless dissemination and the invisibility of editorial ownership.”
