and the cost is a dollar apiece.” See http://www.jonathanlethem.com.
34 Joseph Slaughter writes: “Sellin was careful to avoid the charge of plagiarism, suggesting merely that Schwarz-Bart’s novel provided a ‘blueprint’ for
Ouologuem’s; however, on May 5, 1972, the Times Literary Supplement referred to Sellin’s article to corroborate its evidentiary presentation of two ‘comparable’
passages from Le devoir de violence and Graham Greene’s It’s a Battlefield (1934). The scandal gained momentum as readers from Africa, Europe, the U.S., and
Australia contributed their own discoveries in letters to the editors of newspapers and journals based in London, Paris, and Austin: Ouologuem takes a page from
Proust’s Le Temps retrouvé and a scene from Guy de Maupassant’s ‘Boule de suif’; he borrows verses from T. S. Eliot and Emily Dickinson as well as lines and
‘accents’ from Zola, Hugo, Robbe-Grillet, and Godard’s films; he echoes Camara Laye’s L’Enfant noir and Cheikh Hamidou Kane’s L’Aventure ambigue; he
incorporates passages from the Bible and the Qur’an that he mixes with cheap devices imported from popular detective fiction; he lifts histories from Jean Suret-Canale
and the Arabic chronicles of the old Sahelian empires—Tarïkh el-Fettach and Tarïkh es-Soudan; etc.” Joseph Slaughter, “Critic-Detectives Putting Yambo Ouologuem
in His Place,” unpublished manuscript.
35 Ricardo Piglia, “Homage to Roberto Arlt,” in Assumed Name, trans. Sergio Gabriel Waisman, Pittsburgh: Latin American Literary Review Press, 1995, 127, 128;
Bruno Bosteels, “In the Shadow of Mao: Ricardo Piglia’s ‘Homenaje a Roberto Arlt,’”Journal of Latin American Cultural Studies 12: 2, 2003, 231, 234.
36 Jonathan Lethem, “The Ecstasy of Influence: A Plagiarism,” Harper’s, February 2007, 65, 65.
37 This Situation explicitly recalls work of the ’60s, such as Allan Kaprow’s “happenings” or Lawrence Weiner’s 1968 Statements, in which the owner-producer of
the artwork was supplanted by the “receiver.” See Benjamin Buchloh’s discussion of Weiner’s Statements in Art Since 1900, ed. Hal Foster, Rosalind Krauss, Yves-
Alain Bois and Benjamin H. D. Buchloh, London: Thames and Hudson, 580.
38 Anne Midgette, New York Times, November 25, 2007.
39 Pierre Bayard, Il était deux fois Romain Gary, Paris: Presses Universitaires de France, 1990, 59.
40 Romain Gary, Vie et mort d’Emile Ajar, Paris: Gallimard, 1981, 22. Translations from the text are my own.
41 Roberto Bolaño, Nazi Literature in the Americas, trans. Chris Andrews, New York: New Directions, 2008, 128.
42 The Whitney Museum of American Art, The Price Of Everything: Perspectives On The Art Market, ed. Martin Braathen et al., New Haven, CT: Yale University
Press, 2008.
43 Étienne Balibar, “‘Possessive Individualism’ Reversed: From Locke to Derrida,” Constellations 9, 2002, 300. Balibar disallows a model of ownership based solely
on “disfruting” the ability of the other to enjoy property on the grounds that, in Lacanian terms, the self is barred access to the object (das Ding). When the political
institution says to man, “Thou shalt never enjoy,” it prescribes, Balibar maintains, “legitimate ‘private’ property as the renunciation of real or intimate enjoyment” (306–
7).
44 In its enhanced propertization, literature comes increasingly to resemble the modern body of bioscience. As Ed Cohen has argued: “As more and less than a
natural phenomenon, the modernized body arises as an artifact of intense human interest and investment. Informed by a confluence of finance capital, philosophical
reflection, and scientific theory, not to mention military formations, colonial relations, religious reformations, technological developments, kinship dynamics, industrial
processes, educational regimes, health care protocols, among many other factors, the modern body aspires to localize human beings within an epidermal frontier that
distinguishes the person from the world from the duration which we call a life. If we think of Mikhail Bakhtin’s ‘grotesque body’—a body radically open to the world both
temporally and spatially, simultaneously eating, shitting, fucking, dancing, laughing, groaning, giving birth, falling ill, and dying—as an icon for a nonmodern or
premodern body, then by contrast the modern body proffers a proper body, a proprietary body, a body whose well-bounded property grounds the legal and political
rights of what C. B. Macpherson famously named possessive individualism.” Ed Cohen, A Body Worth Defending: Immunity, Biopolitics, and the Apotheosis of the
Modern Body, Raleigh, NC: Duke University Press, 2009, 7.

III
Dispossessive Collectivism
Despite the tendency to think of World Literature as a politically abstracted meeting ground—the anthology or university course syllabus—
where the languages and cultures of all peoples freely mix, in my view we should do more to frame world literatures as a parlous collection of
national canons each dedicated to “possessive collectivism.” Rebecca Walkowitz used this expression to speak of the nation’s association
“with permanent, unique qualities such as a soul, spirit, and personality,” and contrasts it with Benedict Anderson’s paradigm of “imagined
communities,” which, she suggests, “associates the nation with the experience of simultaneity among people who never meet.” Possessive
collectivism points to an arena of translation theory that focuses on how communities treat language as a form of exclusive cultural property
that entitles them to impose monolingualism, or a policy of other-language abstinence, on its speakers. Relevant here are studies in art and
archaeology that focus on patrimonial claims to heritage. Explicit attention is paid to the diplomacy necessary for access to cultural property;
whether it is the complex negotiations between museums and governments over liens on displaced and disfruted art treasures, or discrepant
ethical codes of stewardship, or the politics of incursion into native art production. The ethics of access are at the heart of Michael Brown’s
book Who Owns Native Culture?, in which Brown maintains that “Hopi anxiety about use of their traditional images in the world beyond their
isolated mesas is a local expression of global concern about the disposition of cultural heritage.” He reviews the efforts of the Hopi Cultural
Preservation Office (HCPO) to contest a ruling in the 1990s that undercut “existing laws governing Hopi tribal ownership and control” (laws
that guaranteed the Hopi an “exclusive right to produce Kachinas [paintings or carvings representing Pueblo spirit-beings]”). The
determination to control the Kachina heritage extends in many cases to cultural information that anthropologists have gathered that is
deemed private:
When indigenous activists demand new controls on the kind of cultural information gathered by H. R. Voth and Frances Densmore, they often
struggle to identify an appropriate rationale. Their rhetoric moves restlessly from references to copyright and intellectual property to ideas of
blasphemy and misrepresentation. Also common in recent years are allusions to a principle known as the “right of cultural privacy.” … A right to
cultural privacy is presented as self-evident and morally unassailable, even if its scope remains unspecified.1
The right to cultural privacy—manifest in actions designed to keep outsiders at bay or assert a moral perimeter against affront—is an
important problem for the teaching of world literatures, especially in cases where the incompatible logics of copyright and blasphemy come
up against each other. How one adjudicates the decision on when a text should be off-limits for instruction (because to teach it would violate
cultural secrecy or access restrictions placed on certain kinds of cultural property) depends on how one centers, or de-centers, the place
from which one speaks and reads. In the academy, it is often difficult to develop the right GPS because of the prevalence of “hegemonic
secularism”; a secularism that sanctions the right to traduce another’s right to cultural privacy. Religious reason, in these circumstances, is
ignored or dismissed, and the politics of moral injury and blasphemy oversimplified. As Talal Asad surmises, “The charge of blasphemy is
said to be an archaic religious constraint, and free speech a principle essential to modern freedom … [but] are there any resemblances
between the idea of blasphemy and the prohibitions established by secular law?”2 The answer to this question is surely affirmative, and
leads us to perceive more clearly the extent to which so-called “archaic” religious notions of “property blasphemy” haunt secular/legal claims
to heritage and patrimony.
Property blasphemy, as a site of contestation and mistranslation, has roiled the world of the global museum, vexing its institutional
practices of acquisition, gifting and loans. The very word “acquisition” is particularly rife with controversy, suspected of being little more than
a euphemism for transgressive appropriation. Drawing on the example of the Memnon head, offered as a “gift” to the British Museum, Elliott
Colla charts the “new set of values” that emerged to legitimate the traffic in antiquities and provide a revisionist narrative for the history of
colonial spoliation and plunder:
The traffic continued apace and even increased. However the meaning of that traffic changed with the emergence of artifact discourse.
Excavation and transport now took place in the name of disinterested management and study, that is, “acquisition.” This new way of speaking
about and treating Pharaonic antiquities enabled Europeans to gain control over antiquities sites throughout the nineteenth century, and its logic
expanded British and French power and profit even as it disavowed both. Once generalized, the discourse of the artifact gave both shape and
substance to later forms of colonial discourse about managing all the resources of modern Egypt.
Acquiring artifacts from conquered territories and piling them up in national museum collections enhances the possessive collectivism of
imperially aggrandized nation-states. Similarly, archaeological sites become crucial to “owning” a national identity, especially in regions of
contested ground. As Nadia Abu El-Haj demonstrates in Facts on the Ground: Archaeological Practice and Territorial Self-Fashioning in
Israeli Society, the
significance of archaeology to the Israeli state and society and the role it played in the formation and enactment of its colonial-national-historical
imagination and in the substantiation of territorial claims … The convergence of the nation-form with historical scholarship fashioned the
perspective through which the past would be viewed.
El-Haj confirms how archaeology was used both to justify Israeli expansionism in the Middle East and to provide a territorial imaginary for the
fashioning of a modern state, in this case an “old-new Jewish national home (the altneuland)” and “a wider cartographic project that was very
much about ‘world-making.’” In “Nationalism, Politics, and the Development of Archaeology in Iran,” Kamyar Abdi traces a similar pattern in
modern Iran’s self-fashioning. Here, too, claims to the ancient past are politicized within the vocabulary of modern nation building: “The Near
East, owing to its rich archaeological and historical past and its contemporary sociocultural diversity, has been particularly interesting for
exploring connections between nationalism, archaeology, and political manipulations of archaeological record to advocate nationalist
agendas.” Historical nationalism, he argues, “is characterized by an elaborate, and sometimes crude attempt to glorify the history and culture
of ancient Iran.”3

In Who Owns Antiquity? Museums and the Battle over Our Ancient Heritage, James Cuno emphasizes the complicity of archaeologists
in these kinds of nationalist heritage politics. To maintain the privilege of working at a site, he suggests, they are willing to support
“retentionist” national policies:
The real argument over the acquisition of undocumented (unprovenanced) antiquities is not what it appears to be. It is not really between art
museums and archeologists, about the protection of the archaeological record from looting and illicit trafficking in antiquities. It is between
museums and modern nation-states and their nationalist claims on that heritage. Archaeologists are part of the argument as allies of those states
(or “source” nations; those with ancient remains within their jurisdiction). Archaeologists encourage the institution of nationalist retentionist
cultural property laws.
Cuno’s skepticism toward heritage claims informs a 2009 article in the New York Times by John Tierney chronicling the efforts of Egypt’s
Secretary General of the Supreme Council of Antiquities, Zahi Hawass, to repatriate the Rosetta Stone from the British Museum and a bust
of Nefertiti from Berlin’s Neues Museum:
Science and curators have generally supported the laws passed in recent decades giving countries ownership of ancient “cultural property”
discovered within their borders. But these laws rest on a couple of highly debatable assumptions: that artifacts should remain in whatever country
they were found, and that the best way to protect archaeological sites is to restrict the international trade in antiquities.
Tierney endorses the theory that European museums have an equal right to these treasures since the modern state of Egypt did not exist
when when the stone was discovered in 1799 by Napoleon’s soldiers (let alone when it was inscribed in 196 BC during the Hellenistic Era).
He insists that it is thanks only to Europe’s invention of modern Egyptology that the Rosetta Stone acquired its great value. Tierney is
seemingly impervious to the possibility that Egypt might have a legitimate interest in the stone regardless of whom invented Egyptology.
Thus, he reads Hawass’s threat to museums to withdraw permits to archaeological sites in Egypt if treasures were not returned simply as
evidence that “cultural-property laws have turned archaeological discoveries into political weapons.”4 While it makes sense to counter that no
country or international body arbitrates the right to cultural property with neutrality—ownership claims are always weaponizable—Cuno and
Tierney make some important points: countries are mutable entities whose property claims shift strategically, and retentionism is
increasingly a force to be reckoned with in the marketplace of world exhibitions and international research, a means of asserting national
privilege and the primacy of indigenous site-specificity.
While clashes over the repatriation of antiquities have no exact correlative in the literary field (unless we are talking about bids to return
manuscripts or tablatures valued by epigraphy to their countries of origin), there are similiar concerns. Texts celebrated for depicting
homeland, context, setting and habits of cultural belonging are especially vulnerable to charges of cultural betrayal and loss of authenticity
once they are transported into the canon of Weltliteratur. The attack on mixed-blood writers by Santee Sioux writer Elizabeth Cook-Lynn “for
losing sight of ‘tribal realism’ and national sovereignty,” or C. K. Stead’s dispute of the award of the Pegasus Prize to Keri Hulme’sT he
Bone People (on the grounds that it was written by a white person and contained inauthentic elements in its description of the Maori),
exemplify this vein of critique.5 Similarly vulnerable to these charges are the debates regarding the claims of essentialism surrounding the
reception of Chinua Achebe’s Things Fall Apart—a work, according to Nirvana Tanoukhi, whose “fusion of description and ethnography,”
and representation of Igbo oral culture and kinship traditions, induced critics to anoint Things Fall Apart the first African novel.”6
The values of “authenticity,” “tribal realism,” and nativist reception are seemingly at odds with the cosmopolitan principles of World
Literature. David Damrosch notes that one of the key ways a work of literature becomes World Literature is its circulation “beyond its
linguistic and cultural point of origin.” Circulation along with what he calls the “detached engagement” of the reader are crucial indices of this
transition in addition to the work’s “ellipitical refraction of other national literatures” and high incidence of translation. Yet it is precisely this
detachment, or disengagement, as much physical as it is affective, that ignites opposition. Vilashini Cooppan describes how “literary
globalism” has come to be associated with
a suspect ethics of (dis)engagement in which other literatures and cultures are recognized, even valorized, in order that their existence in some
temporal anteriority or spatial exteriority may rehabilitate, shore up, and, to use a popular term, globalize the privileged narratives of the west.7
Severed from place, thrown into the maw of the global culture industry or survey course, and subject to pedagogical transmission by
instructors with low levels of cultural literacy and nonexistent knowledge of a translated work’s original language, local or native literature
relinquishes its defining self-properties once it is exported and trafficked like an artifact.
World Literature, institutionally speaking, increasingly resembles the global museum in its practice of curatorial salvage. It gathers up swaths
of literary culture deemed vulnerable to extinction and performs preservational intervention. The analogy between UNESCO-decreed world
heritage sites—Pompei, the Silk Road, Angkor Wat, the Bamiyan Cave Buddhas—and classical languages and literatures is hardly a
stretch. As Mary Beard notes in the essay “Do the Classics Have a Future?” in the New York Review of Books:
In November 2011 an international petition was formally launched to ask UNESCO—in the light of the increasing marginalization of the classical
languages—to declare Latin and Greek a specially protected “intangible heritage of humanity.” I am not sure what I think about treating classical
languages as if they were an endangered species or a precious ruin.8
Beard’s fear need hardly be restricted to the classics. Modern European languages and literatures—to wit, the closing down of programs in
French, Italian and German in the United States—and countless non-European area studies fields are similarly “endangered.” The point
here, though, is not to bemoan the vulnerability of rarefied fields of the humanities but to emphasize the extent to which World Literature rests
on a conservationist attitude (Gayatri Chakravorty Spivak would call it “arrogance”) that, by default, conceives of the art work as protectable
property. In this scheme, the salvaged text is effectively signed over to the critic who is now, properly speaking, a curator, charged with
conserving it and appraising its worth.
Designating works of literature heritage-grade properties is made possible, as Pascale Casanova has suggested, by Eurocentric gold
standards of excellence and structures of legitimation endorsed by the media (publishing, criticism, prize-granting). The result is a collection
of “best-ofs” cherry-picked from national canons; crowned as “classics”; and, if we believe Peter Hitchcock, tailored to please “the drab
hierarchization of petty-bourgeois desire.”9 Ankhi Mukherjee, in her article “‘What is a Classic?’: International Literary Criticism and the
Classic Question,” draws a distinction between the “classic” and “the canon,” but when it comes to World Literature as global heritage
property, the aesthetic singularity of the classic and the perennialism of the canon would seem to have equal traction:
The classic is primarily a singular act of literature, while the canon, John Guillory states, is “an aristocracy of texts.” Canonicity implies the
formation of a corpus, the congealing of the “literary art of Memory” (Bloom), the making up of a list of books requisite for a literary education,
and the formation of an exclusive club, however painstakingly contested the rules of inclusion (and exclusion) may be. The classic, however, is
inseparable from the endless and unresolved contestations of the question What is a classic? and belongs to what Guillory calls “the conflictual
prehistory of canonformation.” If the canon implies continuity with the past or a perpetuation of tradition, the classic is all that and something else:
the survival of the classic, according to Frank Kermode, depends on its possession “of a surplus of signifier.”
The Iliad, Beowulf, Gilgamesh, The Tale of Genj,i the Mahabharata, The Divine Comedy, Don Quixote, the plays of Shakespeare and

Racine, Vanity Fair, War and Peace, Things Fall Apart, Midnight’s Children—such works are often seen not as belonging to a cultural
commons, or set of all literature, but to a class of limited-edition properties targeting elite cultural consumers. As Mukherjee queries
rhetorically:
If the classic ideal could be renovated for a postcolonial, global world, would it inevitably replicate global capital and what Ian Baucom calls the
“globalizing imaginary” by synchronizing and homogenizing historical difference or differentiation and creating a centralized (not constellated)
literary studies?10
Homogenizing difference, flattening forms, and minimizing cultural untranslatability, these are familiar critiques leveled at World Literature.
They constitute a significant aspect of what makes its “upscaling of the humanities at a global level” problematic. I have emphasized a
related but rather different issue: the extent to which World Literature, like the world-class museum or art collection, affirms a psychopolitical
structure of possessive collectivism normally associated with smaller-scaled collectivities like the nation or some other politically affirmed
form of community. Writ large, that is to say, scaled up to the proportions of World, possessive collectivism resembles “possessive
individualism,” with its self-regarding notion of personhood (of “self” as self-ownership) and happy fit with neoliberalism.
Bruce Robbins provides a way of thinking about why a critique of possessive individualism is necessary to the critique of World Literature. In
search of a cosmopolitan ethics of reading, he endorses Damrosch’s definition of a world-literary text as that which estranges a reader from
her or his own nationality (in What is World Literature?); this estrangement, he feels, encourages an ethical relation with the entire world. But
Robbins is less comfortable with Damrosch’s concession that since “we never truly cease to be ourselves as we read,” this estrangement
can only be partial. He questions whether “it may not after all be a crippling limitation to remain in some sense ‘ourselves.’” “Being-a-self-in-
the-presenttense,” he worries, can signify “the burden of a provinciality or partiality or self-interestedness from which one may need and even
want to be released.”11 Here, Robbins opens up the possibility of a dispossessive ethics of reading that challenges the presumptive self-
interest and self-having assumed to condition the reader’s relation to cultural property. This dispossessive stance casts World Literature as
an unownable estate, a literature over which no one exerts proprietary prerogative and which lends itself to a critical turn that puts the
problem of property possession front and center. In The Afterlife of Property, Jeff Nunokawa heralded this “critical property” discourse in an
analysis of Dickens and Victorian culture more generally. “However else we may have broken from the Victorian frame of mind,” he writes,
its outlines remain visible in our apprehension, traumatized or routine, that the commodity form, like a triumphal army or a thief in the night, has
entranced regions of the psyche, precincts of culture and forms of labor whose worth had been measured by their distance from market value.12
Nunokawa offers no explicit theory of dispossessiveness, but it is there by implication in his dissection of a society in the thrall of
commodities and the propertization of life, from mercenary marriage to the family melodrama structured according to the fate of entailed
estates.
The narrative curiosity Manituana (2007), a group-authored novel by an Italian collective based in Bologna that signs itself Wu Ming,
might be seen as part of a large body of world literatures built on the chassis of dispossessive collectivism. The opening epigraph from
Voltaire’s Philosophical Dictionary glorifies the agent pledged to the collective good:
A solitary man may be sober, pious, he may wear a hair shirt, and he may very well be a saint, but I will call him virtuous only when he has
performed some act of virtue from which other men will benefit. While he remains alone he will be acting neither well nor ill; for us he is nothing.
This view of the solitary as worthless without the multitude can be heard in Wu Ming’s credo: “People think each author has his or her own
voice, one voice. We think that each author has many voices.”13 The name Wu Ming is a double dispossessive: a Chinese disguise donned
by Italian authors and a word meaning “anonymous,” or “no names” in Mandarin. By calling itself Wu Ming, the group telegraphs its solidarity
with Chinese dissidents (whose posts were routinely signed “anonymous”) and its resistance to classification as Italian literature. It could well
have signed itself Anonimo (or Anonymous, in sympathy with the international hacktivist group of that name, which in 2010 successfully
defenestrated Italy’s cyber-crime unit), but that might have reduced the impact of its mystifying setup. A novel in Italian plus a Chinese
signature plus a narrative about colonial America, how could that possibly add up? All the usual cues of reception are trumped in what
amounts to a strategy designed to frustrate the reader’s hunger for writerly identity or the marketer’s ploy to trade on the typecasting of
national authors. The same shock tactic was deployed in the group’s earlier incarnation as Luther Blissett. Spurred by their research on
English sects in the Civil War, they brought out Q in 1999, “a 650-page historical spy novel that used the Reformation as a multivalent
allegory for the ups and downs of 20th-century anticapitalism.” If there is a “world” that can be identified in this model of world literature, it is
defined by a structure of address—to the Left worldwide—and by a concerted effort to map oneworldedness as a network of political
conspiracies and masterminded interests.
Like Q, Manituana uses the past to critique the historical present, in this case the post-9/11 period, in which, as it happened, there was a
spike in books on pre-Revolutionary America: “It’s as if the Americans after September 11, asked themselves some questions about their
own warmongering origins, about the source of the ‘clash of civilizations’ and the subsequent extermination of entire nations,” Wu Ming told
Marco Philopat.14 Following the attack on Afghanistan, in the months preceding the second Gulf War, Wu Ming 1 (Roberto Bui) noted in
another interview:
When there was a sharp difference of opinion about the “war on terror” between the U.S. and Europe, there was a journalistic metaphor: “The
Atlantic Ocean is widening.” We started to reflect on that, and so we went back to the beginning of the relationship, when the U.S. became the
U.S.—when it separated from Europe, in a way.15
Manituana explores the dawning of American imperialism, in the parodoxical way in which it developed out of the desire to be liberated from
British imperialism while suborning native populations. As a study in the complexity of what it means to “have world,” it is especially
revealing, for it takes as its main subject the politics of territorial claims and land expropriation in colonial America. Set in 1775, just two
years after the Boston Tea Party (where the rebel colonists had costumed themselves ironically enough as Mohawks), the novel depicts a
violent real estate tragedy. Joseph Brant, a Mohawk prince also known as Thayendanegea (based on an actual historical figure who was
received in 1776 by the King of England) is bent on redress. His dispossession notice was a “legal” testament to property theft written by the
settler Jonas Klug in the following scene:
Klug had got the right Indian drunk, the stupidest, most boastful one, Lemuel, Lemuel something, and friends of his, as stupid as he was. After
they’d got drunk, before they threw their guts up, they had signed the transfer papers. With a lovely illiterate “X,” which came to the same thing …
In the contract, Lemuel and company declared themselves legal representatives of the inhabitants of Canajoharie, owners of the land. A kind
of tribal council, an Indian thing. Thereunder, they yielded four thousand acres in return for two cases of rum.
“X,” “X” and “X,” in front of witnesses.
Beneficiary: Jonas Klug.
Shortly afterward, on a night when the moon was full, he had sent a surveyor friend, who set out to map the territory. A thousand acres more
than was stated in the contract. So he had sent everything to Albany and a year later had received the deed of ownership …

The Mohawks had taken legal action, saying that Jonas Klug had acted in bad faith, that only the sachems could sign a contract of that kind
and that the negotiation had been conducted without an official interpreter. They had moved heaven and earth, they had appealed to their
baronet, to Governor Tryon, to the British Crown. Petitions in court, and other protests, and threats to go down the path of war. As if a court could
find against an honest farmer with a horde of redskins.16
Brandt’s land will inevitably be awash in blood. He forms a small delegation representing several of the Six Iroquois Nations and takes his
grievance to the British Crown. In London, he encounters a band that calls itself the London Mohocks, stigmatized as mountebanks and
“notorious London Indians.” Hoping for acceptance as the seventh Iriquois nation, the “Emperor” of the London Mohocks invites Brandt to
“imagine the Indians of the Colonies and those of the Motherland joining forces to form a single great nation”; a revolutionary nation united to
put an end to forced relocation, property theft, deprivation, abuse, treatment as cannon fodder, and political nonrecognition. This prospect of
a transnational movement of the oppressed, though it goes nowhere, is part of a political scene of shifting alliances that arouses the anxiety
of the propertied classes:
“If we guarantee freedom to the Negroes and America to the Indians, what are we left with gentlemen?”
“Indeed. To whom will you sell your African slaves, if the Somerset ruling becomes law?”
“For my part, Mr. Pole, I could ask you where you will get the wood for your building sites in New York, if the inland forests remain in the hands
of the redskins.”
“Touché. But the root of both problems is the same; it is called America.”
“Oh no, Mr. Gilbert. America would be the solution. Myopia is the true villain: in trying to get a commercial monopoly, we will end up losing the
whole market.”
“Come on don’t be pessimistic, the spring counteroffensive is under way. His Majesty is sending troops to close the account with these rebels.”
“I have heard that. German mercenaries, it would appear. That’s what started the decline of the Roman Empire.”17
This scene portends imperial decline and fall alongside the rise of a multiracial multitude. The fantasy of a trans-Atlantic Mohawk nation
founders with Brandt’s failure to secure British support. He returns to America just in time to become a victim of George Washington’s
expedition against the hostile tribes of the Six Nations. Retreating, Brandt makes his way to Canada with a straggling band of survivors.
Manituana is the destination, blessed with a creation myth of consecration as a utopia of abundance and harmonious tribal coexistence. But
Manituana will be punished for spawning a “bastard” language. Though the new generation is happy to communicate in a common tongue, it
threatens the old tribal identifications. War breaks out, and the Master of Life punishes Manituana, throwing it into the St. Lawrence River,
where, in a variation of the Babel myth, it shatters into the Thousand Islands. In the novel, the Thousand Islands will become a new national
homeland for Brandt’s descendants. But Brandt himself returns to Iroquireland to settle scores with Jonas Klug. He kills him and stuffs his
mouth with the disputed earth: “You wanted my land, Jonas Klug. Here it is. It’s yours now.”18 This dramatic scene of force-feeding the
colonizer with the land he has stolen illuminates the violence of property-ownership and points to the folly of trying to “have” property or to
possess ground as a property of the person.
A collectively fabricated novel about pillaged native patrimony and the emergent territorial axis of the North American Empire, Manituana
affords a model of world literatures defined by dis-possessive collectivism. Wu Ming has described its own narrative production as “a world,
an example of participatory/transmedia storytelling.” As “the architrave but not the whole architecture,” Manituana invites transmedial
appropriation, and so it multiplies and morphs: into a website, a film, a soundtrack, an assemblage of side stories, a prize-winning board
game, a sample of New Italian Epic characterized by “unexpected points of view, complicated yet accessible stories, alternative realities,
and ‘unidentified narrative objects’”; and generic experiments that “combine reportage and fiction in unsettling ways.”19 A text without a
national foothold, written by a group that is committed to renouncing authorial claims, Manituana asks that we take “ourselves” as readers
out of the equation, to become instead, co-workers in acts of world-making.

1 Rebecca Walkowitz, Born Translated: The Contemporary Novel in an Age of World Literature, forthcoming, Columbia University Press; for discussions of
possessive cultural attachments to native languages, see Yasemin Yildiz, Beyond the Mother Tongue: The Postmonolingual Condition, New York: Fordham University
Press, 2012; Michael Brown, Who Owns Native Culture?, Cambridge, MA: Harvard University Press, 2003, 15; ibid., 19; ibid., 27.
2 Talal Asad, “Free Speech, Blasphemy and Secular Criticism,” in Is Critique Secular?, 27.
3 Elliott Colla, Conflicted Antiquities: Egyptology, Egyptomania, Egyptian Modernity, Raleigh, NC: Duke University Press, 2007, 28; Nadia Abu El-Haj, Facts on the
Ground: Archaeological Practice and Territorial Self-Fashioning in Israeli Society, Chicago: University of Chicago Press, 2001, 2–3, 79; Kamyar Abdi, “Nationalism,

IV
Planetary Dysphoria
I have tried to wean World Literature from its comfort zone—its ready promotion of identifying over differing and its curiously impassive
treatment of “world” and anemic planetary politics—by pressing on what a world is, philosophically, theologically and politically. In concluding,
I will conjecture that theories of cosmos/ nomos, planetarity, world depression or dysphoria, and the premonition of earthly extinction (Ray
Brassier’s “nihil unbound”) advocate a shift in the status of the word “world” in World Literature. Fredric Jameson anticipates such a shift in
his articulation of “four fundamental threats to the survival of the human race today,” specified as “ecological catastrophe, worldwide poverty
and famine, structural unemployment on a global scale, and the seemingly uncontrollable traffic in armaments of all kinds, including smart
bombs and unmanned drones.”1 Jameson’s dystopia belongs to a growing body of theory attuned to geopolitical perturbations,
ecoontological imperatives, and the operating premise of a sentient earth. From Peter Fenves’s exhumation of Kant’s “physical geography”
lectures (which followed his master’s thesis of 1755, “On Fire,” and foreshadowed his meteorological treatises in the 1790s); to Félix
Guattari’s concept of the “universe” as an autistic, subjectively destituted territoriality; to Eugene Thacker’s chthonic divinations of bio-horror;
and to Reza Negarestani’s “cthulhoid ethics,”2 in which the planet resembles an environmental death trap afflicted by radiation, pandemics,
dust, and stellar burnout.3 Peter Sloterdijk has pursued the Faustian “philosophical dimensions of globalization” to the “combat of sphere
expansion.” In the Sphären trilogy, he proceeds “psycho-cosmologically,” setting renewed value on transference (intersubjective and
terrestrial) as the psychic alloy capable of dissolving “cosmic frost,” “stellar coldness,” and the sensation of “shellessness in space,” all
effects of the loss of interiority, of the post-Enlightenment “shattering of celestial domes.” Quentin Meillassoux cedes the human cognition of
a world to an ancestral time of rocks and “arche-fossils” and a cosmic order of après-finitude. Timothy Morton speaks of the viral template of
“the RNA World.” Jane Bennett’s recourse to “a materialism in the tradition of Democritus-Epicurus-Spinoza-Diderot-Deleuze” that privileges
vitalism and “vibrant matter” supports her conviction that “the image of dead or thoroughly instrumentalized matter feeds human hubris and
our earth-destroying fantasies of conquest and consumption.” Thacker meditates on the poetics of planetary extinction and the mystical call
of ungroundedness (Ungrund) to ponder the question “Can there exist today a mysticism of the unhuman, one that has as its focus the
climatological, meteorological, and geological world-in-itself, and, moreover, one that does not resort to either religion or science?” And in
his Nihil Unbound: Enlightenment and Extinction, Ray Brassier, faulting vitalist eschatology for evading “the leveling force of extinction,”
invokes “the cosmological re-inscription of Freud’s account of the death-drive,” Nietzschean nihilism, and Lyotard’s “solar catastrophism” to
underscore extinction’s inexorable “truth.” Brassier is interested in nihilism that decouples thinking from the life of the planet, prompting
questions like: “How does thought think a world without thought? How does thought think the death of thinking?” Such queries arise from
Jean-François Lyotard’s concept of “solar catastrophe,” which occasions the destruction of thought’s “terrestrial shelter.” “With the
disappearance of earth,” Lyotard wrote, “thought will have stopped—leaving that disappearance absolutely unthought of.”4 The fixation on
extinction as thought—which is to say as the ultimate fulfillment of philosophical nihilism’s remit—sets this strain of catastrophism apart from
past traditions of Christian eschatology (end-of-the-worldism, end of days) as well as from the old Cold War fear of mutual annihilation or the
particular terrors associated with geological mass extinction events and nuclear energy accidents on a scale far surpassing Chernobyl and
Fukushima.
In Nihil Unbound, Brassier resizes Freud’s theory of the death-drive, from germ plasm, individual psyche, and civilization to the scale of a
cosmically proportioned ontic subject. Roger Caillois’s psychasthenic reworking of the repetition compulsion, where adaptive mimicry is
taken to the extreme of an “assimilation to space” (dehiscence), becomes the basis, for Brassier, of “mimicry’s non-adaptive thanatosis.”5
Brassier’s text culminates in a Gotterdämmerung moment when philosophy sloughs off meaning, sense, experience and purpose in favor of
the “intelligibility of extinction.”
Like Brassier, Thacker writes with the chiaroscuro of Goth spiritualism. In After Life, he positions “dark pantheism” against theological
pantheism (“for which an anthropomorphic God still serves as sovereign Creator and Source”), imputing to it “the challenge of thinking under
the sign of the negative” and the challenge of ontologizing “life beyond its physico-biological reduction.” Working through a political ecology
that assumes a level playing field between human and nonhuman forms of life, Thacker would seem to revel in a possible world uncognized
by humans and steeped in “planetary disasters, emerging pandemics, tectonic shifts, strange weather, oil-drenched seascapes, and the
furtive, always-looming threat of extinction.” The challenge for philosophy, he maintains, is to try to comprehend extinction, and its
unthinkability, politically.6
It has often been alleged that the speculative materialists—many claiming a pronounced allegiance to Marxism and antiglobalist activism
—pay only lip service to politics, but there is arguably a political edge to their hyperbolic evocations of a planet in the grip of revolutionary
ressentiment: sullen, wounded, and ready to retaliate against the hubris of humans who “forget” that their own psychic fates are tethered to
the earth’s distressed crust, depleted mineral veins, and liquid, molten nihilism.
Drafted from solar nihilism, from the neo-Romantic return to Schelling’s Naturphilosophie (as exemplified by Ian Hamilton Grant’s
“transcendental geology”), and from geo-psychoanalysis, this dark ecology may be further unpacked through a construct that I will call
“planetary dysphoria,” which is a variation on Melanie Klein’s “depressive position,” suffusing every aspect of economic, social and terrestrial
life. Dysphoria, from the Greek δσφορος (dysphoros), means to endure that which is difficult. It denotes an unpleasant or uncomfortable
mood: sadness, a downer moment, anxiety, restlessness, irritability, spleen, manic swings, withdrawal (from addictive cravings), and the total
evacuation of euphoria. It qualifies as an economy contoured more and more by a recessionary drive, and by subjects of melancholia. For
Freud, there was in fact a planetary dimension to melancholia: “In mourning it is the world which has become poor and empty; in melancholia
it is the ego itself.” Melancholia extends the sense of the world as “poor and empty” to the ego. In melancholia, as Freud saw it, the ego was
in the grips of
a profoundly painful dejection, cessation of interest in the outside world, loss of the capacity to love, inhibition of all activity, and a lowering of the
self-regarding feelings to a degree that finds utterance in self-reproaches and self-revilings, and culminates in a delusional expectation of

self-regarding feelings to a degree that finds utterance in self-reproaches and self-revilings, and culminates in a delusional expectation of
punishment.7
This internalization of anger against self—masochistic and suicidal at its most extreme—jives with Peter Sloterdijk’s civilizational diagnosis
of repressed thymos (literally, “spiritedness,” a term whose Platonic ascription refers to manifestations of pride, indignation, shame and
bellicosity). He charts a shift in the Greek psyche from a phase of testosterone-fueled “heroic militancy” to one extolling “civic excellence” in
which rage is downgraded as a “charismatic affect” to mere “enthusiasm.”8 In a predictably Nietzschean vein, Sloterdijk sees the modern
politics of the general will, pluralism, and liberal consensus-building, as well as therapeutic culture in general, as symptoms of thymotic
deprivation. The sole reprieve comes intermittently, either in the form of vulture capitalism (“creative aggression”) or as capitalism turned
against itself—which is to say, revolution (“the furious resentment against property”) and radical movements that provoke “huge releases of
rage, resentment, and fantasies of extermination … a chain of thymos catastrophes … the organization of a novel economy of rage.”9
Lars von Trier’s film Melancholia (2011), whose title refers to the name of a rogue planet that crashes into earth, and whose lowering
approach on the horizon tugs at the latent suicidal inclinations of people in depression, is a thymotic tragedy in precisely these terms. The
structure adheres to the exigencies of apocalyptic genre, where, as Peter Szendy reminds us, “It is necessary that the last image be the very
last image, which is to say the last of them all—of all images past, present and future.”10 Structure is key: Melancholia’s division into two
parts, titled “Justine” and “Claire,” underscores the reliance on structural divisions between the contrasting states of day and night, a blue
planet (Earth) and a red planet (Melancholia), euphoria and depression. Justine (played by Kirsten Dunst) is blond, exuberant and willful, and
Claire (played by Charlotte Gainsbourg) is dark, recessive, and controlling. But, as we approach the midpoint of Justine’s wedding
celebration, there is a decided mood swing, with Justine starting her free-fall into catalepsy and Claire opening up to the exquisite
anticipation of extinction. The thymotic tipping point comes when guests at the lavish party pass from assurances of Justine’s happiness in
statements such as “How’s your wonderful night going?” to scenes of rage, starting with Justine’s mother who remarks at the toast “I don’t
believe in marriage” and climaxing with Justine’s public denunciation of her boss in the words “I hate you … You are a power-hungry little
man Jack.” By the time we reach Part 2, all traces of Justine’s high animal spirits have vanished, and she is like the victim of a gunshot,
where the path of the bullet has entered and exited her body, pulling her psyche into the slingshot orbit of the planet Melancholia proceeding
on its course closer and closer to earth. Taken into this cosmic force field, Justine becomes a planetary avatar. In one shot, the viscous water
of the stream in which she floats reclaims her body, and she morphs into Ophelia, with a baleful stare fixated on an unseen extraterrestrial
Hamlet. In another sequence, she moves arduously across the greensward wearing a wedding dress whose skeins grow into moss-like
tendrils that act as restraints, restricting her movements and tethering her to the ground as if in chthonic servitude. Yet another scene fixates
on Justine naked on the rocks at night, her body radioactively luminescent, communing directly with her planet in a pantomime of Brünnhilde
(whose funeral pyre would end up destroying the world in Wagner’s Götterdämmerung). There are also searing moments when her hands
become conductors of lightning, and the sky, sensing her moods, rains down dead birds, freakish hail and rain, and clouds of insects.
Throughout, von Trier relies on ecological embodiments of the death-drive, conscripting the devices of pathetic fallacy with no apology. And
while the film may fall short of Brassier’s full-on solar posthumanism—there is humanist redemption in Justine’s caring preparation for death,
easing the way for her sister and nephew—it is arguably just as dysphoric in its lunar clinamen; the way the film swivels into position as an
allegory of cosmic self-destitution.
Planetary dysphoria captures the geo-psychoanalytic state of the world at its most depressed and unruhig, awaiting the triumphant
revenge of acid, oil and dust. These elements demonstrate a certain agency; they are sentient materials even if they are not fully licensed
subjectivized subjects. McKenzie Wark, with reference to Reza Negarestani’s occult treatise on oil “polytics” and Tellurian insurgencies in the
book Cyclonopedia: Complicity with Anonymous Materials, underscores the “abstract” look of the world’s “chemical signature” at the end of
human time:
Our permanent legacy will not be architectural, but chemical. After the last dam bursts, after the concrete monoliths crumble into the lone and
level sands, modernity will leave behind a chemical signature, in everything from radioactive waste to atmospheric carbon. This work will be
abstract, not figurative.
This passage resonates with Brassier’s opening quote from Nietzsche’s The Will to Power, in which the fate of the human is hitched to a
gravitational force beyond the earth, symbolized by X: “Since Copernicus, man has been rolling from the center toward X.”11 The “world” in
world literatures, I have tried to show, is on a similar flight path; increasingly identified not just with the expansion of canons or with translation
technologies that extend the reach of translatability, but with something abstract and Untranslatable; which is to say, with thanotropic
projections of how a planet dies.
